                                                       Obstructions to Erdős-Pósa Dualities for Minors∗
                                                    Christophe Paul†             Evangelos Protopapas†               Dimitrios M. Thilikos†

                                                                                    Sebastian Wiederrecht‡
arXiv:2407.09671v2 [math.CO] 16 Jul 2024




                                                                                              Abstract


                                                Let G and H be minor-closed graph classes. We say that the pair (H, G) is an Erdős-Pósa pair
                                                (EP-pair) if there exists a function f such that for every k and every graph G ∈ G, either G has k
                                                pairwise vertex-disjoint subgraphs which do not belong to H, or there exists a set S ⊆ V (G) of
                                                size at most f (k) for which G − S ∈ H. The classic result of Erdős and Pósa says that if F is the
                                                class of forests, then (F, G) is an EP-pair for all graph classes G. A minor-closed graph class G is
                                                an EP-counterexample for H if G is minimal with the property that (H, G) is not an EP-pair.
                                                    In this paper, we prove that for every minor-closed graph class H the set CH of all EP-
                                                counterexamples for H is finite. In particular, we provide a complete characterization of CH for
                                                every H and give a constructive upper bound on its size. We show that each class G in CH can be
                                                described as the set of all minors of some, suitably defined, sequence of grid-like graphs ⟨Wk ⟩k∈N .
                                                Moreover, each Wk admits a half-integral packing, i.e., k copies of some H ̸∈ H where no vertex
                                                is used more than twice. This implies a complete delineation of the half-integrality threshold of
                                                the Erdős-Pósa property for minors and as a corollary, we obtain a constructive proof of Thomas’
                                                conjecture on the half-integral Erdős-Pósa property for minors which was recently confirmed by
                                                Liu. Our results are algorithmic. Let h = h(H) denote the maximum size of an obstruction to H.
                                                For every minor-closed graph class H, we construct an algorithm that, given a graph G and an
                                                integer k, either outputs a half-integral packing of k copies of some H ̸∈ H or outputs a set of at
                                                        Oh (1)                                                         kOh (1)
                                                 most 2k       vertices whose deletion creates a graph in H in time 22       · |G|4 log |G|. Moreover,
                                                 as a consequence of our results, for every minor-closed class H, we obtain min-max-dualities, which
                                                 may be seen as analogues of the celebrated Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour, for the
                                                 recently introduced parameters H-treewidth and elimination distance to H.



                                           Keywords: Erdős-Pósa dualities; Graph parameters; Graph minors; Treewidth, Elimination distance;
                                           Universal Obstructions; Parametric graphs; Decomposition theorems


                                           Mathematics Subject Classification: 05C83; 05C85; 05C10; 05C75, 68R10

                                                    3
1     Introduction
Min-max dualities like Menger’s Theorem or the related Max-Flow/Min-Cut Theorem are among the
most powerful results in discrete mathematics, usually having strong algorithmic consequences. In
many cases, exact dualities like the ones above do not exist, however, allowing for some gap may
yield approximate results. Two emblematic theorems of this type in structural graph theory are the
Erdős-Pósa Theorem for hitting and packing cycles [19] and the Grid Theorem of Robertson and
Seymour which ties treewidth to the existence of large and highly connected planar minors [62]. In this
paper we generalize both results above, maintaining their deep connection, to arbitrary minor-closed
graph classes H. Regarding the former, we determine all minor-closed classes G where, for each of
its members, the absence of a small hitting set for the obstructions of H implies the presence of
many disjoint such obstructions. Moreover, regarding the second, we prove that any graph where the
parameter “H-treewidth” is large must contain a certificate for this in the form of a highly regular
grid-like minor containing many “highly entangled” obstructions to H.

1.1     Erdős-Pósa dualities for graph minors
We begin with a general introduction to Erdős-Pósa-type dualities together with some basic notation
for the presentation of our results.

Minors. A graph Z is a minor of a graph G if Z can be obtained from a subgraph of G by contracting
edges. This induces a partial order on the class of all graphs which we denote by ≤ . A graph class G
is minor-closed if it contains all minors of its elements and it is proper if it does not contain all graphs.
The obstruction set obs(G) of a minor-closed graph class G is the set of ≤-minimal graphs not in G.
We refer to the graphs in obs(G) as the obstructions of G. Due to the main result of the Graph Minors
series by Robertson and Seymour [66] we know that obs(G) is a finite set for all minor-closed graph
classes G.

Apices and barriers. Given a graph class H, an H-modulator of a graph G is a subset of vertices
of G whose deletion yields a graph in H. The H-apex number of G, denoted by apexH (G), is defined
as the minimum size of an H-modulator in G. When H is a minor-closed graph class, the study of this
parameter includes the proof of constructive bounds, as a function in k, for the number of obstructions
of the graph class {G | apexH (G) ≤ k} [2,21] as well as fixed-parameter algorithms for deciding whether
a graph belongs to {G | apexH (G) ≤ k}, parameterized by k [33, 36, 41, 50, 71–73]. Instances of this
problem include the classic case where H is the class of all forests [8, 15, 30, 49] as well as other classes
with “good” algorithmic properties such as planar graphs [33, 36] and graphs of bounded genus [41],
that have attracted particular interest.
    As an attempt to introduce an “obstructing measure” for a graph G to belong to some graph class
H, we consider a collection B of subgraphs of G, each “obstructing” membership to H. When H is
minor-closed, every member of B should contain a common1 element of obs(H) as a minor. If the
subgraphs in B are pairwise vertex disjoint we call B an integral H-barrier (or H-barrier for short).
If every vertex of G belongs to at most two members of B, then B is a half-integral H-barrier. We
may turn this notion into a maximization parameter barrierH (G) (resp. 1/2-barrierH (G)) by defining
it as the maximum size of an H-barrier (resp. half-integral H-barrier) of G. For example, in the case
where H is the class of forests, an integral H-barrier of G is a set of pairwise vertex-disjoint cycles in
G (notice that each cycle contains K3 ∈ obs(H) = {K3 } as a minor).
   1 One might alternatively ask for minor-containment of different elements of obs(H); however this would not define a

parameter that is functionally different, as obs(H) is finite by the Robertson-Seymour Theorem.




                                                          4
Erdős-Pósa dualities. If G contains an H-barrier B, then every H-modulator is forced to contain
at least one vertex from each member of B. Therefore, barrierH (G) ≤ apexH (G). This holds for all
possible choices of H and G. Similarly, one may observe that 1/2-barrierH (G) ≤ 12 · apexH (G). The
question of duality is whether the reverse inequalities hold approximately:

      Under which assumptions does the absence of a large (half-integral) H-barrier imply the
      existence of a small H-modulator?

    Providing a precise and complete answer to the “when” but also to the “why” of the question above
is the objective of this paper.
    In order to discuss this objective formally, let us present a unified way to express Erdős-Pósa type
questions and results. Let H and G be two minor-closed graph classes. We refer to H as the target
class and G as the environment class. The pair (H, G) is an Erdős-Pósa pair, or EP-pair for short,
if there is a function f : N → N, called the gap function, such that, for every graph G ∈ G, it holds
that apexH (G) ≤ f (barrierH (G)). The concept of EP-pair allows us to express all possible Erdős-Pósa
dualities for the minor relation and thereby formalize the “when”-part of the question above as follows

                 Which pairs (H, G) are EP-pairs and what are their gap functions?                                   (1)

Let Gall denote the class of all graphs and let F be the class of all forests. The seminal theorem of
Erdős and Pósa [19] initiated research for Erdős-Pósa dualities and motivated their name. It can be
reformulated as:

                   (F, Gall ) is an EP-pair whose gap function belongs to O(k log k).                                (2)

Robertson and Seymour, as a consequence of their celebrated Grid Theorem, proved the following: [62]

                (H, Gall ) is an EP-pair if and only if obs(H) contains a planar graph.                              (3)

The proof of (3) identifies candidates for graph families that indicate an answer to the “why”-part
of (1): they are wall-like structures representing the graphs embeddable in non-spherical surfaces
such as the projective plane or the torus (see Figure 1). Cames van Batenburg, Huynh, Joret, and
Raymond [11] proved that for every EP-pair (H, Gall ) the gap function2 is Oh (k log k) where h := h(H)
denotes the maximum size of a member of obs(H), thereby matching the bound of (2).
    Using 1/2-barrierH (G) instead of barrierH (G) in the discussion above yields the concept of an
1/2-Erdős-Pósa pair, or a 1/2-EP-pair for short. In a major breakthrough result, Liu [47] proved3 the

conjecture of Thomas (see [35]) stating the following:

                (H, Gall ) is a 1/2-EP-pair for all proper minor-closed graph classes H.                             (4)

A major drawback of Liu’s result is that it does neither provide explicit bounds on the gap function
for (4), nor does it provide a constructive way to obtain either the H-modulator, or the half-integral
H-barrier. For this reason, algorithmic applications of (4) are not immediate. Pursuing a constructive
version of Liu’s result appears to be a crucial step in the further development of the algorithmic aspects
of Graph Minors series and its applications.
    The Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour [62] implies that the target graph classes H which
    2 Given two functions χ, ψ : N → N, we write χ(n) = O (ψ(n)) to denote that there exists a computable function
                                                           x
f : N → N such that χ(n) = O(f (x) · ψ(n)). Moreover, we write χ(n) = polyx (ψ(n)) to denote that there exists a function
f : N → N such that χ(n) = f (x)nO(1) .
    3 A proof of Thomas’ Conjecture was announced by Norin in 2010 at the SIAM Conference on Discrete Mathematics

but it was never published [54]. The proof we present in this paper is independent of both Liu’s and Norin’s work.




                                                           5
together with Gall form EP-pairs are exactly those of bounded treewidth4 . This leads to the following
variant of (1):
     Given a minor-closed class H of unbounded treewidth, what is the most general environment
     class G such that (H, G) is an EP-pair?

Counterexamples to the Erdős-Pósa property. To answer the question above, we define CH as
the collection of all graph classes G for which (H, G) is not an EP-pair, while (H, G ′ ) is an EP-pair
for every minor-closed class G ′ properly contained in G. In other words, CH contains all environment
classes that are subset-minimal counterexamples for being an EP-pair with H as the target class.
Observe that, by (3), CH = ∅ if and only if H has bounded treewidth.
    We wish to stress the “second order” nature of the collection of graph classes CH .

An order-theoretic perspective. With respect to set containment, we may think of CH as an
obstruction for a minor-closed environment class G to form an EP-pair with H. Indeed, if EH is the
collection of all environment minor-closed graph classes that form EP-pairs with the target minor-closed
class H, then CH contains the ⊆-minimal minor-closed graph classes that do not belong in EH . In this
way, CH can be seen as a second-order obstruction for the collection of classes EH .
    Therefore, providing a characterization of CH is a way to describe all possible Erdős-Pósa duality
theorems for minor-closed graph classes. The fact that the collection CH exists and is countable for
every H is a consequence of the fact that (Gall , ≤) is a WQO, as proven by Robertson and Seymour [66].
Indeed, this follows by the fact that every graph class in CH is minor-closed and thereby is characterized
by a finite set of obstructions. However, since CH is a collection of graph classes, WQO for the minor
relation does not imply that CH is finite. In fact, the finiteness of CH , for all H, would follow
immediately from the stronger conjecture that (Gall , ≤) is an “ω 2 -WQO” [55]. This conjecture, as well
as the more general one that (Gall , ≤) is a Better Quasi Ordering (BQO) remain central open problem in
order theory [57]. Unfortunately, proving these conjectures seems to be a “hopelessly difficult problem”,
as mentioned by Robertson, Seymour, and Thomas in [69]. Moreover, it is unlikely that if such a proof
ever appears, it is a constructive one. Even a constructive proof that (Gall , ≤) is a WQO appears to be
out of reach [20, 24, 45].
    An alternative way to obtain explicit bounds for the gap function would be a constructive proof for
Erdős-Pósa-type dualities. This would constitute the basis of an algorithmic theory. For these reasons,
to tackle such theorems, it seems imperative to study the collections CH explicitly.

Our results. In this paper we fully characterize the collection CH for every proper minor-closed
graph class H and prove that it is finite.
    A parametric graph is a minor-monotone sequence G = ⟨Gt ⟩t∈N of graphs, i.e.for every t ∈ N,
Gt ≤ Gt+1 . As we shall see, the concept of parametric graph is central to the characterization of the
collection CH , for every proper minor-closed graph class H. Indeed, for every proper minor-closed
                                                                       1/2
graph class H, we construct a finite collection of parametric graphs FH = {FH,1 , . . . , FH,r }, r := r(H),
consisting of “wall-like” parametric graphs, hereafter called walloids, and prove that, every class in CH
                                                            1/2
is the set of all minors of some parametric graph from FH . In particular, we prove the following.
Theorem 1.1 (Main result). For every proper minor-closed graph class H and every minor-closed
                                                                                                      1/2
graph class G, (H, G) is an EP-pair if and only if the class G excludes a member of F, for every F ∈ FH .
    Notice that Theorem 1.1 indeed provides an answer to the “when” part of our leading question
(1): the absence of a large H-barrier in a graph G ∈ G implies the existence of a small H-modulator if
                                                                                              1/2
and only if G excludes a graph for each of the classes generated by the parametric graphs in FH . An
                        1/2
important feature of FH (see the definition in (9)) is the following.

we have that barrierH (Ft ) = 1.

in FH are those where it is impossible to pack two copies of some member of obs(H), while, with

counterexamples to the Erdős-Pósa property in the exact same way as the surface-based examples of
Robertson and Seymour [62].

    For each proper minor-closed class H, the set FH is constructed using the obstructions of H as a
starting point. We postpone the description of this construction to Subsection 1.5. We stress however

that for every class H of bounded treewidth, as CH = ∅, we have that FH = ∅. As a first non-trivial
example, let us consider H to be a minor-closed class such that obs(H) contains no planar graphs and

at least one single-crossing minor5 . It turns out that FH is composed of the two parametric graphs
that are depicted in Figure 16 .

    This implies that CH is composed of the class of all toroidal graphs and the class of all projective
planar graphs. This generalizes (3), by replacing the condition that the target graph class has a planar
obstruction, to the condition that the target graph class H has obstructions that are all non-planar and
that at least one of them is a single-crossing minor, obtaining that, in this case, (H, G) is an EP-pair if
and only if G excludes some toroidal and some projective planar graph.
                                                                                             1/2
    In fact, for every class H whose obstruction set does not contain a planar graph, FH contains a
parametric graph representing some orientable surface other than the sphere and a parametric graph

representing a non-orientable surface. However, in general not all members of FH correspond to

surfaces. As we shall see in Subsection 1.5, the members of FH may be arbitrarily far away from being
                                                     1/2
surface-embeddable. A wide family of H’s where FH indeed corresponds to surfaces is studied in [56].

1.2     Min-max dualities and a high-level overview of our approach
At its core, our approach is very close to the spirit of the Graph Minor series of Robertson and Seymour.
That is, we do not immediately obtain Theorem 1.1. Instead, we take a detour and first describe the
structure of graphs that “locally” exclude a large (half-integral) H-barrier.
    The Grid Theorem [62] states that there exists a function f : N → N such that for every graph G
and every integer k, if G has treewidth at least f (k) then G contains a k-wall 7 as a subgraph. For this
we need two ingredients. The first is a description of what “local” with respect to a wall means. Let k
be a positive integer, W be a k-wall in a graph G and S ⊆ V (G) be a set of strictly less than k vertices.
Then there exists at least one pair of a horizontal and a vertical path of W which avoids S. Notice that
all possible choices of such pairs belong to the same connected component of G − S. We say that this
component contains the majority of G − S 8 . The second ingredient is another collection of parametric
graphs whose explicit definition we postpone to Subsection 1.5. For every proper minor-closed graph
class H, we will describe a finite collection of parametric graphs WH = {WH,1 , . . . , WH,w }, where
w = w(H) is a constant depending only on the class H, consisting of “wall-like” parametric graphs
which have, among others, the following three important properties:

   1. if, for some W = ⟨Wt ⟩t∈N ∈ WH , there does not exist q ∈ N such that Wq contains two disjoint
                                                                1/2
      copies of some member of obs(H) as a minor, then W ∈ FH ,
                                                                                        1/2
   2. there exists fH
                    1
                      : N → N such that for every W = ⟨Wt ⟩t∈N ∈ WH \ FH and every k ∈ N, WfH
                                                                                            1 (k)

      contains an H-barrier of size k, and

   3. there exists a function fH  2
                                    : N → N such that for every i ∈ [w] and every positive integer k,
        H,i
      Wf 2 (k) contains a half-integral H-barrier of size k and an fH
                                                                    2
                                                                      (k)-wall such that the wall and the
         H
      barrier cannot be separated by fewer than k vertices.

    At the heart of our proof sits the following “local structure theorem”. We present here an equivalent
but slightly simplified version of Theorem 9.2 which avoids a lot of the heavier notations and technical
definitions.

Theorem 1.3 (Local structure theorem (simplified)). For every proper minor-closed graph class H
there exist functions f 1 , f 2 : N → N such that for every positive integer k and every graph G, if W is a
f 1 (k)-wall in G, then

    • there exists a parametric graph W = ⟨Wt ⟩t∈N ∈ WH such that G contains a subdivision W ′ of
      Wk and W ′ cannot be separated from W by fewer than k vertices, or

    • there exists a set S ⊆ V (G) of size at most f 2 (k) < f 1 (k) such that the component of G − S
      which contains the majority of the degree-3-vertices of W − S belong to H.

   With Theorem 1.3 we have a powerful tool at our disposal which allows us to describe any graph G
which excludes a member of every parametric graph in WH in terms of a tree decomposition into pieces
that are either small or belong to H. This type of behavior is very reminiscent of the Grid Theorem of
Robertson and Seymour [62], but also of their general structure theorem for H-minor-free graphs [65],
and it is similar to other, more recent results such as the one in [76]. To provide a description of this
   7 See Subsection 2.4 for the definition of walls.
   8 For the reader familiar with the theory of graph minors it is probably apparent that this notion does indeed define a

tangle. However, for our purposes it suffices to deal with tangles only in this implicit fashion.




                                                            8
intermediate result and how it leads to Theorem 1.1, we require some additional definitions. In the
following we describe, roughly, how all of our results reduce to Theorem 1.3.
    A (very) brief outline of the proof of Theorem 1.3 is given in Subsection 1.4, for a slightly more
in-depth discussion and overview of the proof, including several of the more technical definitions we
postponed so far, we refer the reader to Subsection 1.5 and Subsection 1.6.

Parametric families. Let G = ⟨Gt ⟩t∈N and G′ = ⟨G′t ⟩t∈N be two parametric graphs. We write G ≲ G′ ,
if there is some function f : N → N such that Gt ≤ G′f (t) for all t ∈ N. If G ≲ G′ and G′ ≲ G we say that
G and G′ are equivalent, denoted by G ≡ G′ .
     Let G and G′ be parametric families. We say that G ≲∗ G′ if for every G′ ∈ G′ there is some
G ∈ G such that G ≲ G′9 . Moreover, G and G′ are said to be equivalent if G ≲∗ G′ and G′ ≲∗ G,
this is denoted by G ≡∗ G′ . A parametric family G is an antichain if for every pair G, G′ ∈ G with
G= ̸ G′ it holds that G ̸≲ G′ and G′ ̸≲ G. If G is a parametric family, we say that a subset G′ of G is a
minimization of G if G′ ≡∗ G and G′ is an antichain. Notice that all minimizations of G are pairwise
equivalent. This permits us to use min(G) to denote an arbitrarily chosen minimization of G.

Graph parameters and universal obstructions. A graph parameter is a function p : Gall → N
mapping graphs to non-negative integers. Given two graph parameters p and q, we write p ⪯ q if there
is a function f : N → N such that p(G) ≤ f (q(G)) for every graph G. We refer to f as the gap function
of this relation. It is not hard to observe that ⪯ is a quasi-ordering on the set of graph parameters.
We also say that p and q are equivalent with gap function f, denoted by p ∼ q, if p ⪯ q and q ⪯ p
where f is a gap function witnessing both relations. Let P be a finite set of graph parameters. We
define max(P) as the parameter p(G) := max{q(G) | q ∈ P}.
    Given a parametric graph G = ⟨Gt ⟩t∈N , we define the graph parameter pG : Gall → N to be

                                            pG (G) := max{t | Gt ≤ G}.

Let G be a parametric family. We define pG (G) := max({pG | G ∈ G}). One may now observe that
G1 ≲∗ G2 if and only if pG1 ⪯ pG2 . We say that a parametric family G is an obstructing set for a
graph parameter p if p ∼ pG . Moreover, G is a universal obstruction for p if it is an obstructing set for
p and a ≲-antichain. Observe that G1 and G2 are both obstructing sets for the same graph parameter
if and only if G1 ≡∗ G2 .
   With these notions at hand, we are now ready to describe how our approach yields a far-reaching
generalization of the Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour. Indeed, as we will see, the parameter
pWH is equivalent to a generalization of treewidth which has received considerable attention in recent
years, specifically by the algorithmic community.

H-treewidth. Given a graph G and a set X ⊆ V (G), we define torso(G, X) as the graph obtained
from G[X] by turning, for each component K of G − X, the set NG (V (K)) (i.e., the neighbors of
the component in X) into a clique. Given a class H, the parameter H-treewidth, denoted by H-tw, is
defined as follows.

         H-tw(G) := min k ∈ N | there exists X ⊆ V (G) such that tw(torso(G, X)) ≤ k and      (6)
                       

                                         every component of G − X belongs to H

Here tw(G) denotes the treewidth of the graph G (see Section 11 for a definition).
   9 This “lifting” of the relation ≲ on parametric graphs to families of parametric graphs is called the Smyth extension

of ≲ in the theory of WQOs.




                                                           9
    The above definition was proposed by Eiben, Ganian, Hamm, and Kwon [18] as a natural extension
of treewidth that measures the tree decomposability of a graph into pieces belonging to some class H
and has interesting algorithmic applications (see [4, 5, 18, 29, 31, 32, 53]). A direct consequence of the
Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour is that H-tw ∼ tw if and only if obs(H) contains a planar
graph (see Observation 11.2).
    Our first core result is that, for every H, WH is an obstructing set for H-tw. For this, we need two
results. The first is the following.
Theorem 1.4. For every proper minor-closed graph class H, every parametric graph W = ⟨Wt ⟩t∈N in
WH , and every t ∈ N it holds that H-tw(Wt ) ∈ Ωh (t).
    The proof of Theorem 1.4 is based on an appropriate adaptation of the notion of brambles to the
concept of H-treewidth. Brambles are known to act as lower bounds to treewidth [59]. For the notion
of H-treewidth we adopt the following definition. A strict10 H-bramble in a graph G is a collection
B of connected subgraphs of G which pairwise intersect such that B ∩ H = ∅. The order of a strict
H-bramble H is the minimum size of a hitting set for all elements of B. In Figure 2 we provide a
sketch of the construction of a strict H-bramble of large order in a member of WH . The fact that
the members of WH contain large order strict H-brambles stems from their construction. We show
that for each ⟨Wt ⟩t∈N ∈ WH there exists some constant c = c(H) such that Wc contains a member
of obs(H) as a minor (see Theorem 10.1). In fact, for increasing t, Wt contains a large half-integral
H-barrier consisting of such minors and this barrier also acts as the strict H-bramble.




Figure 2: A strict bramble of subgraphs, all obstructing membership to H, in a member Wk of WH .
The blue strip at the bottom represents the large wall that constitutes the basis of Wk .
At the top of this wall, we attach gadgets with different properties. These gadgets are, from left to
right, two “handle segments” and two “crosscap segments” which model a surface. What follows are
several “flower segments”. These segments model parts of an obstruction Z ∈ obs(H) which attach
to the rest of the graph through few (that is less than three) vertices. Each “flower segment” hosts k
disjoint copies of the same part. Finally, on the far right, there are additional “flower segments”. These
flowers still model parts of Z, but these parts are now allowed to attach to the rest of the graph via
boundaries of size larger than three (although we will show that the boundaries are still of bounded
size).
The gray area serves two purposes. First, it illustrates one possible way Z might fit into Wk as a minor.
Second, it indicates how to draw several copies of Z in a way that no vertex of Wk is contained in more
than two copies and that any two copies intersect. Such a family of copies of Z, embedded as minors
into Wk forms the strict bramble. See Figure 9 and Lemma 11.7 for a more detailed description.

    The proof that strict H-brambles of large order act as lower bounds to H-treewidth follows the line
of fairly standard arguments (see Lemma 11.3). The most technical part of this paper is the upper
bound towards the parametric equivalence between H-tw and pWH . This proof spans Section 4 to
Section 10. The key step, which is the proof of Theorem 1.3, can be found in Section 9 while the most
involved and novel technical parts are confined to Section 7 and Section 8.
   10 Notice that the original notion of brambles (see for example [61]) allows for two bramble elements to be disjoint

if they are joined by an edge. While we believe that such a (more relaxed) definition of H-brambles could yield even
tighter duality results, strict H-brambles occur more naturally within our proofs.


                                                          10
   Due to the wall-like nature of the parametric graphs in WH , Theorem 1.5 and Theorem 1.4 provide
a grid theorem for H-treewidth for every proper minor-closed graph class H. The final proof of
Theorem 1.5 utilizes Theorem 1.3 together with known balanced separator arguments.
Theorem 1.5 (General grid theorem). There exists a function f1.5 : N2 → N such that for every proper
minor-closed graph class H, where h = h(H), every graph G, and every k ∈ N, if H-tw(G) ≥ f (h, k),
then there exists ⟨Wt ⟩t∈N ∈ WH such that G contains a subdivision of Wk . Moreover, f1.5 (h, k) =
  O (1)
2k h .
   As a direct corollary, we also obtain a similar statement for the set Wmin

            H such that G contains a subdivision of Wk .

From H-tw to apexH . To express the structural behavior of apexH in the form of an equivalence
                                                 1/2
between parameters we must consider the family FH and all possible H-barriers. We define (see (10) and
                                                     1/2
the surrounding definitions) FH to be the union of FH and the parametric family {WpZ | Z ∈ obs(H)}
where WpZ := ⟨t · Z⟩t∈N .
   With this, we obtain the following lower bound towards our desired parametric equivalence between
the parameters apexH and pFH .
Observation 1.7. For every proper minor-closed graph class H and every parametric graph ⟨Wt ⟩t∈N ∈
FH it holds that apexH (Wt ) = Ωh (t) where h := h(H) is the maximum size of a member of obs(H).
    The less trivial direction of the equivalence between apexH and pFH and, in particular, obtaining its
single exponential gap function are mostly a matter of carefully combining our results on H-treewidth
with established techniques from the study of the Erdős-Pósa property for minors.
Theorem 1.8. There exists a function f1.8 : N2 → N such that for every proper minor-closed graph
class H, where h = h(H), every graph G, and every k ∈ N, if apexH (G) ≥ f1.8 (h, k), then there exists
                                                                            O (1)
⟨Ft ⟩t∈N ∈ FH such that G contains Fk as a minor. Moreover, f1.8 (h, k) = 2k h .
    The proof of Theorem 1.8 makes use of the three core properties of the family WH which allow, by
Theorem 1.5 to reduce to the case of bounded H-tw. This reduction goes as follows: If we find a large
                                                    1/2
enough member of one of the parametric graphs in FH we are done. Moreover, by combining the first
and the second core property of WH we know that, whenever we find a large enough member of one
                                     1/2
of the parametric graphs in WH \ FH , we have found a member of one of the parametric graphs in
{WpZ | Z ∈ obs(H)}. Hence, whenever H-treewidth of some graph G is large enough, we immediately
obtain that pFH (G) is also large by Theorem 1.5. It follows that the only instances we have to check
are those where H-treewidth is bounded.
    From here, we are able to use standard arguments in two steps. First, we show the theorem for
every class, minor-excluding some connected graph Z (Subsection 12.1). Roughly speaking, we employ
the same recursive method as described by Diestel in his proof of (3) (see [13]). This leaves open only
cases where obs(H) contains disconnected graphs. These cases need some additional arguments using
the connected case as a basis (Subsection 12.2).
    It is worthwhile to mention that the mechanism that allows us to deduce a finite obstructing set for
apexH appears far more universal. In Section 13 we discuss this approach in a more general setting and
conjecture that our results are the basis for a whole family of similar results based on the knowledge
of obstructing sets for certain parameters. As a proof of concept, we apply this mechanism to the
parameter H-treedepth (also known as elimination distance to H) in Subsection 12.3 and obtain a finite
obstructing set for every choice of a proper minor-closed graph class H. This parameter was originally
introduced by Bulian and Dawar [9,10] and has inspired its own avenue of research [4,5,18,29,31,32,53].

1.3      Algorithmic consequences
Let us address some important algorithmic consequences of our results along with subsequent open
problems.

A constructive (approximation) FPT-algorithm for half-integral packing. Our results imply,
for every proper minor-closed graph class H, the existence of an algorithm AH that, given a graph G
and an integer k, either outputs a half-integral H-barrier of size k or an H-modulator of size at most
  O(1)           kOh (1)
2k     in time 22      · |G|4 log |G|. Observe that AH can be used as an FPT-approximation algorithm
for the following problem, defined for every graph Z. Given a graph Z, we define excl(Z) to be the
class of all graphs that exclude Z as a minor.
            Half-Integral Z-Packing
            Input: A graph G and a non-negative integer k.
            Question: Does G contain a half-integral excl(Z)-barrier of size k?
   Due to the algorithmic consequences of the Robertson and Seymour theorem, there exists an
algorithm that resolves the problem above, for every H, in time f (k) · |G|2 (see [20]), however it is
non-constructive as it is based on the knowledge of the set obs({G | 1/2-barrierH (G) ≤ k}). Due to
the lack of an explicit upper bound on the size of this set and of its members, so far no constructive
algorithm is known for any non-planar graph Z. We observe that for the class excl(Z), the algorithm
Aexcl(Z) discussed above, either decides that (G, k) is a yes-instance of Half-Integral Z-Packing or
                                                                                  O (1)
that (G, f (k)) is a no-instance of Half-Integral Z-Packing, where f (k) = 2k h . This constitutes
the first constructive (approximation) FPT-algorithm for the Half-Integral Z-Packing problem.
In Section 13 we discuss further algorithmic consequences of our results.

Deciding when an enviroment class has the Erdős-Pósa property. Given a minor antichain
R, weTdenote by excl(R) the class of graphs that excludes the graphs in R as minors, i.e., the class
Gall ∩ R∈R excl(R). Consider the following problem for any given non-empty minor antichain R.

            Checking EP-pairs for R-minor free target
            Input: An antichain Z for the minor relation.
            Output: Is excl(R), excl(Z) an EP-pair?
                                       

    The antichain R (resp. Z) provides a finite representation of a target (resp. environment) class and
the question is whether the fixed target class excl(R) makes an EP-pair with the input environment
class excl(Z).
    For every F = ⟨Ft ⟩t∈N ∈ Fexcl(R) , we define F↓:= {G | G is a minor of Ft for some t ∈ N}. Notice
that, by definition, F↓ is a minor-closed class. Let now

                                    OR := {obs(F↓) | F ∈ Fexcl(R) }.


                                                   12
Observe that our results imply that the set above is finite. Moreover, by Robertson and Seymour
theorem, it consists of finite sets of graphs. In light of the definitions above, a possible restatement of
our main result (Theorem 1.1), is the following:
      For any two antichains R and Z for the minor relation, where R =   ̸ ∅, the tuple excl(R), excl(Z)
                                                                                                         

      is an EP-pair if and only if for every O ∈ OR , there exists some graph Z ∈ Z such that no
      graph in O is a minor of Z.
     The description above is given in terms of finite obstruction sets to emphasize that the entire
problem has a finite description. The formulation above reflects that for each F ∈ Fexcl(R) , Z ∩ F↓̸= ∅,
i.e., none of the F↓ is fully contained in excl(Z).
     This impliesPthat thePChecking EP-pairs for R-minor free target problem can be solved
by performing O∈OR Z∈Z |O| calls to the almost-linear minor-checking algorithm of Korhonen,
Pilipczuk, and Stamoulis [42] (see also [37, 64] for previous algorithms, dating back to the Graph
Minor series). Therefore, for every non-empty antichain R of the minor relation, Checking EP-pairs
for R-minor free target is solvable in almost-linear time. Making this algorithm constructive,
assuming the knowledge of R, is an interesting open question.

Erdős-Pósa dualities for topological minors. A relevant question on Erdős-Pósa dualities is,
to what extent an analogous theory of half-integrality, based on obstructing sets, can be build for
other partial-orders one might define on graphs. A particularly natural cousin of the minor relation
that we may consider is the topological minor relation. Here, we already have the half-integrality
result of Liu [47]. Let H and G be graph classes that are closed under topological minors. We say that
(H, G) is an Erdős-Pósa pair for topological minors, in short is a tEP-pair, if there exists a function
f : N → N such that every graph G ∈ G, without k disjoint subdivisions of graphs that do not belong
to H, contains a set S of at most f (k) vertices such that G − S ∈ H.
    An immediate question is: When is (H, Gall ) a tEP-pair? Is there a characterization analogous to
the one of (3) for the topological minor relation? This question was already asked by Robertson and
Seymour in [62] and, as mentioned in [47], Liu, Postle, and Wollan have indeed found a complete (but
complicated) characterization. Moreover, this characterization seems to be qualitatively more complex
than the planarity criterion in (3) for the case of minors.
    Let texcl(R) be the class of graphs excluding the graph R as a topological        minor. According to
Liu [48], the problem that, given a graph R, asks whether texcl(R), Gall is a tEP-pair is NP-complete
                                                                            

even if we restrict
                  its inputs to trees. We wish to stress the contrast to the fact that, by (3), the question
“is excl(R), Gall an EP-pair?” is just planarity testing and thus, can be answered in polynomial time.
This suggests that a theory of obstructing sets such as the one for minors presented in this paper is
either impossible or, at least, extremely complex for the case of topological minors. A possible source
of this complexity gap seems to be the fact that graphs ordered by the topological minor relation
do not form a WQO. However, it is an interesting question whether our approach may help in the
direction of finding a constructive proof of Liu’s theorem with explicit bounds. In Figure 3 we illustrate
the current landscape of Erdős-Pósa dualities.

Improving the gap function. All parametric equivalences that we prove in this paper, namely
                                                                                               O (1)
pWH ∼ H-tw, pTH ∼ H-td, and pFH ∼ apexH , have exponential gap functions, i.e., f (h, k) ∈ 2k h .
A natural question is whether this gap can be improved to a polynomial one, that is, one of the form
k Oh (1) . This would also imply an improvement of the approximation gap of the FPT-approximation
algorithm for the Half-Integral Z-Packing problem (mentioned on page 12). One of the two
sources of exponential explosion can be found in Lemma 4.2 whose proof is given in [77] which, in
turn uses [39, Theorem 11.4] as a departure point where the parametric dependencies are already
exponential. This indicates that any improvement on this might require a general improvement of
the dependencies of the Graph minors Structural Theorem (GMST) in [39, 65]. The second level of


                                                     13
                                                             Erdős-Pósa

                              planar
                                                                   tw
                              obstructions     (H, Gall )




                                                                               Thomas’
                    minors                                              H-tw   conjecture

      topological                                                                            Liu’s
      minors                                                                                 theorem




                                   NP-complete instances of the question:
                                   “is (texcl(R), Gall ) a tEP-pair?”




Figure 3: The iceberg provides a visual metaphor for the landscape on Erdős-Pósa dualities for
(topological) minor-closed graph classes.


exponentiation comes from Section 6 where we need thickets with nests of exponential size around
their vortices in order to split those vortices. This is because each split consumes a part of the nest to
maintain the large radial linkage which leads back to the wall, and to extract new flowers. Interestingly
the reason for the exponentiality in the proof of [39, Theorem 11.4] is also the vortex splitting operation.
It seems reasonable to conjecture that finding a way to bound the dependencies of the GMST by
polynomials would also provide a way to avoid the first source of exponentiation. Such a result would
be a major advancement.
                                                                             O (1)
    Finally, the degree of the polynomial in k, even in the exponent of 2k h , is highly dependent on
the “meta parameter” h = h(H). In fact, this dependency is at least double exponential in h. Therefore,
another avenue of improvement could be to lower this dependency. A bound on the gap function of
the form 2polyh (k) , or, if the improvements to polynomial dependency we mention above can indeed be
achieved, even polyh (k) cannot be ruled out.

1.4    A short description of the proof of Theorem 1.3
Since this proof is by far the most technical and involved part of the entire paper, to properly explain
it requires a large number of deeply nested definitions. Several of these definitions are given in
Subsection 1.5 and the majority of the remaining definitions can be found in Section 2. Here we
provide a very brief and informal overview of the different steps and our approach. A second, more
in-depth but also more technical overview of our proof can be found in Subsection 1.6.
    To avoid dealing with graph classes H directly in our proof, we aim for the following variant of
Theorem 1.3.

Theorem 1.9. There exist functions f 1 , f 2 : N2 → N such that for all positive integers h and k
and every graph G, if W is an f 1 (h, k)-wall in G, then there exists a set S ⊆ V (G) of size at most
f 2 (h, k) < f 1 (h, k) such that for every graph Z on at most h vertices, either

   • there exists a parametric graph W = ⟨Wt ⟩t∈N ∈ Wexcl(Z) such that G contains a subdivision W ′


                                                       14
     of Wk and W ′ cannot be separated from W by fewer than k vertices, or

   • the component of G − S which contains the majority of W − S is Z-minor-free.

The Graph Minor Structure Theorem of Robertson and Seymour. To explain our approach,
it is necessary to introduce the Graph Minor Structure Theorem (GMST) of Robertson and Seymour
[39, 65]. We present here an abbreviated version of the local version of their theorem which avoids
most of the technical statements. Here local means again “with respect to a large wall”.
     Let α, b, d be non-negative integers. We say that a graph G is α-almost embeddable with breadth b
and depth d in some surface Σ if there exists a set A ⊆ V (G) of size at most α and there are graphs
G(i) such that G − A = G(0) ∪ G(1) ∪ · · · ∪ G(b) where G(0) is a graph embedded in Σ and each vortex
G(i) , i ∈ [b], is a graph of pathwidth at most d which is “attached along a path decomposition11 ” to
the vertices of some face of G(0) .
     The local version of the GMST of Robertson and Seymour now reads as follows.



      There exist functions f 1 , f 2 : N → N such that for every positive integer k and every
      graph G with an f 1 (k)-wall W, there either exists (1) a Kk -minor which cannot be
      separated from W by deleting fewer than k vertices, or (2) there exists a subgraph
                                                                                                     (7)
      G′ of G which cannot be separated from W by deleting fewer than k vertices and
      whose torso is f 2 (k)-almost embeddable with breadth k 2 and depth f 2 (k) in a surface
      where Kk does not embed.

Instead of starting at (7) we make use of a recent refinement by Thilikos and Wiederrecht [77] which
allows us to obtain one of three things. We either find: (1) a large clique minor; (2) a walloid of large
order that represents a surface Σ of large enough Euler-genus such that every graph on at most h
vertices can be drawn on Σ; or (3) a subgraph G′ with the properties from the second outcome of (7)
together with a walloid W ∗ of large order which is fully contained in the graph G(0) of the almost
embedding of the torso of G′ in this outcome.
    From here on our proof follows along three crucial steps.

Step 1: Almost embeddings of a graph Z. Let us fix G(0) to be the part of the torso of G′ as
above which is drawn in the surface. Moreover, let M be a minimal connected subgraph of G such that
M contains some graph Z on at most h vertices as a minor and M contains a vertex of the wall W.
Now some part of M belongs to G(0) , but other parts of M may belong to the vortices G(i) or to the
so-called “flaps”. Flaps are those parts of G which are separated from G′ − A by at most three vertices.
Since these separators are so small, they do not have to be caught within the vortices. Instead, they
form facial triangles in G(0) (see Proposition 2.1 and the definitions surrounding it). This induces a
specific 0-almost embedding of bounded breadth and depth for the graph M. A flower of M is any
maximal subgraph of M which is fully contained in either a vortex or a flap12 . Our proof introduces a
procedure that either finds many duplicates of every single flower of M, or finds a small hitting set for
at least one of those flowers.
    In Section 7 and Section 8 we show, among others, that it suffices to repeat this procedure for only
a bounded number of such graphs M for every graph Z on at most h vertices. Moreover, we show that
we may assume that M has only a bounded number, in |Z|, of vertices of degree different than two.
    For any such M, where each individual flower appears in abundance, we will then be able to
construct a corresponding member of Wexcl(Z) of large order. This is made precise in Lemma 9.1. A
 11 Please see Subsection 2.2 for a formal definition.
 12We refer to Subsection 1.5 for a formal definition.




                                                         15
formal definition of flowers and the way we obtain these graphs M and their 0-almost embeddings,
together with a full definition of the family Wexcl(Z) is given in Subsection 1.5.

Step 2: Refining an almost embedding. The “gathering” and “hitting” of the flowers of the
graphs M as above happens in two stages. In the first stage we gather all possible flowers which
can easily be found to be abundant. Meanwhile all flowers where this is impossible, or success is at
least “not as apparent”, are confined within a small number of disks described with reference to the
embedding of G(0) . Dealing with these disks, we call them thickets, is the second stage and the only
part where we actually find the hitting set.
    The first stage is exclusively concerned with flowers of M that are produced via the flaps of G(0) .
Recall that we have the walloid W ∗ within G(0) which fully represents our surface Σ. With the help of
W ∗ we now produce a tiling of Σ using only hexagons. We may assume that there are only a bounded
number of M ’s we have to consider and each M can only produce a bounded number of different
flowers. This allows us to classify, using the pigeonhole principle, those flowers that appear (a) in
many different and far apart hexagons of our tiling and those (b) that appear either clustered closely
together or a bounded number of times. This substep is done in Section 4 and Section 5.
    Let us mark all hexagons which contain flowers of type (b). By imitating a technique developed by
Kawarabayashi, Thomas, and Wollan [39] in their new proof of (7) we now gather all marked hexagons
in a small number of disks within the drawing of G(0) and then further refine our distinction between
abundant and scarce flowers until each of the disks that still contains marked hexagons can be seen as
a vortex of bounded depth itself. This step is done in Section 6.

Step 3: Harvesting the flowers of a “thicket”. The vortices created in the previous steps are all
equipped with additional infrastructure. This allows us to route anything “of value” we might find
inside, back to W ∗ . We call these objects thickets. The second purpose of Section 7 and Section 8 is
to apply a vast generalization of the “vortex killing” argument of Thilikos and Wiederrecht [76]. The
outcome of this argument is, for any possible flower F of any M we have to consider, to either find
many disjoint copies of F pasted along a single face of G(0) and highly connected to W ∗ , or find a
small hitting set for all occurrences of F within this thicket. Let S be the union of all of these hitting
sets for all thickets. Finally, we prove that whenever some M avoids the set S, then this must mean
that there is an equivalent M ′ whose flowers have been found in abundance in Step 2 and this one,
and thus we are able to produce the corresponding member of Wexcl(Z) (see Lemma 9.1).
    The proof of Theorem 1.9, or its more technical incarnation Theorem 9.2, is now only a matter of
carefully combining the previous steps.

                                             1/2
1.5     Defining the families FH and WH : A constructive description of CH
In order to state Theorem 1.1 in full formality, we first need to make our definitions of the graphs M
                                                                                                     1/2
that appear in Step 1 of Subsection 1.4 and then describe how the families WH and ultimately FH
are defined. Once this is done, the collection CH of counterexamples is fully described, in accordance
with Theorem 1.1, as the collection of every graph class generated through the minor closure of a single
              1/2
member of FH . Some definitions presented in this section still hide further technicalities which are
expanded upon in Section 2.
    As a first step, we make the 0-almost embeddings of our “minor models” 13 explicit.

Hypergraphs from graphS partitions. A subgraph partition of a graph Z is a collection P of
subgraphs of Z such that14 P = Z and {E(P ) | P ∈ P} is a partition of E(Z). Given a subgraph
  13 Later we will call these graphs the extensions of Z.
  14 Given a set S of objects where ∪ has been defined, we use

                                                                   S as a shorthand for        S∈S S. Here the union of two
graphs G1 = (V1 , E1 ) and G2 = (V2 , E2 ) is defined as G1 ∪ G2 = (V1 ∪ V2 , E1 ∪ E2 ).


                                                            16
P ∈ P, we define the boundary of P in Z to be the set ∂Z (P ) containing every vertex of V (P ) that is
also a vertex of some other member of P \ {P }. We define P(Z) to be the set containing all subgraph
partitions P of Z such that for every P ∈ P, ∂Z (P ) ̸= ∅.
    Given a subgraph partition
                             S P ∈ P(Z), we define the hypergraph KP = (VP , EP ) where EP =
{∂Z (P ) | P ∈ P} and VP = EP . It follows that each hyperedge e of KP is the boundary of some
member, say Ge , of the graph partition (notice that EP may be a multi-set, as in the case of the
example of Figure 5).
   In Subsection 1.4 we spoke a lot about “pasting graphs along the vertices of a face” of “confining”
parts of a graph within a disk. The following provides a formal framework to deal with notions like
those in a way that is, at first, independent of topology.

Linear societies and their decompositions. A linear society is a pair ⟨H, Λ⟩ where H is a
non-empty graph and Λ is a linear ordering of some non-empty subset of vertices of H. We use V (Λ)
to denote the set of vertices of Λ and we refer to V (Λ) as the boundary of the linear society ⟨H, Λ⟩.
Moreover, we always demand that every connected component of H contains some vertex of V (Λ).
   Let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be a linear society where Λ = ⟨y1 , . . . , yr ⟩. Consider a drawing, possibly with crossings,
of H in a closed disk ∆ such that the vertices drawn on the boundary, denoted by bd(∆), are exactly
the vertices of Λ drawn with respect to the order Λ. We also demand that all edges of H are drawn in
the interior of ∆. For simplicity, we do not distinguish between the vertices of V (Λ) and the points of
bd(∆) where these vertices are drawn.
    Whenever different components of H are drawn within ∆ in the way above such that there exists a
curve γ in ∆ with exactly its endpoints in bd(∆) where γ does not intersect the drawing of H in any
way, we are able to “split” ⟨H, Λ⟩ into smaller linear societies using γ while also respecting the original
linear ordering Λ up to shifts. We formalize this intuition with the definition below to obtain a notion
of “components” of a linear society.
    We see the set bd(∆) \ V (Λ) as a set of open lines between consecutive vertices in Λ. For each
i ∈ [r], let pi be a point of the line with endpoints yi−1 and yi (where y0 = yr ). A closed subdisk
∆′ ⊆ ∆ is splitting for ∆ if

   • H can be drawn (possibly with crossings) inside ∆ \ ∆′ ,

   • {p1 } ⊆ bd(∆) ∩ bd(∆′ ) ⊆ {p1 , . . . , pr }, and

   • the cardinality of |bd(∆) ∩ bd(∆′ )| is maximized.

Let bd(∆) ∩ bd(∆′ ) = {pi | i ∈ I} for some [1] ⊆ I ⊆ [r] where I = {i1 = 1, i2 , . . . , iq } and
observe that I is the same for every choice of a splitting subdisk ∆′ for ∆. For each j ∈ [q], let
Λj := ⟨yij , . . . , yij+1 −1 ⟩ and let Hj be the union of all connected components of H that contain
vertices of Λj . We denote the decomposition of ⟨H, Λ⟩ into the linear societies ⟨Hi , Λi ⟩ as above by
dec(H, Λ) := ⟨⟨H1 , Λ1 ⟩, . . . , ⟨Hq , Λq ⟩⟩.
   Next, we reintroduce the topology to fully understand how linear societies may be used to encode
the “almost” part of our embeddings.

Surfaces and linear societies. By surface we mean a two-dimensional manifold (possibly with
boundary). We say that a surface Σ is contained in some surface Σ′ if Σ′ can be obtained from Σ by
adding handles and crosscaps. Given a pair (h, c) ∈ N × [0, 2] we define Σ(h,c) to be the surface without
boundary created from the sphere by adding h handles and c crosscaps. If c = 0, the surface Σ(h,c) is
orientable, otherwise it is non-orientable. By Dyck’s theorem [17, 23], two crosscaps are equivalent (via
homeomorphisms) to a handle in the presence of a (third) crosscap. This implies that the notation
Σ(h,c) is sufficient to capture all surfaces without boundary.


                                                         17
    Given a graph Z we denote by SZ the set containing all surfaces without boundary where Z cannot
be embedded. We define sobs(SZ ) to be the set of all containment-wise minimal surfaces not in SZ . It
follows that sobs(SZ ) contains one or two surfaces, see [77]. We define S(Z) as the set containing every
surface that is contained in some surface of sobs(SZ ).
    Let now Σ be some surface and let M be a graph, let P ∈ P(M ) be a subgraph partition of M, and
assume that Γ is a Σ-embedding. In such an embedding all vertices of a hyperedge e ∈ EP are drawn
on the boundary of some closed disk ∆e of Σ and two disks ∆e and ∆e′ intersect only on the common
vertices of e and e′ . Given a choice of starting vertex y ∈ e and some rotation of the boundary of ∆e ,
we obtain a linear ordering Λe,y of the vertices of e starting from y. The rotation of each hyperedge is
chosen to be counterclockwise (that is, it has ∆e on the left) in case Σ orientable, and it is chosen to
be an arbitrary rotation if Σ is non-orientable. Each ⟨Ge , Λe,y ⟩, for y ∈ e, can now be seen as a linear
society. We say that the linear society ⟨Ge , Λe,y ⟩ is disk embeddable if the graph Ge can be embedded
in a closed disk ∆ such that exactly the vertices of Λe,y are drawn on its boundary.
    We are finally ready to give a definition of those 0-almost embeddings of graphs that sit at the core
of our description of CH .

Embedding pairs. Let Z be a graph. An embedding pair of Z is a pair (Σ, B), where Σ ∈ S(Z) is
a surface, and B is the set of all linear societies which are generated, as described below, by a fixed
triple of the form (M, P, Γ) where
   1. M is a connected graph containing Z as a minor such that |M | ≤ ext(|Z|),
   2. P ∈ P(M ), and
   3. Γ is a Σ-embedding of KP ,
                                                      2
where ext : N → N is a function of order 2O(ℓ(|Z| )) that will be determined in the proof of Lemma 9.1
in Section 9 and ℓ(·) is the unique linkage function (see [3, 26, 40, 51, 67]).
    Given a triple as above, we further consider some function η : EP → V (M ) that assigns a starting
vertex η(e) ∈ e to every e ∈ EP . Thereby, η generates the following set of linear societies

                   {⟨Ge , Λe,η(e) ⟩ | e ∈ EP and ⟨Ge , Λe,η(e) ⟩ is not disk-embeddable}.

    Notice that B may be empty. We denote by Pexcl(Z) the set containing all embedding pairs of

there exist at most 2|Z|2 + 1 many different surfaces in S(Z) and for each choice of P ∈ P(Z) we have
|P| ≤ ext(|Z|)2 and |P | ≤ ext(|Z|) for every P ∈ P which implies that there are at most ext(|Z|)3

    The walloids that make up our families FH and WH are constructed from several smaller pieces
that can be circularly arranged. Such a modular definition allows for a nicer presentation, but it also
facilitates our proofs as it allows us in many places to reduce to the analysis of single pieces of the
walloid rather than its entire global structure.
                                      1/2
t-segments. Each member W of FH is a wall-like parametric graph to which, from now on, we refer
as an elementary guest walloid. These parametric graphs W are built using the embedding pairs from
PH . The main building components of elementary guest walloids are called elementary t-segments.
While the formal definitions are explained in detail in Subsection 2.4. Here, we provide an intuitive
                                                               1/2
description which will be sufficient to understand the family FH and the more detailed summary of
the proof of Theorem 1.3.


                                                          18
    The three basic components are the elementary (t′ , t)-wall segment, the elementary t-handle segment,
and the elementary t-crosscap segment. The elementary (t′ , t)-wall segment is an elementary (t′ , t)-wall
and acts as the starting point of our construction of all other elementary t-segments. The leftmost
drawing of Figure 4 depicts an elementary (12, 3)-wall segment. We depict the t “leftmost” vertices of
an elementary (t′ , t)-wall segment in red and its t “rightmost” vertices in blue. Later, we will use these
vertices in order to glue together distinct t-segments, using new edges depicted in orange. The t′ “top”
vertices of the elementary (t′ , t)-wall segment are drawn with white interiors.
    The elementary t-handle segment is an elementary (4t, t)-wall segment where we added two
collections of t edges, each between the top vertices of the (4t, t)-wall, as indicated in the second
drawing of Figure 4. The elementary t-crosscap segment is an elementary (4t, t)-wall segment obtained
by adding a collection of 2t edges between the top vertices of the elementary (4t, t)-wall in a pairwise
crossing manner, as indicated in the third drawing of Figure 4.

    As asserted by our main result, Theorem 1.1, FH provides a precise and finite delineation of the
half-integrality for H. In the next subsection, we give some further intuition why this is the case.

1.6     A (more) detailed outline of the proof of Theorem 1.3.
The proof of Theorem 1.1 we presented at the end of Subsection 1.2 utilized as a core tool Theorem 1.8
which, in turn, can be obtained from Theorem 1.5. Let us begin by giving a short description of how
we obtain Theorem 1.5 from the local structure theorem Theorem 1.3.

Balanced H-separators and wall(oid)s. A core concept from the (algorithmic) theory of tree
decompositions is the notion of “well-linked” sets and balanced separators [60]. We define analogues of
these concepts with respect to a graph class H. A similar concept has been used by Jansen, de Kroon,
and Włodarczyk [32] in their approximation algorithm for H-treewidth.
    A set X ⊆ V (G) is k-H-linked if for every set S ⊆ V (G) of size at most k − 1 there exists a
component K of G − S such that |V (K) ∩ X| ≥ 23 |X|, and K ∈       / H. This means that whenever we
delete few vertices, most of X still belongs to some subgraph of G which contains an obstruction of
H. It is straightforward to verify that any graph with a k-H-linked set must have H-treewidth at
least k. Moreover, under the assumption that there are no k-H-linked sets in G one can construct a
tree-decomposition that witnesses small H-treewidth using standard arguments.
    Since the existence of a highly H-linked set X implies large H-treewidth, it, in particular, implies
large treewidth. By using the Grid Theorem [12, 62] together with some arguments from [39] we are
able to find a large wall which is “associated” 15 with our highly H-linked set. This wall may now be
used as the input for an application of Theorem 1.3. Finally, since the wall and X are “associated”, it
is impossible that the second outcome of Theorem 1.3 holds. Therefore, Theorem 1.5 follows from
the parametric equivalence between the largest number k for which a graph has a k-H-linked set and
H-treewidth together with Theorem 1.3.
    In the remainder of this section, we give a more detailed overview of our proof for Theorem 1.3 and
the techniques involved. As mentioned earlier, we prove a more general theorem, that is Theorem 1.9,
which yields the outcome of Theorem 1.3 for all proper minor-closed graph classes H with h(H) ≤ h
simultaneously. In most of the discussion that follows, we focus on a single graph Z on at most h
vertices for ease of presentation.
                                                                                   (h,c)            (h,c)
    Let Σ = Σ(h,c) be some surface. We define the parametric graph W(h,c) = ⟨Wt ⟩t∈N where Wt
is obtained from the cylindrical concatenation of a single t-wall segment, h t-handle segments, and
  15 Formally speaking and to help a reader familiar with the terminology of graph minors, the tangle of the wall agrees

with the tangle of the highly linked set.




                                                          21
c t-crosscap segments. A key tool we borrow from [77] is an algorithm that takes as an input some
graph G and a large wall W together with integers t, k, and g. This algorithm then returns one of
three things.

   1. a Kk -minor,
                          (h,c)
   2. a subdivision of Wt         where g = 2h + c, or

   3. a small “apex” set A ⊆ V (G) together with a so-called Σ-decomposition δ of G − A which
      “respects” the wall W, where the Euler-genus of the surface Σ is 2h′ + c′ < g for appropriate
                                                      (h′ ,c′ )
      choices of h′ and c′ , and a subdivision W1 of Wt         which is also “respected” by δ. Moreover,
      the number and depth of the vortices of δ is bounded in some function in t and k.

For most intents and purposes, one may think of a Σ-decomposition as an almost-embedding where
the depth of the vortices is not bounded. The way δ “respects” W and W1 may be understood as: they
appear as subdivisions in G(0) when δ is understood as a 0-almost embedding. See Subsection 2.2 for
a full definition of Σ-decompositions.
    An alternative way to understand a Σ-decomposition δ is through subgraph partitions and their
hypergraphs as follows. On an intuitive level, one may see δ as an embedding of the hypergraph
                       ′ ′
KP = (VP , EP ) in Σ(h ,c ) where P ∈ P(G) is a subgraph partition of G where only a bounded number
of hyperedges have size ≥ 4. Moreover, each hyperedge e ∈ EP is seen as a disk c, called cell of δ where
the graph Gc := Ge is drawn (possibly with crossings) inside c and the vertices of e are drawn on the
boundary c in some liner ordering Λc . We call a cell with at least 4 vertices drawn on their boundary a
vortex while we call the others flaps. This defines a linear society (Gc , Λc ) that has bounded depth in
the sense that there is no big flow in Gc from some set Q ⊆ e of vertices appearing consecutively on
bd(∆) to e \ Q (we call such a flow a transaction of the linear society (Gc , Λc )). Part of the outcome
of the algorithm from [77] (originally proven in [39]) ensures also that all vortices are situated “inside”
the biggest cycle of W1 . We refer to this cycle as the exceptional cycle of W1 (see Figure 6 for a
visualization). We refer to the set Vδ := V (VP ) as the set of ground vertices of δ.
    By editing the Σ-decomposition δ and collecting everything that is drawn in the unique disk defined
by the exceptional cycle of W1 which avoids all of the other cycles of W1 into a single cell, we obtain
a new Σ-decomposition δ1 of G1 := G − A. This produces a triple (G1 , δ1 , W1 ) which represents the
arable land mapped out by W1 . At this stage, we call the triple (G1 , δ1 , W1 ) a (t, Σ)-meadow.
    From here on, we begin a sequence of cultivation steps that turn the wildly grown meadow into a
neatly organized orchard whose “flowers” are ready for the harvest. Below we provide a description of
the individual steps and provide a sketch of how these ultimately lead to Theorem 1.3.
    For the purpose of our proofs, it is helpful to consider a specific type of witness for the existence
of a graph Z as a minor. We call these objects extensions of Z and they are defined in Section 7.5.
For the purpose of this introduction, it suffices to think of an extension as a subgraph M of G which
is minimal with respect to the following properties: 1) M contains Z as a minor, 2) M is connected,
3) M contains a vertex of W1 , and 4) the total number of vertices of M whose degree is different
from two is bounded by some function in |Z| which will be determined later. In our proofs we do not
actually require property 3), but our arguments are tailored to specifically deal with extensions of this
type. Moreover, 4) is not part of the actual definition of an extension of Z, but an integral part of the
content of Section 7 and Section 8 is to prove that it suffices to only consider extensions of this type.

Plowing the meadow. Notice that all but one cell of δ1 are flaps. For each flap we define a set
folio(Gc , ∆c ) that encodes all possible ways an extension of Z may “invade” the linear society (Gc , Λc ).
We also define the set folio(δ1 ) as the union of all possible folios of the flaps of δ1 . We will show that
                                                                                                         ′ ′
the size of this set is bounded by a function in |Z|. We now divide the non-vortex part of Σ = Σ(h ,c)
into 2h′ + c′ + 1 enclosures, which are areas that may be treated separately throughout our cultivation


                                                     22
process. In Figure 6 we provide an example of such a division. Each face F, with respect to δ1 , of
an enclosure is called a brick and we define folio(F ) to be the union of all folios of the flaps that are
either fully inside or on its boundary.
    Our first step is to plow the meadow and thereby prepare it for further cultivation. That is we
homogenize it by updating the triple (G1 , δ1 , W1 ) to a new triple (G1 , δ2 , W2 ) such that for every
enclosure C of the new walloid W2 , all bricks of C have the same folio. This is done by both “merging”
bricks of (G1 , δ1 , W1 ) and by “pushing” some of the flaps of δ1 into the exceptional face of W2 . Notice
that such a process comes at a great price in terms of the order of the walloid W1 and thus W1 must
be chosen to be enormous from the beginning. This completes the plowing procedure.


        exceptional face
t          wall segment     handle segment         handle segment         crosscap segment    crosscap segment
                                             simple face       bricks

Figure 6: A (t, Σ(2,2) )-meadow with two handle segments and two crosscap segments. The black line is
the boundary of the exceptional face and the red line is the boundary of the simple face. The colored
territories are the enclosures of W. The vortices are represented by the gray polygons and are all inside
the exceptional face.



Sowing a garden. So far we have prepared our meadow and turned it into a field ready to receive
the seeds that will eventually grow into the flowers and trees of our orchard. Towards this goal, the
next step on our agenda is to plant the first flowers. This means that we are going to represent every
linear society (H, Λ) in folio(δ2 ) by “planting” a sequence of h := |Z| many consecutive (t, b, H, Λ)-flower
segments in our meadow. We refer to the newly created “super segment” consisting of all these flower
segments as a (t, b, h, H, Λ)-parterre segment where b is a function in k. The key feature of our plowed
meadow (G1 , δ2 , W2 ) that allows for this procedure is the homogeneity of its enclosures which implies
that every society in folio(δ2 ) appears in abundance. This allows us to rearrange W2 in a way that
such parterre appears on the boundary of the exceptional face. Once this process is complete, we have
obtained a new triple (G1 , δ2 , W3 ) called a (blooming) (t, b, h, Σ)-garden. This garden now contains, for
each member F of folio(δ2 ) a parterre segment whose flowers represent this particular F. Notice that
δ2 still has only one vortex of (possibly) unbounded depth which sits in the exceptional face of W3 .

                                                                   -parterre segment          -parterre segment
         exceptional face                                  -flower segments            -flower segments



    t



Figure 7: A (t, b, h, Σ(2,2) )-garden with two handle segments, two crosscap segments, two parterre
segments each containing three flower segments of two distinct linear societies (depicted in red and
green). Each society appears 4 times inside each flower.


Growing an orchard. With (G1 , δ2 , W3 ) we have reached a first milestone in the cultivation of
our farmland. What is left is the wilderness in the form of the huge vortex that has been created in
the exceptional face of W3 during the previous steps. The time has come to conquer this jungle and


                                                      23
expand the arable land until the remaining wilderness is confined to a small number of rich biotopes
that allow for other means of exploration.
     To put our plan for the next step into more technical terms: In the previous steps, we have given
up on our vortices of bounded depth in order to achieve a form of homogenization with respect to the
folio of the enclosures of W3 . We now want to expand the homogeneous area and recreate (a possibly
larger number of) the vortices of bounded depth. This might create a new type of vortex: Before a
vortex was a cell that could not be embedded in a disk in a way that agrees with our Σ-decomposition
δ2 . Now, some vortices might actually have such a “flat rendition”, but for every such rendition we
choose, the folio of one of its flaps will contain a linear society ⟨H, Λ⟩ that is not abundant. This
means that, apriori, we do not have enough copies of ⟨H, Λ⟩ available to create a large enough parterre
segment for this kind of flower.
     To fully formalize the new target structure, we need to introduce a new type of t-segment: a
(t, s)-thicket segment. While before we always attached flowers or edges in a very structured manner
to the top vertices of a t-wall segment, now we have to be a bit more lax. We select a collection of
s concentric cycles C = {C1 , . . . , Cs }, called the nest, all of which are drawn in δ2 , and extend the
vertical paths of our t-wall segment beyond the top vertices, letting the resulting paths traverse the
cycles of C and reaching the “inner” area, that is the disk ∆ of Σ which is enclosed by the closed curve
that represents C1 in δ2 . These paths are called the rails of the thicket. We then require that within
this disk ∆ there exists a single vortex of δ2 . A triple (G′ , δ ′ , W ′ ) is now called a (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard
if it can be seen as a (t, b, h, Σ)-garden when pushing all of its thicket segments back into a single
vortex, and there exists a (t, s)-thicket segment for every vortex of δ ′ . See Figure 8 for an illustration.
Notice that each thicket segment induces its own linear society based on the outer-most cycle Cs of its
nest. This allows us to define the depth of a thicket in the same way we defined the depth of a vortex
above. Our goal now is to create more and more thicket segments, until all thickets are of bounded
depth or we have created a parterre segment for each possible flower (recall that there are only finitely
many possible flowers because we are only interested in flowers of bounded “detail”).
     Since δ2 has a single vortex, we may immediately interpret (G1 , δ2 , W3 ) as an (t, s)-orchard with a
single thicket16 . Before we describe the process of “splitting” a single thicket segment let us quickly
discuss why there exists an upper bound on the total number of thicket we have to consider at any
point in time. For this, observe first that the original Σ-decomposition δ1 had a bounded number, say
x, of vortices that contained an actual obstruction to disk-embeddability. Let us call these vortices
the real vortices for now. These vortices were already of bounded depth and we may use this fact
to show that there can never be more than x real vortices at any point in time. A parterre segment
requires us to have h · b many isomorphic flowers. Moreover, since the “detail” of the flowers we are
                                                                                      O(d2 )
interested in is bounded by some number, say d, there are at most d = 22                     many possible folios
(see Observation 2.2). This means, if we ever have more than h · b · d2 thicket segments whose vortices
are not real, then there must be h · b · d many, all with the same folio. Let F be this folio. This gives
us the right, using the infrastructure provided by the rails and the nests of these thickets, to extract a
large enough parterre segment for each member of F and afterward, the thicket segments with folio F
can be discarded. This means that we have an upper bound on the total number of thickets present at
any given point in time of our procedure.
     We are now ready to describe the operation of “splitting” a thicket. For this, we consider a large
transaction on the linear society defined by the outer-most cycle of the nest. We may assume that
such a transaction exists since otherwise the thicket is of bounded depth and there is nothing to be
done. Moreover, since there are at most x real vortices and all of them are of bounded depth, which
is already certified by δ1 , we may assume that the “area” defined by the transaction can be used to
extend δ2 by reintegrating a part of δ1 that was forgotten earlier. Such a transaction is depicted in
  16 This transformation actually requires to sacrifice a bit of the infrastructure of W as observed in Observation 6.1 but
                                                                                        3
for ease of presentation we skip this very minor transformation step.

                                                                    new -parterre segment(s)
                   vortex                              vortex                                      vortex




                      t                                     t                                         t




Figure 8: Left: a (t, b, h, s, Σ(2,2) )-orchard with one (s, t)-thicket and a big transaction traversing it (in
black). The black vertices mark the boundary of the thicket society. Right: the result of splitting a
thicket along this transaction and creating a new parterre. The magenta vertices form the base of the
