the left part of Figure 8. When we have found this new “flat” area, we may use the same arguments
as those used for the plowing step above to further refine the transaction into a homogenized area
with respect to the folios of its cells. In case we find a linear society in these folios that has not been
represented in a parterre segment of W3 , we may now use the infrastructure provided by the rails
and the nest of the thicket, together with the paths of the transaction, in order to extract additional
parterre segments. An illustration of this can be found in the right part of Figure 8. Finally, the paths
of the transaction, together with the cycles of the nest C now define up to two new disks in the area
that was a single vortex before. By making use of the paths in the transaction, the nest, and the rails,
this allows to “split’ the thicket into up to two new thicket segments as depicted in Figure 8. Please
notice that all of the operations described here require the sacrifice of some part of our infrastructure.
However, as discussed above, the total number of thickets and the total number of folios, and therefore
parterre segments, is bounded. This means that any of these steps can only occur a bounded number
of times and thus, by choosing the infrastructure provided by (G1 , δ2 , W3 ) to be large enough in the
beginning, we make sure to always have enough “space” available.
    Eventually, the extraction operations above will not be available anymore. We might still find more
large transactions on the societies of our thickets, but these transactions can now only create new nests
that are increasingly “tight” around the vortices. Finally, the entire process must come to a halt and
when this happens, all of our thickets are of bounded depth. We call the resulting triple (G1 , δ3 , W4 ) a
ripe (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard. See Figure 9 for an illustration.

Harvesting. We next focus our attention on how the ripe (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard (G1 , δ3 , W4 ) can be
traversed by an extension M of some graph Z where |Z| ≤ h. As we already mentioned, M has a
bounded (by some quadratic function in |Z|) number of vertices of degree different from two. We call
these vertices terminals of M. As we argued before, the ways M may visit the flaps of δ are already
represented by the flowers of the parterres. So we should fix our attention on how M may invade the
non-flat cells of δ, i.e., its vortices. recall that each vortex sits deep inside the thickets and consider ∆
to be a disk around each vortex c defined as the are enclosed by the closed curve that is the outermost
cycle of a given thicket segment of (G1 , δ3 , W4 ). Since (G1 , δ3 , W4 ) is ripe, there are only few vortices
and therefore few disks as above, and the depth of both the given vortex as well as the society defined
by ∆ is bounded by some function in h and k.
    Next, we fix our attention to the part of M which is drawn inside the disk ∆. Let us denote this
part of M by M∆ . Also, we use T∆ for the terminals of M that are drawn inside ∆. This defines the
notion of a fiber µ = (M∆ , T∆ , Λ)   b where Λ  b is a cyclic ordering of the vertices of M that are drawn

on the boundary of ∆ in the order they appear in Λ.    b While the number of terminals of any fiber is
bounded, we cannot say the same for its boundary vertices, i.e., for the vertices in Λ.      b For this, we
consider fibers whose boundary has size at most b, let us call them b-fibers, and we prove a local
Erdős-Pósa duality on how they may be covered or packed inside ∆ with respect to the boundary
of ∆. This is done by the so-called harvesting procedure which either finds a set that intersects any
b-fiber or finds a sequence of a big enough number of copies of such b-fibers which are well arranged on
the boundary of ∆ in the form of a “primitive flower” (Section 7).
    We see a b-fiber as being drawn inside the union of some collection of sub-disks of ∆, called
pseudo-disk. Here, the number of vertices drawn in the boundary of this pseudo-disk is bounded
by some function in the depth of the vortex c and the depth of the disk ∆ (see Subsection 7.2).
While the depth of both is bounded in a function in h and k, we are able to show that, for a single
b-fiber, the “depth” of its drawing now only depends on h. For the harvesting procedure, we define
the notion of a critical pseudo-disk that “critically” contains a b-fiber µ in the sense that µ is not
contained in any of its proper pseudo-disks (Subsection 7.3). Then we pick a b-fiber µ and we detect a
packing of critical pseudo-disks containing µ (Subsection 7.4). If “enough”, where the value for enough
depends on a specific function in k and h, of them are found we can organize them into packages each
containing many, say k, copies of each of the sub-fibers corresponding to the elements of dec(M∆ , Λ).   b
This collection of packages will be the primitive form of a flower, parts of which will constitute some
of the flower segments of our parametric graphs. If only few critical pseudo-disks are found, we may
consider the vertices of the boundaries of their disks in order to find a covering of all b-fibers of ∆ that
are isomorphic to µ. The size of this set is bounded by a function in k and h. The union of all these
covering sets for all possible fibers of boundary at most d is the (d, k)-cover of the disk ∆. Recall that
fibers were defined as ways that an extension M enters the disk ∆ and thus, this (d, k)-cover should
now constitute a hitting set for all such fibers which are not abundant on the boundary of ∆. We next
prove that we may choose d so that if S is a (d, k)-cover of the disk ∆, then every restriction M∆ of
an extension M in ∆ that defines a fiber with more than d boundary vertices and which is not covered
by S can be rearranged to one that defines a d-fiber and still is not intersected by S (Subsection 7.5).
The proof of this applies a global rerouting of M, based on the unique linkage theorem and some
suitable adaptation of it to our needs (Subsection 3.2). The proof also uses the fact that S resides on
the union of disk boundaries and strongly uses the topological properties of these disks as well as the
cycle collection C insulating the vortex c from ∆. This means that whenever S is not a covering of
our model M some part of this model, in the form of a collection of packages of k fibers can be found
inside ∆ as desired. Moreover, the above choice of d is bounded by some function in h. This implies
that it is enough to “pack” or “cover” b-fibers, for his particular value of b, instead of fibers in general.
Therefore, the (d, k)-cover of the disk ∆ is bounded by some function in k and h. We now define S∆


                                                     26
as the union of all the (d, k)covers of all the disks ∆, as above, corresponding to the thickets of the
(t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard (G1 , δ3 , W4 ). We denote this collection of disks by ∆Sand define Sall to be the
union of the (already bounded) apex set A from the very beginning and ∆∈∆ S∆ . Clearly, |Sall | is
bounded by a function in h and k and by the number of thickets of (G1 , δ3 , W4 ) which is also bounded
by some function in k and h.

Plowing flowers from thickets. Our next step is to analyze how the Erdős-Pósa duality for objects
“anchored” on the society of a vortex from the previous step interacts with the overall way an extension
M of Z may be situated within our (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard (G1 , δ3 , W4 ) and its thickets. It follows that if
Sall intersects every extension of Z that is “bound” to δ (i.e., it contains a vertex of W4 ; for the formal
definition, see Section 2.2), then the component of G − Sall which contains the majority of W4 − Sall is
Z-minor-free and this is the second outcome of the (simplified) local structure theorem (Theorem 1.3).

    Assume now that there is an extension M of Z which is not intersected by Sall . Then, due to
the Erdős-Pósa property above, the parts of M corresponding to the disks ∆ ∈ ∆′ are organized as

observe that among these sub-fibers, only those that invade the corresponding vortices are of interest
as all others, being drawn in the flat parts of δ, are already represented in the parterre of (G1 , δ3 , W4 ).
Also, it follows from the results in Subsection 3.2 than the number of disks intersected by M, we
denoteSthe set of these disks by ∆′ ⊆ ∆, is also bounded by some function in h. Let Σc be the closure

is drawn in Σ in a “flat way” as all invaded vortices are inside the disks. This drawing is encoded as a

of a flower based on M∆ . However, these fibers are rooted on the boundary of ∆ and we need their
boundaries to be in the base of the thicket corresponding to ∆ in order to see them as a flower. For this,
we prove a special variant of the combing lemma from [26] in Subsection 8.3. This lemma permits a
rerouting that, after sacrificing a considerable amount of the fibers, revises M∆         1 , . . . , Mp∆ so that it is

point a sequence of packages M∆      1 , . . . , Mp∆ , for each ∆ ∈ ∆ , corresponding to a collection of fibers

that admit some Σ-flat Z-certifying complement. In our proofs, this flat complement certification
property stays invariant for all revised packages. We now arrived in a situation where all parts of M
that are embedded outside of the vortices of (G1 , δ3 , W4 ) are represented by the flowers of its parterres
while the parts of M that are invading the vortices, i.e., the vortices of the disks in ∆′ , are fibers
corresponding to sequences of packages that permit us see the thickets as flowers of these fibers. Let
M ′ be the graph obtained by considering all boundary vertices of the fiber µ∆ generated by M as


                                                          27
terminal vertices. This defines a subdivision of a graph whose size is bounded by some function in h
that contains Z as a minor. As we prove in Lemma 9.1, this function is the function ext used in the
definition of embedding pairs. Next, using the graph M ′ , we interpret the subgraphs of M ′ drawn in
the cells of the flat complement δ c and the graphs drawn in the disks of ∆′ as a subgraph partition P
of M ′ and this, in turn, defines an embedding pair p = (Σ, B) where B contains societies that are
either the parts of M ′ drawn in the flaps of δ c (which are also flaps of δ) or the parts of M ′ that are
the fibers in {µ∆ | ∆ ∈ ∆′ }. As we already noticed, the first category of societies is already present
in the parterres of (G1 , δ3 , W4 ) and the second category of societies is present as flowers inside the
thickets. As a consequence of this, a subdivision of some big enough walloid in Wp can be found as a
subgraph of G (see Figure 10). This gives the first outcome of the (simplified) local structure theorem
(Theorem 1.3).


2     Preliminaries
In this section, we present the bulk of definitions that will be necessary for the development of the
proofs in the following sections.

2.1    Basic concepts
In this first subsection, we present the basic concepts we require about sets, integers, and graphs.

Sets and integers. We denote by N the set of non-negative integers, by N≥n , n > 1, to be the set
N \ {m ∈ N | m < n}, and by Neven the set of even numbers in N. Given two integers p, q, where p ≤ q,
we denote by [p, q] the set {p, . . . , q}. For an integer p ≥ 1, we set[p] = [1, p] and N≥p = N \ [0, p − 1].
For a set S, we denote by 2S the set of all subsets of S and by S2 the set of all subsets     Sof S of size 2.
If S is a collection of objects where the operation ∪ is defined, then we denote S = X∈S X. Also,
                                                                                         S
given a function f : A → B we always consider its extension f : 2A → B such that for every X ⊆ A,
f (X) = {f (a) | a ∈ X}.

Basic concepts on graphs. A graph G is a pair (V, E) where V is a finite set and E ⊆ V2 , i.e.,
                                                                                                   

all graphs in this paper are undirected, finite, and without loops or multiple edges. When we denote
an edge {x, y}, we use instead the simpler notation xy (or yx). We write Gall for the set of all graphs.
We also define V (G) = V and E(G) = E.
    We say that a pair (A, B) ∈ 2V (G) × 2V (G) is a separation of G if A ∪ B = V (G) and there is no
edge in G between A \ B and B \ A. We call |A ∩ B| the order of (A, B).
    If H is a subgraph of G, that is V (H) ⊆ V (G) and E(H) ⊆ E(G), we denote this by H ⊆ G. Given
two graphs G1 and G2 , we denote G1 ∪ G2 = (V (G1 ) ∪ V (G2 ), E(G1 ) ∪ E(G2 )). We also use G1 + G2
to denote the disjoint union of G1 and G2 . Also, given a t ∈ N, we denote by t · G the disjoint union of
t copies of G.
    Given a vertex v ∈ V (G), we denote by NG  S(v) the set of vertices of G that are adjacent to v in G.
Also, given a set S ⊆ V (G), we set NG (S) = v∈S NG (v) \ S.
    For S ⊆ V (G), we set G[S] = (S, E ∩ S2 ) and use G − S to denote G[V (G) \ S]. We say that G[S]
                                             

is an induced (by S) subgraph of G.
    Given an edge e = uv ∈ E(G), we define the subdivision of e to be the operation of deleting e,
adding a new vertex w, and making it adjacent to u and v. Given two graphs H and G, we say that H
is a subdivision of G if H can be obtained from G by subdividing edges.
    The contraction of an edge e = uv ∈ E(G) results in a graph G′ obtained from G \ {u, v} by adding
a new vertex w adjacent to all vertices in the set (NG (u) ∪ NG (v)) \ {u, v}. A graph H is a minor of a
graph G if H can be obtained from a subgraph of G after a series of edge contractions. We denote


                                                      28
this relation by ≤ . Given a set H of graphs, we use H ≤ G in order to denote that at least one of the
graphs in H is a minor of G. We refer the reader to [13] for any undefined terminology on graphs.

2.2     Surfaces, decompositions, and renditions
In this subsection, we present all definitions we require that are relevant to drawing and embedding
graphs in surfaces as well as more involved definitions such as surface decompositions, societies, and
more.

Surfaces. Given a pair (h, c) ∈ N × [0, 2] we define Σ(h,c) to be the two-dimensional surface without
boundary created from the sphere by adding h handles and c crosscaps (for a more detailed definition
see [52]). If c = 0, the surface Σ(h,c) is an orientable surface, otherwise it is a non-orientable one.
By Dyck’s theorem [17, 23], two crosscaps are equivalent to a handle in the presence of a (third)
crosscap. This implies that the notation Σ(h,c) is sufficient to denote all two-dimensional surfaces
without boundary.

Societies.    Given a graph G and a set X = {x1 , . . . , xn } ⊆ V (G), we consider two types of orderings
of X.
    • a rotation ordering Ω = (x1 , . . . , xn ) where we assume that (x1 , . . . , xn ) = (x1+i , . . . , xn+i ), for
      every i ∈ [0, n − 1]. Note that n + i is interpreted as (n − 1 mod n) + i + 1.
    • a linear ordering Λ = ⟨x1 , . . . , xn ⟩. Given a linear ordering Λ = ⟨x1 , . . . , xn ⟩ we define its i-
      position shift as the linear ordering ⟨x1+i , . . . , xn+i ⟩. Note that, as before, n + i is interpreted as
      (n − 1 mod n) + i + 1.
    Given a rotation (resp. linear) ordering Ω = (x1 , . . . , xn ) (resp. Λ = ⟨x1 , . . . , xn ⟩) we define
its reverse as rev(Ω) := (xn , . . . , x1 ) (resp. Λ := ⟨xn , . . . , x1 ⟩). Given a linear ordering Λ, we
define nextΛ (xi ) = xi+1 , for every i ∈ [n − 1] and nextΛ (xn ) := x1 . We also define previousΛ (xi ) :=
nextrev(Λ) (xi ). When Λ is clear from the context we simply write next(xi ) and previous(xi ). Moreover,
given two distinct linear orderings Λ = ⟨x1 , . . . , xn ⟩ and Λ′ = ⟨y1 , . . . , ym ⟩, we define their concatenation
as the linear ordering Λ ⊕ Λ′ = ⟨x1 , . . . , xn , y1 , . . . , ym ⟩.
    We define the shifts of a linear ordering Λ as the set containing every i-position shift of Λ, for
i ∈ [n]. Observe that, given a rotation ordering Ω = (x1 , . . . , xn ) we can extract n different linear
orderings depending on the choice of the first vertex. Let Λ be any of these linear orderings. Then all
these linear orderings, which are the shifts of Λ, correspond to a single rotation ordering that we call
the rotation ordering of Λ.
    A linear (resp. rotation) society is a pair ⟨G, Λ⟩ (resp. (G, Ω)) where G is a graph and Λ (resp.
Ω) is a linear (resp. rotation) ordering of a subset of the vertices of G. The rotation society of a
linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ is the rotation society (G, Ω), where Ω is the rotation ordering of Λ. We denote
by V (Λ) (resp. V (Ω)) the set of vertices on that correspond to elements of Λ (resp. Ω). We also
use |Λ| := |V (Λ)| (resp. |Ω| := |V (Ω)|). From this point forward we always use the notation ⟨G, Λ⟩
(resp. (G, Ω)) to denote linear (resp. rotation) societies. Whenever we use the term society we will be
referring to a linear society. On the other hand, we will explicitly mention when we are dealing with a
rotation society.
    A subdivision of a linear (resp. rotation) society ⟨G, Λ⟩ (resp. (G, Ω)) is every linear (resp. rotation)
society ⟨M, Λ⟩ (resp. (M, Ω)) where M is a subdivision of G.
    Two societies ⟨G, Λ⟩ and ⟨G′ , Λ′ ⟩ are isomorphic if there is an isomorphism ψ := V (G) → V (G′ )
from G to G′ such that ψ(Λ) = Λ′ , i.e., if Λ = ⟨x1 , . . . , xr ⟩, it holds that Λ′ = ⟨ψ(x1 ), . . . , ψ(xr )⟩.
    Given a linear ordering Λ and a set S ⊆ V (Λ), we define Λ ∩ S (resp. Λ \ S) as the linear ordering
Λ′ obtained from Λ if we remove all elements that do not (resp. do) belong in S. Given a linear society
⟨G, Λ⟩ and a set S ⊆ V (G), we define ⟨G, Λ⟩ − S as the linear ordering ⟨G − S, Λ \ S⟩.


                                                         29
      A cross in a society ⟨G, Λ⟩ is a pair (P1 , P2 ) of disjoint paths17 in G such that Pi has endpoints
si , ti ∈ V (Λ) and is otherwise disjoint from V (Λ), and the vertices s1 , s2 , t1 , t2 occur in Λ in the order
listed.

Drawing a graph in a surface. Let Σ be a surface. If Σ has a boundary we denote it by bd(Σ)
and we refer to Σ \ bd(Σ) as the interior of Σ. A Σ-drawing (with crossings) is a triple Γ = (U, V, E)
such that

   • V and E are finite,
   • V ⊆ U ⊆ Σ and bd(Σ) ∩ Γ ⊆ V,
   • V ∪ e∈E e = U and V ∩ ( e∈E e) = ∅,
        S                   S

   • for every e ∈ E, either e = h((0, 1)), where h : [0, 1]R → U is a homeomorphism onto its image
     with h(0), h(1) ∈ V, or e = h(S2 − (1, 0)), where h : S2 → U is a homeomorphism onto its image
     with h(0, 1) ∈ V (where S2 = {(x, y) ∈ R2 | x2 + y 2 = 1}), and
   • if e, e′ ∈ E are distinct, then |e ∩ e′ | is finite.

We call the set V the nodes and the set E the arcs of Γ. If G is a graph and Γ = (U, V, E) is a
Σ-drawing with crossings such that the nodes V and the arcs E correspond to the vertices V (G)
and the edges E(G) respectively, we say that Γ is a drawing of G in Σ (possibly with crossings) or
alternatively a Σ-drawing of G. Two distinct arcs in E cross if they have a common point. If no two
arcs in E cross, we say that Γ is a Σ-drawing of G without crossings or, alternatively, a Σ-embedding
of G. In this last case, the connected components of Σ \ U, are the faces of Γ.

Surface decompositions. Let Σ be a surface, possibly with boundary. A closed (resp. open) disk
of Σ is any closed (resp. open) disk ∆ that is a subset of Σ. For brevity, whenever we use the term
disk we mean a closed disk. A Σ-decomposition of a graph G is a pair δ = (Γ, D), where Γ = (U, V, E)
is a Σ-drawing of G and D is a collection of disks of Σ such that

   1. the disks in D have pairwise disjoint interiors,

   2. for every disk ∆ ∈ D, ∆ ∩ U ⊆ V, i.e., the boundary of Σ and of each disk in D intersects U only
      in vertices,

   3. if ∆1 , ∆2 ∈ D are distinct, then ∆1 ∩ ∆2 ⊆ V (Γ), and

   4. every edge Γ is a subset of the interior of one of the disks in D.

Let N be the set of all nodes of Γ that do not belong to the interior of any disk in D. We refer to the
elements of N as the nodes of δ. If ∆ ∈ D, then we refer to the set ∆ − N as a cell of δ. We denote the
set of nodes of δ by N (δ) and the set of cells of δ by C(δ).
    Given a cell c ∈ C(δ), we define the disk of c as the disk ∆c := bd(c) ∪ c. For a cell c ∈ C(δ), the
set of nodes of δ that belong to ∆c is denoted by e   c. Thus the cells c of δ such that e  c ̸= ∅, form the
hyperedges of a hypergraph with vertex set N (δ), where e    c is the set of vertices incident with c. For a
cell c ∈ C(δ), we define the graph σδ (c), or σ(c) if δ is clear from the context, to be the subgraph of
G consisting of all vertices and edges that are drawn in ∆c . We define πδ : N (δ) → V (G) to be the
mapping that assigns to every node in N (δ) the corresponding vertex of G. We also define the set
ground(δ) := πδ (N (δ)) of ground vertices in δ. If πρ (p) = x then we say that p is the point of x in δ.
Similarly, if X is a set of ground vertices then we refer to the points of the vertices of X as the points
of X in δ.
 17 Two paths are disjoint when their vertex sets are disjoint.




                                                          30
    A cell c ∈ C(δ) is called a vortex if |e
                                           c| ≥ 4 and a flap otherwise. We call a cell c trivial if σδ (c)
is the graph on two vertices and one edge. We also partition C(δ) into the sets Cf (δ) and Cv (δ),
containing the flaps and the vortices of δ respectively. We say that δ is vortex-free if Cv (δ) = ∅, i.e., if
no cell in C(δ) is a vortex.

Surface decompositions of subgraphs. Let Σ be a surface and δ = (Γ, D) be a Σ-decomposition
of a graph G, where Γ = (U, V, E). Also let H be a subgraph of G. We define δ ∩ H to be the following
Σ-decomposition (Γ′ , D′ ) of H.

   • We obtain Γ′ = (U ′ , V ′ , E ′ ) from Γ = (U, V, E) by defining V ′ (resp. E ′ ) from V (resp. from E)
     by removing every nodeS (resp. arc) that does not correspond to a vertex (resp. edge) in H. Also
     we define U ′ :=  ′
                      V ∪ e∈E ′ e.

   • Let N − be the set of all nodes of δ that have been removed. For every x ∈ N − let Dx be an
     open disk containing x that S
                                 does not intersect U ′ . We define D′ as follows. From every D ∈ D,
     define D ∈ D as D
             ′    ′     ′ :=
                             D \ x∈N − Dx .

    Notice that the above update to the disks of δ is necessary in order to obtain the third property of
the definition of a Σ-decomposition. In this way, we certify the existence of an injection between the
disks of δ and those of δ ∩ H, which only become smaller.

δ-aligned disks. Let δ = (Γ, D), Γ = (U, V, E), be a Σ-decomposition of a graph G. We call a disk
∆ of Σ δ-aligned if U ∩ bd(∆) ⊆ N (δ). Clearly, for each c ∈ C(δ), ∆c is a δ-aligned disk. Given a
δ-aligned disk ∆, we define B∆  δ
                                  = πδ (bd(∆) ∩ N (δ)). When δ is clear from the context, we may write
B∆ instead of B∆ .δ

    Given an arc-wise connected set ∆ ⊆ Σ such that U ∩ ∆ ⊆ N, we use G ∩ ∆ to denote the subgraph
of G consisting of the vertices and edges of G that are drawn in ∆. Note that, in particular, the above
definition applies in the case that ∆ is a δ-aligned disk of Σ. We also define Γ ∩ ∆ := (U ∩ ∆, V ∩ ∆, {e ∈
E | e ⊆ ∆}) and observe that Γ ∩ ∆ is a drawing of G ∩ ∆ in ∆.
    Given that ∆ is a δ-aligned disk we define the rotation ordering Ω∆ of B∆ as follows. If Σ is an
orientable surface, we define Ω∆ as the rotation ordering of the vertices of B∆ , when traversing along
bd(∆) in the counter-clockwise direction. In case Σ is a non-orientable surface, there is no distinction
between the counter-clockwise or clockwise ordering of the disks of Σ. Therefore, we always assume
that δ is accompanied with a rotation tie-breaker that picks one of the two possible rotations of ∆.
Then the rotation ordering Ω∆ is defined by following the rotation direction indicated by the rotation
tie-breaker. Given some y ∈ B∆ , we define Λ∆,y as the linear ordering obtained by Ω∆ by fixing y as
the starting vertex. Given a δ-aligned disk ∆, a ∆-society of δ is a society ⟨G ∩ ∆, Λ∆,y ⟩, for some
y ∈ B∆ . For every c ∈ C(δ), we denote Gc := G ∩ ∆c and Ωc = Ω∆c . A c-society in δ is any ∆c -society,
where c ∈ C(δ). Moreover, in the case that c is a vortex cell, we call any c-society of δ a vortex society
of c in δ.

Renditions. A rendition of a society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆ is a ∆-decomposition ρ of G such that
⟨G, Λ⟩ is a ∆-society of ρ. A cylindrical rendition (Γ, D, c0 ) of a society ⟨G, Λ⟩ around a cell c0 is a
rendition ρ = (Γ, D) of G such that all cells of ρ that are different than c0 are flap cells.
   Given a Σ-decomposition δ of a graph G and a δ-aligned disk ∆ of Σ, we denote by δ ∩ ∆ the
rendition (Γ ∩ ∆, {∆c ∈ D | c ⊆ ∆}) of the society ⟨G ∩ ∆, Λ⟩ in ∆. Moreover, we assume that the
rotation tie-breaker of δ ∩ ∆ is chosen so that the rotation of all disk in ∆ agree with the orientation
of ∆.




                                                     31
Nests. Let ρ = (Γ, D) be a rendition of a society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆ and let ∆∗ ⊆ ∆ be an
arc-wise connected set. An s-nest in ρ around ∆∗ is a set C = {C1 , . . . , Cs } of pairwise disjoint
ρ-grounded cycles where, for i ∈ [s], the trace of Ci bounds a closed disk ∆i in such a way that
∆∗ ⊆ ∆1 ⊊ ∆2 ⊊ · · · ⊊ ∆s ⊆ ∆, bd(∆1 ) ∩ ∆∗ = ∅, and bd(∆s ) ∩ ∆ = ∅. We call ⟨∆1 , . . . , ∆s ⟩ the disk
sequence of the s-nest C and we say that, for i ∈ [s] ∆i corresponds to the cycle Ci .

Rural societies. We say that a society ⟨G, Λ⟩ is a vortex society if |Λ| ≥ 4 and a simple society
otherwise. The next proposition is a restatement of the celebrated “two paths theorem” that gives
an alternative characterization of cross-free societies. Several proofs of this result have appeared,
see [34, 63, 75, 79]. For a recent proof see [38, Theorem 1.3].

Proposition 2.1. A linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ has no cross if and only if it has a vortex-free rendition in a
disk.

   A linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ that has no cross is called rural. Observe that simple societies are trivially
rural. Given a Σ-decomposition δ of a graph G, we say that a cell c of δ (resp. disk ∆ of Σ) is flat if
some –actually any– c-society (resp. ∆-society) of δ is rural.

Grounded and bound graphs. Let           S δ be a Σ-decomposition of a graph G. Let Q ⊆ G be either a
cycle or a path that uses no edge of {σ(c) | c ∈ Cv (δ)}. We say that Q is δ-grounded if either Q is a
non-trivial path with both endpoints in πδ (N (δ)) or Q is a cycle that contains edges of σ(c1 ) and σ(c2 )
for at least two distinct cells c1 , c2 ∈ C(δ). A 2-connected subgraph H of G is said to be δ-grounded if
every cycle in H is grounded in δ. We also say that a subgraph G′ of G is δ-bound if there is a path in
G between a vertex of G′ and a ground vertex of δ.

Disk-embeddable societies. A linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ is disk-embeddable if it has a rendition in some
disk ∆ where all cells are trivial.

Traces. Let δ be a Σ-decomposition of a graph G in a surface Σ. For every cell c ∈ C(δ) with |e           c| = 2
we select one of the components of bd(c) − e       c. This selection is called a trace tie-breaker in δ, and we
assume every Σ-decomposition to come equipped with a tie-breaker. If Q is δ-grounded, we define the
trace of Q as follows. Let P1 , . . . , Pt be distinct maximal subpaths of Q such that Pi is a subgraph of
σ(c) for some cell c. Fix an index i. The maximality of Pi implies that its endpoints are πδ (n1 ) and
πδ (n2 ) for distinct nodes n1 , n2 ∈ N (δ). If |ec| = 2, define Li to be the component of bd(c) − {n1 , n2 }
selected by the tie-breaker, and if |e    c| = 3, define Li to be the component of bd(c) − {n1 , n2 } that is
disjoint from e c. Finally, we define L′i by slightly pushing Li to make it disjoint from all cells in C(δ).
We define such a curve L′i for all i while ensuring that the curves intersect only at a common endpoint.
The trace
       S of Q, denoted by traceδ (Q) or simply trace(Q) when δ is clear from the context, is defined
to be i∈[t] L′i . So the trace of a cycle is the homeomorphic image of the unit circle and the trace of a
path is an arc in Σ with both endpoints in N (δ).
    We say that a cycle Q of G is δ-contractible if Σ \ trace(Q) contains at least one open disk. Let
B ⊆ N (δ). Given a δ-contractible cycle C of G whose trace is disjoint from B, we call the disk of Σ, if
it exists, that is bounded by trace(C) and is disjoint from B, the B-avoiding disk of C. Notice that the
B-avoiding disk of C does not always exist, but if it exists, it is unique.

Linkages. Let G be a graph. A linkage in G is a set L = {L1 , . . . , Lr } of pairwise vertex-disjoint
paths.
    S In a slight abuse of notation, if L is a linkage, we use V (L) and E(L) to denote L∈L V (L)
                                                                                          S
and L∈L E(L) respectively. Given two sets A and B, we say that a linkage L is an A-B-linkage if
every path in L has one endpoint in A and one endpoint in B.



                                                      32
Segments in a society. Let ⟨G, Λ⟩ be a society where Λ = ⟨y1 , . . . , yr ⟩. A segment of Λ is a set
S ⊆ V (Λ) such that there do not exist s1 , s2 ∈ S and t1 , t2 ∈ V (Λ) \ S such that s1 , t1 , s2 , t2 occur in
the rotation ordering of Λ in the order listed. A vertex s ∈ S is an endpoint of the segment S if there is
a vertex t ∈ V (Λ) \ S which immediately precedes or immediately succeeds s in the rotation ordering
of Λ. Notice that for each ⟨i, j⟩ ∈ |Λ| × |Λ| we may define a segment of the society ⟨G, Λ⟩ that starts
with yi and finishes with yj . We call this segment the (i, j)-segment of ⟨G, Λ⟩ and denote it by yi Λyj .

Transactions in a society. Let ⟨G, Λ⟩ be a society. A transaction in ⟨G, Λ⟩ is an A-B-linkage for
disjoint segments A, B of Λ. We define the depth of ⟨G, Λ⟩ as the maximum order of a transaction in
⟨G, Λ⟩.
    Given a Σ decomposition δ of a graph G, if ∆ is a δ-aligned disk of Σ (resp. a cell c of δ), then the
depth of ∆ (resp. c) is the depth of some –actually any– ∆-society (resp. c-society).
    Let T be a transaction in a society ⟨G, Λ⟩. We say that T is planar if no two members of T form
a cross in ⟨G, Λ⟩. An element P ∈ T is peripheral if there exists a segment X of Λ containing both
endpoints of P and no endpoint of another path in T . A transaction is crooked if it has no peripheral
element.
    Let ρ be a rendition of a society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆, let ∆∗ ⊆ ∆ be an arcwise connected set and let
C = {C1 , . . . , Cs } be an s-nest in ρ around ∆∗ . We say that a family of pairwise vertex-disjoint paths
P in G is a radial linkage if each path in P has at least one endpoint in V (Λ) and the other endpoint
of P is drawn in ∆∗ . Moreover, we say that P is orthogonal to C if for every P ∈ P and every i ∈ [s],
Ci ∩ P consists of a single component. Similarly, if P is a transaction in ⟨G, Λ⟩ then P is said to be
orthogonal to C if for every i ∈ [s] and every P ∈ P, Ci ∩ P consists of exactly two components.

2.3    Fibers and folios of linear societies
In this subsection, we define the notion of fibers as a means to formalize a form of “rooted” subdivision
of a given society. Moreover, we define the notion of d-folio which is a set that contains all different
non-isomorphic fibers that represent some society.

Fibers. A triple µ = (M, T, Λ) is a fiber if

   1. ⟨M, Λ⟩ is a linear society,

   2. T ⊆ V (M ),

   3. for every vertex x of V (M ) \ T it holds that

         • if x ̸∈ V (Λ), then x has degree two in M and
         • if x ∈ V (Λ), then x has degree one or two in M.

    The detail of µ is defined as detail(µ) := |T | and the boundary size of µ is |B|.
    We define dissolve(µ) as the society ⟨H, Λ⟩ where H is the graph obtained from M after contracting
all edges with at least one endpoint not in T ∪ V (Λ) (while contracting, vertices in T ∪ V (Λ) prevail).
Notice that, in this case, the society ⟨M, Λ⟩ is a subdivision of the society dissolve(µ).
    We say that two fibers µ1 , µ2 are equivalent if the societies dissolve(µ1 ) and dissolve(µ2 ) are
isomorphic.
    A fiber µ = (M, T, Λ) is a fiber of ⟨G, Λ′ ⟩ if M is a subgraph of G where V (M ) ∩ V (Λ′ ) = V (Λ)
and Λ ⊆ Λ′ (i.e., Λ is a sub-sequence of Λ′ ).




                                                      33
Folio of a society. Let d be a positive integer. We define the d-folio of a linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ as

d-folio(G, Λ)     = {dissolve(µ) | µ is a fiber of ⟨G, Λ′ ⟩, of detail at most d, where Λ′ is a shift of Λ}.

   Let δ be a Σ-decomposition of a graph G in a surface Σ. We define the d-folio of δ as
                                  [
                    d-folio(δ) :=    d-folio(Gc , Λc,x ) | c ∈ Cf (δ) and x ∈ B∆c .

   We note that the size of the d-folio of δ is upper bounded by an exponential function that depends
on the detail d, which is essentially the number of different graphs we can have on d vertices.
Observation 2.2. There exists a function f2.2 : N → N such that, for every d ∈ N≥1 , for every
Σ-decomposition δ of a graph in a surface Σ,

                                               |d-folio(δ)| ≤ f2.2 (d).

Moreover,
                                                                 2
                                                 f2.2 (d) = 2O(d ) .

2.4    t-segments
In this subsection, we introduce various types of wall-like structures that we uniformly call t-segments.
The different types of t-segments will collectively define the notion of a t-walloid which will serve as
the underlying infrastructure, in its various evolving forms, throughout the refinement steps that our
Σ-decomposition will go through in the proofs spanning the next sections.

Grids and (cylindrical) walls. An (n × m)-grid is the graph with vertex set [n] × [m] and edge set

                {(i, j)(i, j + 1) | i ∈ [n], j ∈ [m − 1]} ∪ {(i, j)(i + 1, j) | i ∈ [n − 1], j ∈ [m]}.

We call the path where vertices appear as (i, 1), (i, 2), . . . , (i, m) the ith row and the path where vertices
appear as (1, j), (2, j), . . . , (n, j) the jth column of the grid.
    Let z, t ∈ N≥3 . An elementary (z, t)-wall is obtained from the (2z × t)-grid by removing a maximal
induced matching M that does not contain any edge of its horizontal paths. A (z, t)-wall is any graph
obtained from an elementary (z, t)-wall after arbitrarily subdividing edges. Notice that a (z, t)-wall W
can be seen as the union of t horizontal paths and z vertical paths that are defined as follows.
    The t horizontal paths P1 , . . . , Pt , ordered (and visualized) from top to bottom are the subdivisions
of the t rows of the original (2z × t)-grid. We call i, the index of the path Pi . We refer to P1 (resp. Pt )
as the top (resp. bottom) path of W. The top (resp. bottom) boundary vertices of W are vertices of the
original (2z × t)-grid that belong in the top (resp. bottom) path of W and are incident to edges of the
maximal induced matching M. Notice that there are z top and z bottom boundary vertices. The z
vertical paths Q1 , . . . , Qz , ordered (and visualized) from left to right are the pairwise disjoint paths
whose union meets all vertices of the original (2z × t)-grid, and where each Qi has one endpoint in the
top and the other in the bottom boundary vertices of W.
    For i ∈ [z] the i-th top (resp. bottom) boundary vertex of W is the single vertex of P1 ∩ Qi (resp.
Pt ∩ Qi ). For i ∈ [t] the i-th leftmost (resp. rightmost) boundary vertex of W is the single vertex of
Pi ∩ Q1 (resp. Pi ∩ Qz ). Notice that all top, bottom, leftmost, and rightmost boundary vertices are
vertices of degree at most two in W. Also, notice that P1 ∪ Q1 ∪ Pt ∪ Qz is a cycle, which we call the
perimeter of W.
    Let s ∈ N≥3 . An (t, s)-cylindrical wall is obtained from a (t, s)-wall after adding s pairwise disjoint
non-trivial paths P1∗ , . . . , Ps∗ whose internal vertices are not in the (t, s)-wall we start from and where
for each i ∈ [t], Pi∗ connects the i-th leftmost boundary vertex with the i-th rightmost boundary vertex


                                                         34
of (t, s)-wall we start from. Notice that a (t, s)-cylindrical wall, has neither leftmost nor rightmost
boundary vertices, however, it has top (resp. bottom) boundary vertices that are the top (resp.
bottom) boundary vertices of the original (t, s)-wall plus the path P1∗ (resp. Ps∗ ). Also, the s horizontal
paths of the (t, s)-wall together with the newly added paths, define s vertex disjoint cycles C1 , . . . , Cs ,
ordered from top to bottom. We call the cycle C1 (resp. Cs ) the inner (resp. outer ) cycle of the
(t, s)-cylindrical wall and they are visualized as such.

Segments. We refer to a graph as a t-segment if it belongs to one of the following categories.
(z, t)-wall segment. Given z and t ∈ N≥3 , an (elementary) (z, t)-wall segment W is an (elementary)

and the (vt , u1 )-path that is a subpath of the top path of W       c.

(t, b, h)-parterre segment. Given a simple society ⟨H, Λ⟩ and b, h ∈ N≥1 , a (t, b, h, H, Λ)-parterre
segment is defined as follows. Consider a sequence F1 , . . . , Fh of disjoint (t, b, H, Λ)-flower segments.
Then, for i ∈ [h] and j ∈ [t], add an edge between the j-th rightmost boundary vertex of Si and the


                                                                          35
j-th leftmost boundary vertex of Si and subdivide this edge an arbitrary number of times. We refer to
F1 , . . . , Fh as the flowers and to ⟨H, Λ⟩ as the society of the defined (t, b, h, H, Λ)-parterre segment.
Also, we refer to the rainbows and inner cycles of its flowers as the rainbows, and inner cycles of the
parterre segment. Notice that a (t, b, h, H, Λ)-parterre segment has h many flowers, rainbows, and
inner cycles.
(t, s)-Thicket segment. Given t, s ∈ N≥3 we define a (t, s)-thicket segment by considering the disjoint
union of an (t, t)-wall W    f , whose top boundary vertices are v1 , . . . , vt in left to right order, and a
(t, s + 2)-cylindrical wall W  c whose bottom boundary vertices are u1 , . . . , ut in cyclic order. Then, we
add the edges in {vi ui | i ∈ [t]} and we subdivide them an arbitrary number of times. We denote by
P1 , . . . , Pt the paths obtained by the previous subdivisions.
     We refer to the inner (resp. outer) cycle of the cylindrical wall W    c as the inner (resp. outer ) cycle
of the (t, s)-thicket segment. Let R = {R1 , . . . , Rt } be the linkage of W  c that consists of its vertical
paths in left to right order and let C = {C1 , . . . , Cs } be the cycles of W minus its outer and inner cycle
                                                                             c
ordered so that C1 is the cycle succeeding the inner cycle of W        c while Cs is the cycle preceding the
outer cycle of W    c . Notice that each path Ri in R is a path connecting the i-th top boundary vertex of
c with the t-th bottom boundary vertex of W
W                                                 c , say ui . We now enhance the paths R = {R1 , . . . , Rt }
by updating each Ri := Ri ∪ Pi . We call the pair (C, R) the railed nest of the (t, s)-thicket segment.
    Notice that in the definition of any of the different types of a t-segment W, we start by considering
a wall W
       f with t horizontal paths. We call this wall the base wall of W. The leftmost boundary vertices
as well as the rightmost boundary vertices of W f form the left boundary and the right boundary of the
t-segment W.

Host and guest walloids. Given a sequence W = ⟨W1 , .S. . , Wl ⟩ of t-segments, we define the
cylindrical concatenation of W as the graph W obtained from W, by adding, for i ∈ [l] and j ∈ [t],
an edge between the j-th rightmost boundary vertex of Wi and the j-th leftmost boundary vertex of
Wi+1 , where Wl+1 = W1 , and then subdividing these edges an arbitrary number of times. We define
two types of cylindrical concatenations of t-segments and we refer to them as t-walloids. Note that W
contains as a subgraph the cylindrical concatenation of the base walls of all segments from W. We
refer to this cylindrical wall as the base cylinder of W.
    Let t, s ∈ N≥3 , b, h ∈ N≥1 , Σ = Σ(h,c) be a surface, and ℓ, ℓ′ ∈ N. Let W = (W1 , . . . , Wh+c+ℓ+ℓ′ +1 )
be a sequence of t-segments where

    • W1 is a (t, t)-wall segment,

    • W2 , . . . , Wh+1 are t-handle segments,

    • Wh+2 , . . . , Wh+c+1 are t-crosscap segments,

    • Wh+c+2 , . . . , Wh+c+ℓ+1 are (t, b, h)-parterre segments, and

    • Wh+c+ℓ++2 , . . . , Wh+c+ℓ+ℓ′ +1 are (t, s)-thicket segments.

   We call the wall-like graph obtained by the cylindrical concatenation W of W a (t, b, h, s, Σ)-host
walloid. In the above construction, if instead of (t, b, h)-parterre segments and/or (t, s)-thicket segments
we only consider (elementary) (t, b)-flower segments, then we define an (elementary) (t, b, Σ)-guest
walloid. Notice that for host walloids we insist that the societies of their parterre segments aresimple
societies. This is not the case for an (elementary) guest walloid as the societies of their flowers are
assumed to be simple. Moreover, we refer to the t-segments of W as the t-segments of W. We denote
by W the graph obtained from W if we remove all internal vertices of its flowers. Clearly, if W does
not have any parterre or flower segments, then W = W .



                                                       36
   Notice that the essential difference between the elementary and non-elementary version of a (t, b, Σ)-
guest walloid is that the elementary version is uniquely defined while the non-elementary one is a
subdivision of the former.
   Let W be a (t, b, h, s, Σ)-host walloid (resp. (t, b, Σ)-guest walloid) as above that has ℓ (t, b, h)-
parterre (resp. (t, b)-flower) segments and ℓ′ (resp. ℓ′ = 0) (t, s)-thicket segments. For i ∈ [ℓ] (resp.
i ∈ [ℓ′ ]) we refer to Wh+c+1+i (resp. Wh+c+ℓ++1+i ) as the i-th (t, b, h)-parterre (resp. (t, b)-flower)
segment (resp. i-th (t, s)-thicket segment) of W.

Classification of facial cycles. Let W be a (t, b, h, s, Σ)-host walloid or an (elementary) (t, b, Σ)-
guest walloid. Notice that W is (the subdivision of) a 3-regular Σ(h,c) -embeddable graph. Consider a
Σ(h,c) -embedding of W . We call bricks the facial cycles that have exactly six degree-3 vertices of W .
    Among the facial cycles of the Σ(h,c) -embedding of W f that are not bricks, 2h many (resp. c many)
of them correspond to the enclosures of the t-handle (resp. t-crosscap) segments, hℓ many of them
correspond to the inner cycles of its (t, b)-flower segments, which we call the flower cycles of W, and
ℓ′ of them correspond to the inner cycles of the (t, s)-cylindrical walls of the ℓ′ many (t, s)-thicket
segments, which we call the thicket cycles of W.
    In the Σ(h,c) -embedding of W , two facial cycles remain unclassified. One of the two corresponds to
the outer cycle of the base cylinder of W which we call the simple cycle of W, denoted by C si . We call
the only remaining unclassified facial cycle, the exceptional cycle of W, denoted by C ex , which is the
only facial cycle that contains the endpoints of every top path of all base walls of the segments of W.
    Let Wf denote the base cylinder of W. A cycle of W is an enclosure if it is an enclosure of some of
its t-handle or its t-crosscap segments or if it is the inner cycle of W
                                                                       f , which we call the big enclosure
of W. Notice that W has 2h + c + 1 enclosures. A cycle C of W is a fence if it is either a facial cycle of
W or if there exists an edge separation (E1 , E2 ) of W , such that
   • V (C) is the set of vertices that are endpoints of edges in both E1 and E2 ,

   • E2 \ E1 ̸= ∅, E1 \ E2 ̸= ∅, and

   • E(C ex ) ⊆ E2 .
    Notice that for every fence C such an edge separation (E1 , E2 ) is uniquely defined. We say that a
fence C ′ of W is inside the fence C if E(C ′ ) ⊆ E1 . A brick of W is a brick of a fence C if it is inside C.
It is easy to see that, given a Σ(h,c) -embedding of W , the fences of W are exactly the non-contractible
cycles of W .

2.5    Meadows, gardens, and orchards.
In this subsection, we build on the definitions of the previous subsection and present various notions
that aim to formalize all the different intermediate states and all properties that our Σ-decomposition
will attain throughout the refinement steps in the proofs of the sections that follow.
   Let t, s ∈ N≥3 , b, h ∈ N≥1 , Σ be a surface, and h ∈ N and c ∈ [0, 2] such that Σ = Σ(h,c) .
Additionally, let δ be a Σ-decomposition of a graph G and W be a δ-grounded (t, b, h, s, Σ)-host walloid
such that
   • for every δ-aligned disk ∆ such that |bd(∆) ∩ N (δ)| ≤ 3, ∆ or its complement contains a single
     cell of δ,

   • every ground vertex of δ is in the same connected component of G as the one containing W, and

   • all other connected components of G are drawn in the interior of a cell of δ without boundary.
   We say that the triple (G, δ, W ) is


                                                     37
    • a (t, Σ)-meadow if W has no (t, b, h)-parterre or (t, s)-thicket segments and δ has a single vortex
      c that is the unique vortex inside the trace(C si )-avoiding disk of trace(C ex ).

    • a (t, b, h, Σ)-garden if it has no (t, s)-thicket segments and δ has a single vortex c that is the unique
      vortex that is inside the trace(C si )-avoiding disk of trace(C ex ). Moreover the (t, b, h)-parterre
      segments of a garden have pairwise different societies.

    • a (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard if W has as many (t, s)-thickets as the vortices of δ and each vortex of δ
      is inside the trace(C si )-avoiding disk of the trace of the inner cycle of a (t, s)-thicket. As before
      (t, b, h)-parterre segments of a garden have pairwise different societies.
      A thicket society of (G, δ, W ) is a society ⟨H, Λ⟩ where Λ is a linearization of a cyclic ordering of
      those vertices of Λ corresponding to the nodes contained in the trace of the outer cycle Cs+1 of a
      (t, s)-thicket segment of W and H is the subgraph of G drawn in the trace(C si )-avoiding disk of
      trace(Cs+1 ).
      If δ has a single vortex then we say that (G, δ, W ) is a single-thicket (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard.

     A Σ-decomposition      δ of G with vortices c1 , . . . , cℓ is (x, y)-fertile if there are positive integers
x1 , . . . , xℓ where i∈[ℓ] xi = x and such that any vortex society of δ, has a rendition with xi vortices,
                     P
each of depth at most y. We also say that δ is (z, p)-ripe if it has at most z vortices, each of depth at
most p. Notice that if δ is (z, p)-ripe, then it is also (z, p)-fertile.
     The δ-influence of a fence C, denoted by δ-influence(C), is the union of all simple cells of δ that
contain at least one edge of C and the cells of δ whose disk is a subset of the trace(C si ) ∩ N (δ)-avoiding
disk of the trace of C. Note that a cell can belong to the influence of at most three of the fences of W.
Moreover, disjoint fences have disjoint influences.
     Let d ∈ N≥1 . The d-folio of a fence C of W is the union of the d-folios of all c-societies, where c is
a cell in the δ-influence of C, i.e.,

                     d-folio(C) := {d-folio(Gc , Λc,x ) | c ∈ δ-influence(C) and x ∈ B∆c }.

A fence of W is d-homogeneous if all its bricks have the same (in terms of society isomorphism) d-folio.
We moreover say that the triple (G, δ, W ) is

    • d-plowed if all its 2h + c + 1 enclosures are d-homogeneous,

    • (x, y)-fertile if δ is (x, y)-fertile,

    • (z, p)-ripe if δ is (z, p)-ripe and every thicket society of (G, δ, W ) has depth at most 2s + p, and

    • d-blooming if every linear society ⟨H, Λ⟩ in d-folio(δ) is the linear society of a (t, b)-parterre
      segment of W.


3     Preliminary results
In this section, we present two independent results that will be of use for proofs in the following
sections. In the first subsection, we prove a homogeneity lemma for walls and a mono-dimensional
variant for ladders. In the second subsection, we prove a result that allows us to control to some extent
the behavior of a “minimal” linkage traversing multiple nests.

3.1     Finding a homogeneous subwall
In this subsection we show how given a large enough wall whose bricks come equipped with a subset
from a fixed set of colors, we can always find an arbitrarily large subwall of it where all bricks


                                                       38
are accompanied by the same color set. We moreover observe how the same arguments give us a
mono-dimensional variant applied to an object we call a ladder.
   Let ζ ∈ N≥1 , z, t ∈ N≥3 , and W be a (z, t)-wall. Assume that every brick B of W is assigned a
subset of [ζ] which we call the ζ-palette of B, denoted by ζ-palette(B). We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1. There exists a function f3.1 : N2 → N and an algorithm that, given ζ ∈ N≥1 , z, t ∈ N≥3 ,
and an (f3.1 (z, ζ), f3.1 (t, ζ))-wall W, outputs a (z, t)-wall W ′ that is a subwall of W and such that
every brick of W ′ has the same ζ-palette in time

first path of Qi+1 .

    Let L be a subdivision of the (2 × t)-grid, where t ∈ N≥ 2. We call L a t-ladder. Notice that L can
be seen as the union of t horizontal paths P1 , . . . , Pt and two vertical paths Q1 , Q2 which correspond
to the subdivided rows and columns respectively of the original (2 × t)-grid. We say that a ladder
L′ is a subladder of L if L′ is obtained from L by removing the edges of some (maybe none) of its
horizontal paths. Consider a plane-embedding of L obtained from the natural plane-embedding of the
(2 × t)-grid. We call all facial cycles of L, except the cycle that bounds the outer face, the bricks of L.
For ζ ∈ N≥1 , we define the ζ-palette of a brick of L as before.
   We get the following mono-dimensional corollary of Lemma 3.1, by applying the same inductive
argument only in the dimension of the horizontal paths.

Corollary 3.2. There is an algorithm that, given ζ ∈ N≥1 , t ∈ N≥2 , and an f3.1 (t, ζ)-ladder L, outputs
a t-ladder L′ that is a subladder of L and such that every brick of L′ has the same ζ-palette in time

                                                       39
3.2    Taming a linkage crossing large nests
In this subsection we prove that for every linkage in a graph G that “invades” a number of large enough
nests in a Σ-decomposition of G we can appropriately define a “minimal” linkage equivalent to the first
with a number of additional information that will be useful to bound the complexity of the extensions
of models we have to consider. We begin with a few necessary definitions related to the unique linkage
function ℓ : N → N which we already discuss in the introduction.
    Let L be a linkage in a graph G. The endpoints of the paths of L are called terminals. The pattern
of L is the set of pairs of endpoints of the paths of L. L is equivalent to a linkage L′ in G if they have
the same pattern.
    An LB-pair of G is a pair (L, B) where B is a subgraph of G with maximum degree two and L is a
linkage in G. We define cae(L, B) := |E(L) \ E(B)| to be the number of edges of the paths of L that
are not edges of B.
    The following proposition, restated in the terminology of this paper, is proved in [26]. It essentially
states that if a linkage in a graph G crosses through a subgraph of G of maximum degree two in a
complicated way, i.e., creating large treewidth, then there is a way to simplify the linkage.

Proposition 3.3 (Lemma 1, [26]). Let G be a graph and (L, B) be an LB-pair of G. Then, if
tw(L ∪ B) > ℓ(|L|), G contains a linkage R such that

  1. cae(R, B) < cae(L, B),

  2. R is equivalent to L, and

  3. R ⊆ L ∪ B.

   Given a δ-grounded cycle C of some Σ-decomposition δ of some graph, we denote by G−
                                                                                     C the graph
obtained by the union of the graphs Gc , where c ⊆ ∆C and Gc ∩ C = ∅.
    We proceed with the main result of this subsection which gives us our desired minimal linkage with
all of the necessary additional properties that we discussed. The running time follows from standard
irrelevant vertex arguments used in order to compute the minimal linkage implied by the lemma.

index n ∈ [bi ] such that xn = sw and xn+1 = tw′ , for some w, w′ ∈ [ri ]. Let Rw (resp. Rw′ ) be the
sw -tw -(resp. sw′ -tw′ )-path of R. Let I ∈ R′i (resp. O ∈ R′i ) be the subpath of Rw (resp. Rw′ ) such
that xn (resp. xn+1 ) is an endpoint of I (resp. O). Let y (resp. z) be the other endpoint of I (resp.
O). Then, let Is (resp. It ) be the minimal subpath of I whose endpoints are xn (resp. y) and a vertex
of V (C1i ). Symmetrically, let Ot (resp. Os ) be the minimal subpath of O whose endpoints are xn+1
(resp. z) and a vertex of VC1i . Also let (P1 , . . . , Psi ) be the sequence of the paths in Pi ordered from
innermost to outermost, and let (P1′ , . . . , Ps′i ) be the xi -yi -paths that are minimal subpaths of Pi ,
where ui ∈ V (Is ) and vi ∈ V (Ot ). Observe that, by the previous arguments and since the endpoints
xn and xn+1 are consecutive in Λi , Pt′i +1 as well as Ps′i −ti −1 is not intersected by any other path in
Ri . Hence we can obtain a new linkage R′ from R by swapping the pairs (sw , tw ) and (sw′ , tw ) of the
pattern of R as follows. We define R′ := R \ {Rw , Rw′ } ∪ {S, T }, where

                     S := sw Rw xn ∪ xn Is usi −ti −1 ∪ Ps′i −ti −1 ∪ vsi −ti −1 It ∪ yRw′ tw′

and
                      T := sw′ Rw′ z ∪ zOvti +1 ∪ Pt′i +1 ∪ vti +1 Oxn+1 ∪ xn+1 Rw′ tw′ .
   Since, sigΛL (Qi , Λi ) = sigΛL (Qj , Λj ) we can perform the exact same change within sigΛL (Qj , Λj ) and
obtain a linkage R′′ that is equivalent to R and we can moreover observe that cae(R′′i , Ci ) < cae(Ri , Ci )
and cae(R′′j , Cj ) < cae(Rj , Cj ), which is a contradiction. Hence R also satisfies property 4.


4     Plowing a meadow
In this section, we obtain a preliminary version of our Σ-decomposition in the form of a meadow
and our main goal is to prove Lemma 4.3. That is, we show that any meadow with large enough
infrastructure can be refined to a meadow that is d-plowed. The first subsection deals with the creation
of our preliminary meadow. The second subsection deals with the proof of Lemma 4.3.

4.1     Creating a meadow
In this subsection we use the results of [77] to create our preliminary meadow. This preliminary
step makes an important case distinction for us as it will have two outcomes, either we find a large
clique minor, or we obtain the preliminary meadow which will then be further refined in the following
subsections. Due to this, the only other case where we have to deal with the clique minor is in Section 9
where we combine all of the intermediate steps into one central theorem, namely Theorem 9.2.


                                                          42
    We need a definition to describe what it means for a clique-minor to be “local” with respect to some
wall. For this notice that, if a graph G contains Kt as a minor, there exist t pairwise vertex disjoint
connected subgraphs X1 , . . . , Xt such that for every pair i, j ∈ [t] there exists an edge with one end in
Xi and the other in Xj . Indeed, every Kt -minor in G is witnessed by such a family. Now let W be a
k-wall where k ≥ t + 1. We say that a clique minor is grasped by W if there exist sets ⟨Xi |i ∈ t⟩ as
above such that for every i ∈ [t] there exists a pair (hi1 , hi2 ) ∈ [k] × [k] such that the intersection of the
h1 st row and the h2 nd column of W is contained in Xi . Moreover, for i =       ̸ j we have that hi1 ̸= hj1 and
 i      j
h2 ̸= h2 .

Observation 4.1 (Meadow creation). There exist functions f4.1  1     2
                                                                  , f4.1 : N3 → N and an algorithm that,
given positive integers g, k, t and h, a graph G, and an f4.1 (h, k, t)-wall W ′ as input, finds, in time
                                                          1

Oh,t (|G|3 ) either

   1. a K3k2 +k·h -minor grasped by W ′ ,

   2. there exist non-negative integers h′ and c′ ≤ 2 such that 2h′ + c′ = g and a subdivision W ′ of a
      walloid which is a cylindrical concatenation of h′ t-handle segments and c′ t-crosscap segments
      in G − A such that the base cylinder of W ′ is a subgraph of W, or
                                         2
   3. a set A ⊆ V (G) of size at most f4.1 (h, k, t) and an (x, y)-fertile (t, Σ)-meadow (G − A, δ, W )
      where Σ is of Euler genus less than g.

Moreover, we have x ∈ O(k 4 · h), y ∈ 2polyg (k) · t, f4.1
                                                       1                                  2
                                                           (h, k, t) ∈ polyg (k, t), and f4.1 (h, k, t) ∈ 2polyg (k) · t.

    For this let us first state the technical key result of [77] which acts as our point of departure. Please
notice that the following statement is an abbreviated version of Lemma 5.5 from [77] where we only
state those parts of the conclusion that are relevant to our case. The main difference to the way the
Lemma is stated in [77] is that it produces a walloid representing a surface instead of a clique-minor
(this walloid is actually found within the clique-minor in the proof of [77]). For our purposes, however,
the clique-minor is much more convenient. Moreover, notice that the first and the second outcome are
merged together in the original statement. For our purposes, it is slightly more convenient to state
them separately.

Lemma 4.2 (an abbreviated version of Lemma 5.5 from [77]). Let g, k, and q be non-negative integers,
where r ≥ 16 · 12(1622g + 4)k and q ≥ 6k, and set

                                         t   :=   12(1622g + 4)k,
                                       M     2(1622g + 4)q,
                                             :=
                                                                   
                                                 1       107 t26
                                        µ :=           t         · M , and
                                               105 t26
                                        R := 49152 · t24 (3 + 4M ) + µ.

Let W be an R-wall in a graph G. Then either,

   1. G contains a Kt -minor grasped by W,

   2. there exist

          • a surface Σ of Euler-genus g ′ < g,
          • a set A ⊆ V (G) of size at most µ, and
          • a Σ-decomposition δ = (Γ, D) of G − A with the following properties:
             (a) δ is of breadth at most 2t2 and depth at most µ,


                                                           43
             (b) there exist non-negative integers h′ and c′ ≤ 2 such that 2h′ + c′ = g ′ and a subdivision
                 WΣ of a walloid which is a cylindrical concatenation of h q-handle segments and c
                 q-crosscap segments in G − A such that WΣ is grounded in δ,
             (c) the base cylinder of WΣ is a subgraph of W, and
             (d) let X be the set of all vertices of G drawn in the interior of cells of δ and let Gδ be the
                 torso of the set V (G) \ (X ∪ A) in G − A, then Gδ is a quasi-4-connected component of
                 G − A.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that returns one of the outcomes above in time Og,h,k,q (|G|3 )

Proof sketch of Observation 4.1. To see how Observation 4.1 follows from Lemma 4.2 for an appro-
priate choice of numbers first notice that the first outcome of Lemma 4.2 gives the first outcome of
Observation 4.1. So we only have to deal with the second outcome of Lemma 4.2. In this outcome, we
are already given the walloid WΣ . In case the Euler-genus of Σ is at least g we find the desired walloid
for the second outcome of Observation 4.1 within WΣ . Hence, we may assume that the Euler-genus
of Σ is less than g. Let now C denote the exception cycle of WΣ and ∆ be the disk bounded by its
trace that avoids the simple face of WΣ . Notice that ∆ induces a society that has a rendition in δ with
breadth at most x and depth at most y. By declaring this disk a new cell of δ we obtain the desired
meadow.

4.2     The meadow plowing lemma
We are now ready to proceed with the proof of the main result of this section. This lemma marks
our first major refinement of our initial Σ-decomposition. We prove that given a meadow with large
enough infrastructure, we can maintain a still large infrastructure that has the property that we can
divide into large territories (enclosures) that all have the property of being d-homogeneous, i.e., that
small parts of the model (with respect to d) that invade such a territory can be represented anywhere
in the area defined by the territory.

Lemma 4.3 (meadow plowing). There exists a function f4.3 : N3 → N and an algorithm that, given
t ∈ N≥3 , d ∈ N≥1 , γ ∈ N, and an (x, y)-fertile (f4.3 (t, d, γ), Σ)-meadow (G, δ, W ), where Σ = Σh+c and
γ = h + c, outputs an (x, y)-fertile (t, Σ)-meadow that is d-plowed in time
                                                             2
                                                         2O(d )
                                                   (tγ)2          |G|2 .

Moreover                                                                  2
                                                                      2O(d )
                                             f4.3 (t, d, γ) ∈ (tγ)2            .

f3.1 (2t, f2.2 (d))}.

f2.2 (d). Our proof works as follows. We first show how to define a (t, Σ)-walloid W ′ that is the
cylindrical concatenation of h many t-handle segments and c many t-crosscap segments, as a subgraph
of W, with the property that all its 2h + c + 1 enclosures are d-homogeneous in δ. Then, we show how
to define a Σ-decomposition δ ′ from δ such that (G, δ ′ , W ′ ) is the required (x, y)-fertile (t, Σ)-meadow
(G, δ ′ , W ′ ) that is d-plowed.

where each of their t′ many horizontal paths contains a common vertex with two vertical paths of W3 .

of each of these bricks and then apply Corollary 3.2, to obtain t-ladders L′i , Ri′ and 2t-ladders Si′ whose
bricks individually all have the same d-folio. Finally, we can obtain our desired (t, Σ)-walloid W5 by
considering a sub-cylindrical wall of W3 that contains all cycles of W3 but only the vertical paths that
contain an endpoint of some horizontal path of some L′i , Ri′ , Si′ , union all L′i , Ri′ , Si′ . Notice that all
2h + c + 1 enclosures of W5 are now d-homogeneous.

considering the same drawing of G in Σ, Γ′ = Γ, and defining the cells D′ as follows. Let ∆ be the
trace(C si )-avoiding disk C ex , where C ex is the exceptional cycle of W5 while C si is the simple cycle of
W5 . Define D′ to contain all cells of δ not drawn in ∆, as well as a new cell that is the closure of all
cells of δ drawn in ∆. it is easy to observe that δ ′ is indeed as required.


5     Sowing a garden
In this section, we obtain the second major refinement of our Σ-decomposition in the form of a
d-blooming garden and our main goal is to prove Lemma 5.4. We show how given a d-plowed meadow
with enough infrastructure, we can refine it into a garden that is d-blooming.

5.1    Planting the seeds
In this subsection, we first introduce the notion of a fence at some distance from some other fence in a
walloid, which will be useful for several arguments concerning the routing of paths in our walloid in
the following proofs. We then prove a couple of auxiliary results that will serve as intermediate steps
towards the proof of Lemma 5.4.
   Let (G, δ, W ) be a (t, Σ)-meadow of a graph G in a surface Σ for some t ∈ N≥3 . Let F be a fence of
W. We say that F0 := F is the fence at distance 0 from F in W. Given r ∈ N≥1 , we define recursively


                                                       46
the fence at distance r from F in W as follows. If Fr−1 is disjoint from C si and C ex , then Fr is the
unique fence of W whose bricks are exactly the bricks of Fr−1 union all bricks of W that share a
vertex with some brick of Fr−1 . We call a fence F of W r-internal if the fence at distance r from F is
well-defined.
    Given an 1-internal fence F of W, we define its internal pegs (resp. external pegs) as its vertices
that are incident to edges of W that are not edges of F and that are edges of bricks of F (resp. of
bricks that are not bricks of F ).
   It follows from the properties of δ that for every flap cell c of δ there are |e
                                                                                  c| vertex-disjoint paths to
any |e
     c| many distinct vertices not in σδ (c). This observation allows us to use the following proposition
which is a restatement of [7, Lemma 22] in the terminology used in this text.

Proposition 5.1 (Lemma 22, [7]). Let t ∈ N≥3 , Σ be a surface, and (G, δ, W ) be a (t, Σ)-meadow
of G. For every 2-internal brick B of W and every flap cell c in the δ-influence of B, W contains |e        c|
                           c to the external pegs of the fence F at distance 1 from B in W. Moreover

2 from B in W.

   The following lemma will be useful in order to extract the flower segments needed to obtain the
desired garden in Lemma 5.4.

Lemma 5.2. Let t ∈ N≥3 , Σ be a surface, and (G, δ, W ) be a (t, Σ)-meadow of G. For every 4-internal

Proof. Let B be a 4-internal brick of W, c be a flap cell in the δ-influence of B, and fix a c-society
(Gc , Λc,y ). Moreover, let F1 , . . . , F4 denote the fences at distances 1 up to 5 from B and W ′ be a
(10 × 10)-wall W ′ that is a subgraph of W that is a wall and contains all bricks of F4 .
    We argue about how to find the desired paths when |e     c| = 3. Then, the other cases follow immediately.
W.l.o.g. we assume that the rotation tie-breaker gives c the counter-clockwise rotation. Let Λc,y =
⟨x, y, z⟩. First apply Proposition 5.1 for B and c, and let Px , Py , Pz be the implied paths where x′ , y ′ , z ′
are their respective endpoints in F1 . Observe that F1 has 12 external pegs which can be partitioned in
four sets T, L, B, R, where T contains the 4 top external pegs, L contains the 2 leftmost external pegs,
B contains the 4 bottom pegs, and R contains the 2 rightmost pegs.
    We begin visiting the external pegs of F in counter-clockwise ordering starting from z ′ . Respecting
this ordering the next vertex to appear is x′ and then y ′ . We proceed to distinguish cases depending
on where the vertices x′ , y ′ , and z ′ are on F1 , and define three paths Px′ , Py′ , Pz′ as follows.
Case distinction for z ′ .
    z ′ ∈ T. We define the path Pz′ starting from z ′ , moving upwards towards F4 following the vertical
path of W ′ that contains z ′ , following F4 in clockwise direction until we reach a vertex of F4 that
belongs to the rightmost possible vertical path of W ′ , and then following this vertical path downwards
to its bottom boundary vertex of W ′ .
Case distinction for x′ and y ′ , when z ′ ∈ T.
    x′ ∈ T (resp. y ′ ∈ T ). We define the path Px′ (resp. Py′ ) starting from x′ (resp. y ′ ), moving
upwards towards F3 (resp. F2 ) following the vertical path of W ′ that contains x′ (resp. y ′ ), following
F3 (resp. F2 ) in counter-clockwise direction until we reach a vertex of F3 (resp. F2 ) that belongs
to the leftmost possible vertical path of W ′ , and then following this vertical path downwards to its
bottom boundary vertex of W ′ .


                                                       47
    x′ ∈ L (resp. y ′ ∈ L). We define the path Px′ (resp. Py′ ) starting from x′ (resp. y ′ ), moving to the
left towards F3 (resp. F2 ) following the horizontal path of W ′ that contains x′ (resp. y ′ ), and then
following the vertical path that contains the vertex of F3 (resp. F2 ) we encountered downwards to its
bottom boundary vertex of W ′ .
    x′ ∈ B (resp. y ′ ∈ B). We define the path Px′ (resp. Py′ ) starting from x′ (resp. y ′ ), moving
downwards towards F3 (resp. F2 ) following the vertical path of W ′ that contains x′ (resp. y ′ ), and
then following the vertical path that contains the vertex of F3 (resp. F2 ) we encountered downwards
to its bottom boundary vertex of W ′ .
    x′ ∈ R (resp. y ′ ∈ R). We define the path Px′ (resp. Py′ ) starting from x′ (resp. y ′ ), moving to
the right towards F2 (resp. F3 ) following the horizontal path of W ′ that contains x′ (resp. y ′ ), and
then following the vertical path that contains the vertex of F2 (resp. F3 ) we encountered downwards
to its bottom boundary vertex of W ′ .
   We skip the remaining cases in favor of brevity and note that they are similar to the first. Now,
observe that, by the specifications of Proposition 5.1 and by definition, the paths Px ∪ Px′ , Py ∪ Py′ ,
and Pz ∪ Pz′ are indeed vertex-disjoint with one endpoint in e   c and the other being a bottom boundary
vertex of W ′ , respecting the order of Λc,v , ordered left to right.

5.2      The garden creation lemma
We require one more auxiliary lemma that shows how to further refine our garden by extracting a
parterre segment that represents a linear society of the d-folio of our Σ-decomposition which is not yet
represented by any other parterre segment.
Lemma 5.3. There exists a function f5.3 : N3 → N and an algorithm that, given t ∈ N≥3 , b, h, d ∈ N≥1 ,
    • an (x, y)-fertile d-plowed (f5.3 (t, b, h), b, h, Σ)-garden (G, δ, W ), and
    • a linear society ⟨H, Λ⟩ ∈ d-folio(δ),
outputs an (x, y)-fertile d-plowed (t, b, h, Σ)-garden (G, δ ′ , W ′ ) such that
    • the set of linear societies of the parterre segments of W ′ contains ⟨H, Λ⟩,
    • is a strict superset of the set of linear societies of the parterre segments of W, and
    • the d-folio of δ ′ is the same as the d-folio of δ
in time
                                                     O(h(t + b)|G|2 ).

                                               f5.3 (t, b, h) ∈ O(h(t + b)).
Proof. We define f5.3 (t, b, h) := t + 6 + h · (2t + 10 · 3b).
    Assume that the Euler-genus of Σ is 2h + c. Let W be the cylindrical contatenation of a sequence
W = (W1 , W1h , . . . , Whh , W1c , . . . , Wcc , W1p , . . . , Wℓp ), where W1 is a (f5.3 (t, b, h), f5.3 (t, b, h))-wall seg-
ment, W1h , . . . , Whh are f5.3 (t, b, h)-handle segments, W1c , . . . , Wcc are f5.3 (t, b, h)-crosscap segments, and
W1p , . . . , Wℓp are (f5.3 (t, b, h), h)-parterre segments.
    Let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be the given linear society in d-folio(δ). By definition ⟨H, Λ⟩ = dissolve(µ), where µ is
a fiber of a c-society ⟨Gc , Λc,x ⟩ of some cell c ∈ Cf (δ) of detail at most d, where Λ is a shift of Λc,x .
First, notice that c belongs in the δ-influence of at least one of the enclosures of W. Moreover, since
(G, δ, W ) is d-plowed, i.e., all 2h + c + 1 enclosures of W are d-homogeneous, the δ-influence of every
brick of an enclosure whose δ-influence contains c, contains a cell c′ ∈ Cf (δ) such that a c′ -society of δ
has the same d-folio as ⟨Gc , Λc,x ⟩.


                                                             48
     The proof proceeds by demonstrating how to extract a (t, b, h, H, Λ)-parterre segment depending

     First, let us observe the following. Let t′ := t + 6 + h · (2t + 10b), and F1 := C ex . For every fence Fr ,
r ∈ [t′ ], at distance r from C ex , we can naturally define a (t′ − r, Σ)-walloid Wr with the same number
of handle and crosscap segments and with the same number of parterre segments whose linear societies
are the same, where only their order changes. Indeed, let Wr be the graph obtained by the union of the
cycles of the base cylindrical wall of W that F1 , . . . , Fr do not intersect, the paths of the rainbows all
handles, crosscap, and parterre segments that F1 , . . . , Fr do not intersect, the vertical paths of W that
F1 , . . . , Fr do not intersect, all linear societies of the parterre segments, and the maximal subpaths of
all vertical paths of the walloid’s wall segment whose endpoints lie in cycles that F1 , . . . , Fr do not
intersect.
     Observe that F1 , . . . , Fr intersect the cycles C1 , . . . , Cr of the base cylindrical wall of W, the first r
paths of both rainbows of handle segments, the first r and the last r paths of rainbows of crosscap
segments, the first r paths of rainbows of parterre segments, as well as all vertical paths that are not
disjoint from the former rainbow paths. Also, note that the linear society of each parterre segment is
unaffected as we simply extend the boundary with paths towards the new top boundary vertices of the
new base cylindrical wall of W ′ . Finally, note that the number of vertical paths in the wall segment of
Wr is the same as in W.

avoiding disk of C ex , where C ex is the exceptional cycle of W ′ while C si is the simple cycle of W ′ .
Define D′ to contain all cells of δ not drawn in ∆, as well as a new cell that is the closure of all cells of
δ drawn in ∆. Observe that the big enclosure of W ′ is a fence of W contained in the big enclosure of W
and as such remains d-homogeneous. Moreover the remaining 2h + c enclosures of W ′ that correspond
to the enclosures of its handle and crosscap segments can be seen to also be d-homogeneous as they


                                                           51
contain all bricks of their corresponding handle and crosscap enclosure in W as well as bricks that were,
in W, contained in its big enclosure, and all such bricks had the same d-folio. Additionally, since the
new unique vortex cell of δ only grew by the addition of flap cells of δ, implies that (G, δ ′ , W ′ ) remains
(x, y)-fertile, and the previous fact combined with the fact that all enclosures of W are d-homogeneous,
also implies that the d-folio of δ ′ is the same as the d-folio of δ. Finally, by construction, it is easy to
see that the set of linear societies of the parterre segments of W ′ contains ⟨H, Λ⟩ and is a superset of
the set of linear societies of the parterre segments of W.
    Note that in the way we extract flowers with the previous argument each copy of a linear society
within the flowers contains one copy for each linear society in dec(H, Λ). If we replace b with 3b we
can deal with this issue by keeping from each linear society only the copy of the component of the
decomposition we want in the correct order.




                      Figure 13: Extracting a flower segment from a wall segment.

    We are now in the position to prove the main result of this section, which is an iterative application
of Lemma 5.3. Our goal is to represent each linear society of the d-folio of our Σ-decomposition by a
parterre segment containing many enough copies of the linear society it represents.
Lemma 5.4 (Garden creation). There exists a function f5.4 : N4 → N and an algorithm that, given
t ∈ N≥3 , b, h, d ∈ N≥1 , and an (x, y)-fertile (f5.4 (t, b, h, d), Σ)-meadow (G, δ, W ) that is d-plowed,
outputs an (x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, Σ)-garden that is d-blooming in time
                                                           2
                                            h(t + b)2O(d ) · |G|2 .

Moreover
                                                                       2
                                      f5.4 (t, b, h, d) ∈ h(t + b)2O(d ) .
Proof. We define f5.4 (t, b, h, d) := f5.3 (t, b, h) · f2.2 (d).
    The proof of the lemma is an iterative application of Lemma 5.3 applied each time to a linear
society of the d-folio of the corresponding Σ-decomposition that is not represented by the parterre
segments of the corresponding walloid. First observe that by definition (G, δ, W ) can be seen as an
(x, y)-fertile d-plowed (f5.4 (t, b, h, d), b, h, Σ)-garden with no parterre segments.
    Let (G, δ0 , W0 ) = (G, δ, W ) and i = 0. Iteratively apply Lemma 5.3 on (G, δi , Wi ) with a linear soci-
ety ⟨H, Λ⟩ ∈ d-folio(δi ) that is not the linear society of any parterre segment of Wi . Let (G, δi+1 , Wi+1 )
be the resulting garden. Set i := i + 1, and repeat.
     Observe that the above procedure will repeat at most d′ := |d-folio(δ)| times, since the d-folio
of any of the intermediate Σ-decompositions is the same. Then, we conclude with the (G, δd′ , Wd′ )
(t, b, h, Σ)-garden, which by the specifications of Lemma 5.3 has all the desired properties.


6     Growing an orchard
The goal of this subsection is to prove Lemma 6.2. That is, we show that any d-blooming garden with
big enough infrastructure can be transformed into an orchard that is both d-blooming and ripe. This
step marks the last refinement of our Σ-decomposition before the harvest can begin.


                                                      52
Observation 6.1 (Garden seen as a single thicket orchard). There is an algorithm that, given an
s ∈ N and a (x, y)-fertile and d-blooming (t + 1 + s, b, h, d, Σ)-garden (G, δ, W ), outputs an (x, y)-fertile
and d-blooming single-thicket (t, b, h, s, d, Σ)-orchard (G, δ, W ) in time O(t · s · (b + h)|G|).

Lemma 6.2 (Bounding the depth of a orchard). There exist functions f6.2   1       2

                                                                                     , f6.2    4
                                                                                            , f6.2 : N7 → N and
an algorithm that, given an (x, y)-fertile and d-blooming single-thicket (t′ , b, h, s′ , Σ)-orchard (G, δ, W )
where

                                         t′ ≥ f6.2
                                               1
                                                   (t∗ , b, h, s∗ , x, y, d) and
                                         s′ ≥ f6.2
                                               2
                                                   (t∗ , b, h, s∗ , x, y, d),
                                                                                   ∗ Oh (1)
outputs a (q, p)-ripe and d-blooming (t∗ , b, h, s∗ , Σ)-orchard in time 2t                   |G|3 where
