    The proof of Lemma 6.2 will be an inductive one via a sequence of lemmas which we derive in the
following subsections.

6.1     Developing the tools
Before we begin, we need some preliminary results on transactions in societies.

Strip societies. Since our goal is to refine an (x, y)-fertile blooming garden, or better an (x, y)-fertile
orchard with a single thicket, it suffices to deal with societies for which we already know that there
exists a rendition in a disk with at most x vortices, each of depth at most y. This immediately allows
us to deduce that any large enough transaction in such a society contains a still large subtransaction
which is planar and satisfies a number of additional properties.
   Let G be a graph and H be a subgraph of G. An H-bridge is either an edge e with both endpoints
in H such that e ∈/ E(H), or a subgraph of G formed by a component K of G − V (H) along with all
edges of G with one endpoint in V (K) and the other endpoint in V (H). In the first case, we call the
endpoints of e the attachments of the bridge, and in the second case the attachments of the bridge are
those vertices that do not belong to K.
    Let ⟨G, Λ⟩ be a society and P be a planar transaction of order at least two in ⟨G, Λ⟩. The P-strip
society of ⟨G, Λ⟩ is defined as follows. Let P = {P1 , P2 , . . . , Pℓ } be ordered18 such that for each i ∈ [ℓ],
Pi has the endpoints ai and bi and a1 , a2 , . . . , aℓ , bℓ , bℓ−1 , . . . , b1 appear in Λ in the order listed. We
denote by H the subgraph of G defined by the union of the paths in P together with all vertices of Λ
and by H ′ the subgraph of H consisting of P together with the vertices in V (a1 Λaℓ ) ∪ V (bℓ Λb1 ). We
consider the set B of all H-bridges of G with at least one vertex in V (H ′ ) \ V (P1 ∪ Pℓ ). For each B ∈ B
let B ′ be obtained from B by deleting all attachments that do not belong to V (H ′ ). Finally, let us
denote by G1 the graph defined as the union of H ′ and all B ′ where B ∈ B and let Λ1 := a1 Λaℓ ⊕ bℓ Λb1 .
The resulting society ⟨G1 , Λ1 ⟩ is the desired P-strip society of ⟨G, Λ⟩. The paths P1 and Pℓ are called
the boundary paths of ⟨G1 , Λ1 ⟩.
    We say that the P-strip society ⟨G1 , Λ1 ⟩ of ⟨G, Λ⟩ is isolated if no edge of G has one endpoint in
V (G1 ) \ V (P1 ∪ Pℓ ) and the other endpoint in V (G) \ V (G1 ).
    Moreover, an isolated P-strip society is separating if after deleting any non-boundary path of P
there does not exist a path from one of the two resulting segments of Λ to the other.
  18We assume here that neither the segment a Λa nor the segment b Λb contains both endpoints of Λ. If this is not
                                                1   ℓ                   ℓ   1
the case we may slightly rotate Λ to obtain a new linear ordering Λ′ neither segment contains both endpoints and use Λ′
to derive the P-strip society of ⟨G, Λ⟩.



                                                            53
Lemma 6.3. Let t, x ≥ 1, p, s, y ≥ 2, and b, h be positive integers. Moreover, let (G, δ, W ) be an
(x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard and let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be a thicket society of (G, δ, W ). Then every transaction
in ⟨H, Λ⟩ of order at least (x + 1)(2xy + p) contains a planar transaction P of order p such that the
P-strip society of ⟨H, Λ⟩ is isolated, separating, and rural.

Proof. Since (G, δ, W ) is (x, y)-fertile there exists a rendition ρ of ⟨H, Λ⟩ in the disk with at most x
vortices, each of depth at most y.
    Let L be a transaction of order at least (x + 1)(2y + p) in ⟨H, Λ⟩. Then there exist two disjoint
segments, say I and J such that every path in L has one endpoint in I and the other in J. Let us
assume that I and J are chosen minimally with this property and let L = {L1 , L2 , . . . , Lℓ } be ordered
with respect to the occurrence of the endpoint ui of Li in I.
    Suppose there exists a transaction L′ ⊆ L such that there exists a path P in L′ which crosses all
other paths in L′ Suppose |L′ | > xy then there must exist a transaction L′′ ⊆ L′ of order at least y + 1
and a vortex c of ρ such that L′′ induces a transaction of order at least y + 1 in the vortex society of c.
Since c is of depth at most y this is impossible and thus |L′ | ≤ xy.
    We partition I into x + 1 consecutive segments I1 , I2 , . . . , Ix+1 each containing at least 2xy + p
endpoints of members of L. For each i ∈ [x + 1] there exists a family Li containing p consecutive paths
such that Ii has at least xy other endpoints on either side. Notice that, by the observation above, it
is not possible that a path from Li crosses a path from L which does not have an endpoint in Ii . In
particular, this means that for any choice of i ̸= j ∈ [x + 1] there does not exist a vortex of ρ which
contains an edge of a path in Li and an edge of a path of Lj . Therefore, there must exist i ∈ [x + 1]
such that no path in Li has an edge or a vertex that belongs to a vortex of ρ. It follows that Li is
isolated, separating, and rural as desired.

Exposed transactions. To ensure we are actually making progress with our constructions we
require our transactions to run through the vortex region of our thicket.
    Let x, y, t, b, h, and s be positive integers and (G, δ, W ) be an (x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard.
Moreover, let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be a thicket society of a (t, s)-thicket segment of (G, δ, W ) with nest C =
{C0 , . . . , Cs+1 } and cylindrical rendition ρ := δ ∩ ∆, where ∆ is the traceδ (C si )-avoiding disk of
traceδ (Cs+1 ). A transaction P in ⟨H, Λ⟩ is said to be exposed if the traceρ(Cs+1 )-avoiding disk of
traceρ (C0 ) intersects every path in P.
    Please note that our definition of exposed transactions differs slightly from the definition on [39].
Indeed, we do not require the presence of a vortex at all for a transaction to be exposed, it suffices
that the transaction crosses through the disk defined by the innermost cycle of the nest.

Pruning of an orchard. In [39] it was ensured to always find an exposed transaction because
Kawarabayashi et al. only considered crooked transactions which are forced to traverse the vortex
in their setting. For us, however, since our transactions are not necessarily crooked and we do not
require our cylindrical renditions to be “around” a vortex, this is not the case. We get around this by
using a trick from [76] that allows us to either find, in every large enough transaction, either a big
exposed transaction or a refinement of our orchard that reduced the part of G that belongs to our
thicket society ⟨H, Λ⟩.
    Let x, y, t, b, h, and s be positive integers and (G, δ, W ) be an (x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard. An
(x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard (G, δ, W ′ ) is said to be a pruning of (G, δ, W ) if

    • all handle, crosscap, and parterre segments of W equal the handle, crosscap, and parterre
      segments of W ′ ,

    • if W has ℓ thickets, then there exists j ∈ [ℓ] such that W ′ also has ℓ thickets and all thickets of
      W, expect for the jth thicket, equal the thickets of W ′ except for the jth thicket, and


                                                       54
   • if ⟨H, Λ⟩ is a thicket society of the j-th thicket of W with nest C = {C0 , C1 , . . . , Cs+1 } and
     cylindrical rendition ρ := δ ∩ ∆, where ∆ is the traceδ (C si )-avoiding disk of traceδ (Cs+1 ), and
     ⟨H ′ , Λ′ ⟩ is a thicket society of the j-th thicket of W ′ with nest C = {C0′ , C1′ , . . . , Cs+1
                                                                                                       ′
                                                                                                          } and
     cylindrical rendition ρ′ := δ ∩ ∆′ , where ∆′ is the traceδ (C si )-avoiding disk of traceδ (Cs+1
                                                                                                     ′
                                                                                                         ), then

         – either ∆′ is properly contained in ∆ or
         – there exists i ∈ [s] such that
              ∗ if ∆i is the traceρ (Cs+1 )-avoiding disk of traceρ (Ci ), ⟨Hi , Λi ⟩ is a ∆i -society in ρ, and
              ∗ ∆′i is the traceρ′ (Cs+1
                                     ′
                                         )-avoiding disk of traceρ′ (Ci′ ), ⟨Hi′ , Λ′i ⟩ is a ∆′i -society in ρ′ ,
            then either Hi′ − V (Ci′ ) ⊊ Hi − V (Ci ) or E(Hi′ ) ⊊ E(Hi ).

   Hence, the pruning of an orchard is essentially achieved by “pushing” one of the cycles of the nest of
one of the thickets either a bit closer to the untamed area in the center of the thicket or by extending
the nest into the untamed area, thereby taming a part of the actual vortex inside the thicket.

Lemma 6.4. Let p, x, y, t, b, h, and s be positive integers and (G, δ, W ) be an (x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-
orchard. Let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be a thicket society of (G, δ, W ) and P be a transaction of order at least 2s + p in
⟨H, Λ⟩. Then there exists

   • an exposed transaction Q ⊆ P of order p, or

   • a pruning of (G, δ, W ).

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time O(|G|).

Proof. Let {C0 , . . . , Cs+1 } be the nest paired with the (t, s)-thicket segment that corresponds to
⟨H, Λ⟩, ρ := δ ∩ ∆ be the cylindrical rendition of ⟨H, Λ⟩, where ∆ is the traceδ (C si )-avoiding disk of
traceδ (Cs+1 ), and C = {C1 , . . . , Cs }. If P contains an exposed transaction of order p we are immediately
done. Moreover, we can check in linear time for the existence of such a transaction by checking for
each path in P individually if it is exposed.
    First, observe that we may assume that every path of P is intersected by the traceρ (Cs+1 )-avoiding
disk of traceρ(Cs ). Indeed, if there is a path P which violates this condition then we can define a
δ-grounded cycle Cs+1 ′
                           by replacing a subpath of Cs+1 whose endpoints are the same as the endpoints
of a minimal V (Cs+1 )-V (Cs+1 )-subpath of P in a way that defines a cycle whose trace in δ bounds a
disk ∆′ which contains traceδ (C0 ). It is then easy to see that ∆′ is properly contained in ∆ and by
replacing our working thicket with a thicket whose thicket society corresponds to any ∆′ -society in δ
yields a pruning of (G, δ, W ).
    Hence, we may assume that there exists a linkage Q ⊆ P of order 2s + 1 such that no path of Q is
exposed and every path is intersected by the traceρ (Cs+1 )-avoiding disk of traceρ (Cs ). Let ∆∗ denote
the traceρ (Cs+1 )-avoiding disk of traceρ (C0 ). It follows that Q must be a planar transaction and no
path in Q intersects the ∆∗ . Notice that each member Q of Q naturally separates ∆ into two disks,
exactly one of which intersects ∆∗ (in fact this disk must contain ∆∗ ). Let us call the other disk the
small side of Q. Given two members of Q then either the small side of one is contained in the small
side of the other, or their small sides are disjoint. It is straightforward to see that if we say that two
members are equivalent if their small sides intersect, this indeed defines an equivalence relation on Q.
Moreover, there are exactly two equivalence classes, one of which, call it Q′ , must contain at least s + 1
members. Since |C| = s there must exist some i ∈ [s] and some subpath L of some path in Q′ such that

   • both endpoints of L belong to V (Ci ),

   • L is internally disjoint from i∈[s] V (Ci ),
                                  S

   • L contains at least one edge that does not belong to Ci , and


                                                       55
   • L is drawn in the trace(Ci )-disk that is disjoint from the nodes corresponding to the vertices of
     Λ.

Notice that Ci ∪ L contains a unique cycle C ′ different from Ci whose trace separates ∆∗ from the
nodes corresponding to the vertices of Λ. Moreover, the ∆∗ -containing disk ∆′′ of trace(C ′ ) is properly
contained in the ∆∗ -containing disk of trace(Ci ). In particular, there exists an edge of Ci which does
not belong to Ci′ , and there exists an edge that belongs to Ci′ but not to Ci and therefore, the graph
drawn on ∆′′ after deleting the vertices of Ci′ is properly contained in the graph drawn on ∆′ after
deleting the vertices of Ci .
    We would now like to define the new nest C ′ := {C1 , . . . , Ci−1 , C ′ , Ci+1 , . . . , Cs }. However, to fully
obtain the desired pruning of (G, δ, W ) we need to use the radial linkage R of the thicket in W to
obtain a radial linkage R′ of the same order which is orthogonal to C ′ . First, we are going to change
the cycle C ′ once more. Consider C ′′ to be the cycle obtained from C ′ after iteratively replacing
a subpath of C ′ , in the same way as above, with any V (Ci )-V (Ci ) subpath R′ of path in R that
satisfies the same four properties as L above. It follows that there can be at most one path in R,
say R, that is not orthogonal to C ′′ after performing these replacements. To conclude, now we are
going to change the path R. Let us traverse along C ′′ in the clockwise direction. We may define
xR to be the first vertex of L on R and yR to be the last such vertex. Then there exists a unique
xR -yR -subpath R′′ of R and a unique xR -yR subpath L′ of C ′ such that replacing R′′ by L′ results in
a new path that is now orthogonal to C ′′ . This defines our desired path R′ and allows us to define the
nest C ′ := {C1 , . . . , Ci−1 , C ′′ , Ci+1 , . . . , Cs } and the radial linkage R′ = R ∪ {R′ } \ {R} together with
the new walloid W ′ to obtain our desired pruning of (G, δ, W ).

    Notice that whenever we find a pruning, some edge of G crosses over one of the cycles in some nest
in some thicket of (G, δ, W ). Since we are in a graph excluding some minor, there are at most O(|G|)
many edges. Moreover, the total number of thickets is upper bounded by some constant depending
only on the parameters which also determine the size of the excluded minor. Indeed, similarly, the size
of each nest of each thicket can also be considered to be a constant with the same arguments. This
means that after finding at most O(|G|) many big enough transactions on the thicket societies we have
either found a big exposed transaction or may conclude that all thicket societies are of bounded depth.
    Notice that, in the end, we want to guarantee that not only each thicket society is of bounded depth,
but within every thicket is a vortex which is also of bounded depth, and this vortex is surrounded by a
large nest. The tools we develop in the upcoming subsections will allow us to achieve just that.

Orthogonality. So far we know that we can always either find a pruning of our blooming orchard,
or we find an exposed planar transaction whose strip society is isolated, separating, and rural. An
important ingredient that we will need to develop the tools in the upcoming subsections is that, in
the latter case above, we should also be able to make an exposed planar transaction orthogonal, by
possibly slightly altering the nest that comes with some thicket society. For this purpose we develop
the following tool that builds on ideas from [56].

Lemma 6.5. Let x, y, t, b, h, and s be positive integers and (G, δ, W ) be an (x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-
orchard. Let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be a thicket society of (G, δ, W ) with railed nest (C, R) and ρ := δ ∩ ∆ be the
cylindrical rendition of ⟨H, Λ⟩ around cρ where ∆ is the disk bounded by the trace(C si )-avoiding disk of
the outer cycle of the (t, s)-thicket segment of (G, δ, W ) that corresponds to ⟨H, Λ⟩. Let P be a planar
exposed transaction of order s · (p + 2) in ⟨H, Ω⟩ such that the P-strip society of ⟨H, Λ⟩ is isolated,
separating, and rural. Then there exists

   • an s-nest C ′ in δ ∩ ∆ around the same arcwise connected set as C,

   • a radial linkage R′ that is orthogonal to C ′ and has the same endpoints as R on BΛ , and



                                                         56
   • a planar exposed transaction Q of order p in ⟨H, Ω⟩ that is orthogonal to C ′ such that the Q-strip
     society of ⟨H, Λ⟩ is isolated, separating, and rural.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds the outcome above in time (poly(s · p) + poly(t)) · |G|.

Proof. It is straightforward to obtain a rendition ρ∗ of ⟨H, Ω⟩ by combining a vortex-free rendition of
the P-strip society of ⟨H, Λ⟩ and δ ∩ ∆ such that cρ is divided into a set U of vortex cells in ρ∗ . Note
that, since P is exposed it follows that |U| ≥ 2. Moreover, we can define ρ∗ as a restriction of δ ∩ ∆,
i.e., so that every cell of ρ∗ that is not contained in cρ is a cell of δ ∩ ∆. For what follows, we work
with the rendition ρ∗ . Notice that in ρ∗ the drawing of the paths in P never cross one another and
this is crucial for the arguments that follow.
     Let q := s · (p + 2). Let ΛP be an ordering of the paths in P such that while traversing the rotation
ordering of Λ we first encounter one of the two endpoints for all paths in P and then all others. Let
P1 , . . . , Ps be the paths in P respecting the ordering ΛP . Moreover, for every cycle C ∈ C we define the
interior (resp. exterior ) of C as the disk bounded by the trace of C that contains (resp. avoids) BΛ .
     Now, for i ∈ [s] and j ∈ [q], let P be a V (Pj )-V (Pj )-path B that is a subpath of Ci . Let PB be
the subpath of Pi that shares its endpoints with B. Let ∆B be the disk bounded by the trace of the
cycle B ∪ PB that does not fully either the trace of C1 or BΛ . We call B a bend of Ci at Pj if ∆B does
not intersect the drawing of Ci \ B. Moreover, we call a bend B shrinking if ∆B is a subset of the
interior of Ci and expanding otherwise. We call an expanding bend B loose if ∆B does not intersect
the drawing of any cycle in C that is not Ci and tight if any expanding bend B ′ of Ci where B ′ is a
subpath of B is not loose.
     We also define the height of a bend B as the non-negative integer r + 1, where r is the maximum
value such that B intersects at least one of Pj+r or Pj−r and j + r ≤ s or j − r ≥ 1 (if j + r exceeds q
we consider Pj+r = Pq and if j − r drops below 1 we similarly assume that Pj−r = P1 ). Finally, we
call any minimal V (P1 )-V (Pq ) path contained in a cycle C ∈ C a pillar of C.
     First, notice that an expanding bend B of a cycle C ∈ C that is neither loose nor tight must contain
a subpath B ′ that is a loose expanding bend of C. Moreover, if B is any expanding bend of a cycle
C ∈ C, then the graph obtained by replacing the subpath B of C with the path PB defines a cycle C ′
and the interior of C ′ strictly contains the interior of C, hence the name expanding. In the case of a
shrinking bend the opposite happens, particularly we obtain a cycle with strictly smaller interior.
     Additionally, notice that for every C ∈ C, since P is an exposed transaction, C contains at least
one pillar and since C is a cycle, it must contain an even number of pairwise disjoint pillars.
     We proceed to prove the following statement by an induction on the number of cycles in C: If no
cycle in C contains a loose expanding bend then for every i ∈ [s],

   1. every expanding bend of Ci has height at most s − i + 1,

   2. Ci contains exactly two disjoint pillars Ri , i ∈ [2], and

   3. every shrinking bend of Ci that is a subpath of Ri , i ∈ [2], has height at most s − i + 1.

    Now, let k ∈ [1, s − 1] and C k be the subset of C that consists of its k outermost cycles. Assume
that the statement above holds for C k . We prove that it also holds for C k+1 . Assume that no cycle
in C k+1 contains a loose expanding bend. Let B be any tight expanding bend of Cs−k at P ∈ P
which is the innermost cycle contained in C k+1 and the only cycle of C k+1 not contained in C k . Notice
that any subpath B ′ of B whose endpoints are the endpoints of a maximal subpath of a path in P
that is drawn in ∆B is also an expanding bend of Cs−k where ∆B ′ ⊆ ∆B . Since B is tight, any such
subpath B ′ must also be tight. This implies that the disk ∆B ′ for any such subpath B ′ intersects the
drawing of some other cycle of C. Since B is expanding and hence ∆B is not a subset of the interior of
Cs−k , it follows that ∆B ′ intersects the drawing of cycles only in C k . In fact, it follows that any such
intersection corresponds to an expanding bend of some cycle in C k at P that is contained in ∆P . Then,


                                                    57
by assumption, if all these expanding bends are tight, it must be that the maximum height among all
of them is k. Hence, it must be that the height of B is k + 1.
    Moreover, it is straightforward to observe that Cs−k contains exactly two disjoint pillars. Indeed,
if there were at least four disjoint pillars in Cs−k then it is easy to see that there would exist an
expanding bend of Cs−k on either P1 and/or Pq whose height would be larger than k + 1, which would
imply that it is a loose expanding bend, which by assumption cannot exist.
    Finally, it is also straightforward to observe that the existence of a shrinking bend of Cs−k that is
a subpath of either of the two pillars of Cs−k of height more than k + 1, implies the existence of an
expanding bend of the same height, which as we proved cannot exist.
   We proceed with the description of an algorithm that runs in time (poly(s · p) + poly(t)) · |G| that
computes the desired s-nest C ′ along with the desired railed linkage C ′ that is orthogonal to C ′ , and
moreover computes the desired planar exposed transaction Q of order p that is orthogonal to the new
nest C ′ .

Step 1: We obtain our new set of cycles C ′ := ⟨C1′ , . . . , Cs′ ⟩, where Ci′ , i ∈ [s], is obtained from Ci by
iteratively applying whenever applicable the following two rules.

  1: As long as there exists a path R ∈ R, some i ∈ [s − 1] and a subpath R′ ⊆ R such that R′ has
     both endpoints in Ci and is otherwise disjoint from all cycles of C and moreover R′ is drawn
     entirely in the exterior of Ci , we update Ci to the unique cycle C ′ contained in Ci ∪ R′ different
     from Ci whose trace separates the interior of C1 from BΛ . We call such a subpath R′ of R as
     above, a Ci -expanding path. Now, observe that, since ρ∗ is a restriction of δ ∩ ∆, C ′ is also
     grounded in δ ∩ ∆. Moreover, the interior of C ′ properly contains the interior of Ci (before the
     update).

  2: As long as there exists a loose expanding bend B of a cycle C ∈ C we replace B by PB as defined
     above. Now, observe that, since ρ∗ is a restriction of δ ∩ ∆, the updated cycle remains grounded
     in δ ∩ ∆.

Since for both rules above, whenever we update a cycle of our nest, it is guaranteed that the interior of
the updated cycle strictly contains the interior of its predecessor, the above procedure will terminate
in at most poly(s · p) · |G| many steps and the resulting nest C ′ will satisfy the following two properties.
There is no loose expanding bend of any cycle in C ′ , which implies that C ′ satisfies the three properties
above, and there is no C-expanding subpath of any path in R for any cycle C ∈ C ′ .

Step 2: Now, update R to contain the minimal V (Λ)-V (C1′ ) subpath of each path in R.
    It follows from the previous step that any subpath R′ of any path R ∈ R such that R′ has both
endpoints on Ci′ for i ∈ [2, s − 1] and is otherwise disjoint from Ci′ must either intersect Ci+1 ′
                                                                                                     or it
cannot be drawn in the exterior of Ci . In the case that R has both endpoints on Cs it can only be
                                           ′                    ′                         ′

that R′ is not drawn in the exterior of Cs′ . From now on we call such a subpath R′ of a path R ∈ R a
retrogression of R.
    Let the paths in R = {R1 , R2 , . . . , Rt } be ordered according to the occurrence of their endpoints
on the rotation ordering of Λ. Moreover, assume there exists an i ∈ [t] and a j ∈ [2, s] such that Ri
has a retrogression R′ with both endpoints on Cj′ . Notice that there exists a subpath LR′ of Cj′ that
shares its endpoints with R′ which is internally disjoint from Ri . This is because otherwise, we could
find a subpath of Ri which witnesses that we are in the previous case. Let ∆R′ be the disk bounded
by the trace of R′ ∪ LR′ which avoids the trace of C1 . We assume that R′ is chosen maximally with
the property of being a retrogression.
    Suppose there exists some path Q, possibly Q = R, in R whose drawing intersects the interior of
∆R′ in a node or arc distinct from those of R′ . Then, this intersection of Q with ∆R′ must belong


                                                      58
to a maximal retrogression Q′ of Q and there must exist j ′ ∈ [2, s], j ′ > j, such that both endpoints
belong to Cj ′ . Hence, there must exist some maximal retrogression P ′ of some path P ′ ∈ R such that
no other part of R intersects the corresponding disk ∆′P . Thus, by replacing P ′ with LP ′ , we obtain a
new radial linkage still with the same endpoints as those of R, but with strictly less retrogressions.
This means that, after at most E(R) many such refinement steps, we must have found a radial linkage
R′ which is orthogonal to C ′ . This step can be performed in time poly(t) · |G|.

Step 3: We now define a transaction P ′ ⊆ P of order p by skipping the first and last s paths in P
and then choosing the first path from every bundle of s many consecutive paths from the remaining
paths of P. By the arguments above the height of every expanding bend of every cycle in C ′ at P1 or
Ps is at most s and therefore by definition no such bend can intersect any of the paths in P ′ . It follows
that any other bend of any cycle must be a bend that is a subpath of a pillar of the given cycle. By
definition of P ′ , this implies that any such bend of any cycle can intersect at most one path in P ′ .
This implies that the transaction P ′ which is a planar and exposed transaction in (H, Ω) is almost
orthogonal to our new set of cycles C ′ .
    We need to make one more adjustment to each of the paths in P ′ to finally make them orthogonal
to C ′ . We define the final transaction Q where each path Q ∈ Q is a path obtained from a path in
P ∈ P ′ that shares the same endpoints with P and is defined by starting from one of the two endpoints
of P and while moving towards the other endpoint of P taking any possible “shortcut”, that is by
greedily following along any bend of any pillar of a cycle in C ′ at P that appears, until we reach the
other endpoint of P. Since as we already observed any bend of any pillar of a cycle can intersect at
most one path in P ′ , it is implied that Q is a linkage. Moreover, by construction, it now follows that
Q is orthogonal to C ′ as desired and that the paths in Q are grounded in ρ∗ which also implies that
the Q-strip society of ⟨H, Λ⟩ is isolated, separating, and rural. Finally, since the shortcuts we take
are subpaths of pillars it follows that Q contains at least one edge drawn in the interior of C1′ and
therefore is exposed (with respect to δ ∩ ∆).

Rooting transactions. Recall that every thicket comes with a railed nest (C, R) where R is
orthogonal to C. Moreover, R acts as our connection of the thicket to the cylindrical wall of the walloid
of our orchard. While refining, and possibly splitting, the thicket we wish to maintain the existence of
such a radial linkage and we need to make sure that new flowers that potentially need to be extracted
from the thicket can be routed through the paths in R.
   To this end we prove the following lemma. For a visualization, see Figure 14.




Figure 14: A visualization of Lemma 6.6. The cycles in C are red, the paths in L are green, and the
paths in L′ are orange. The vertices in X ∪ Y are red and the vertices in X ′ ∪ Y ′ are blue.


Lemma 6.6. Assume that G is a graph embedded in an annulus A such that G is the union of

   • r-cycles C = {C1 , . . . , Cr } drawn in its interior such that no disk of A bounds some of the cycles
     in C,


                                                    59
   • an X-Y -linkage L = {L1 , . . . , Lr }, and a X ′ -Y ′ -linkage L′ = {L′1 , . . . , L′r },

such that the vertices of X ∪ Y are drawn in one of the two boundaries of A, the vertices of X ′ ∪ Y ′
are drawn in the other, and both L and L′ are orthogonal to C. Then G contains an orthogonal
X-Y ′ -linkage of order r.
   Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds the outcome above in time poly(r) · |G|.

Proof. Notice that for every set S ⊆ V (G) such that |S| < r, there exists at least one cycle, one path in
L, and one path in L′ fully contained in G − S. By applying Menger’s theorem, this implies that there
exists an X-Y ′ linkage R in G of order r. Moreover, in the terminology of the proof of Lemma 6.5,
since L and L′ are orthogonal to C, we can immediately deduce that there are no C-expanding paths
of any path in R for any cycle C ∈ C. Then, by applying the arguments of step two in the proof of
Lemma 6.5, we can make R orthogonal to C.

   Lemma 6.6 allows us to root any given orthogonal transaction on our orthogonal radial linkage. To
formalize this we borrow the following notion from [39].
    Let ρ be a rendition of a society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆ together with a nest C = {C1 , C2 , . . . , Cs }
around an arcwise connected set ∆∗ ⊆ ∆. We say that a linkage L is coterminal with a linkage R up
to level Ci if there exists a subset R′ ⊆ R such that L ∩ H = R′ ∩ H where H is the graph drawn in
the ∆∗ -avoiding disk of trace(Ci ).

6.2    Cultivating a transaction
With the tools developed in the previous section, we know that we can always either find a pruning
of our blooming orchard, or we find an exposed planar transaction that is isolated, separating, and
rural which because of Lemma 6.5 we can also assume to be orthogonal to the nest of our thicket. The
next step is to apply the ideas of plowing to the transaction to make sure that the area covered by it
is homogeneous with respect to its folio of detail d which would then allow us to extract additional
flowers and parterre from it.
   Let d, x, y, t, b, h, and s be positive integers and (G, δ, W ) be an (x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard.
Moreover, let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be a thicket society with nest C = {C1 , . . . , Cs } of (G, δ, W ). We say that a
transaction in ⟨H, Λ⟩ is barren if it is planar, exposed, and orthogonal to C, and the P-strip society of
⟨H, Λ⟩ is isolated, separating, and rural. Let P be a barren transaction in ⟨H, Λ⟩. A parcel of P is a
cycle C consisting of two vertex-disjoint subpaths of C1 , say X1 and X2 , and a shortest V (C1 )-V (C1 )
subpath from two distinct paths P1 ̸= P2 ∈ P, such that C1 contains an X1 -X2 -subpath of Pi for
each i ∈ [2], and C1 intersects no other member of P. Notice that all but the boundary paths of a
barren transaction belong to exactly two parcels and the boundary paths belong to exactly one parcel
each. Let d be a positive integer. The d-folio of a parcel O of P is the union of the d-folios of all
vortex-societies of all cells from the δ-influence of O. A transaction P is said to be d-cultivated if it is
barren and all of its parcels have the same d-folio.

Lemma 6.7. Let d, x, y, t, b, h, p, and s be positive integers. Let (G, δ, W ) be a d-blooming and
(x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard, let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be one of its thicket societies together with its nest C =
{C1 , C2 , . . . , Cs }. There exists a function f6.7 : N2 → N such that for every barren transaction P of
order f6.7 (p, d) in ⟨H, Λ⟩ there exists a d-cultivated transaction Q ⊆ P of order p.
                                    2
                                2O(d )
   Moreover, f6.7 (p, d) ∈ p2            .

Proof. By Observation 2.2 we know that the folio of any parcel of any barren transaction in ⟨H, Λ⟩
                             2                                                    z1
has at most f2.2 (d) ∈ 2O(d ) members. Moreover, recall that f3.1 (z1 , z2 ) = z12 . We set f6.7 (d, p) :=
f3.1 (p, f2.2 (d)) and thus meet the upper bound claimed in the assertion.


                                                          60
    Let P1 and P2 be the two outer paths of P and for each i ∈ [2] let Pi′ be the shortest V (C1 )-
V (C1 )-subpath of Pi with at least one edge not in C1 . Then there exist two vertex-disjoint subpaths
L1 and L2 of C1 such that each Li has one endpoint in common with P1′ and the other endpoint
in common with P2′ , S and every path P ∈ P contains a unique V (L1 )-V (L2 )-subpath P ′ . It follows
that J    :=  L1 ∪ L2 ∪ P ∈PP is an f6.7 (d, p)-ladder. Moreover, the bricks of J are in a one-to-one
correspondence with the parcels of P. If we now enumerate all possible f2.2 (d) members of a d-folio of
some parcel of P, assign distinct numbers to all of them and then assign to each brick of J the set of
all numbers corresponding to the members of the folio of the corresponding parcel of P we obtain an
f6.7 (d, p)-ladder where each brick is assigned a f6.7 (d, p)-palette. This allows us to call Corollary 3.2
to obtain a p-ladder J ′ ⊆ J where all bricks have the same f6.7 (d, p)-palette. Notice that each of the
p horizontal paths of J ′ is, in fact, a subpath P ′ of some path in P. Let Q be the collection of all
paths Q ∈ P such that Q′ is a horizontal path of J ′ . It follows that Q is d-cultivated and of order p as
desired.

6.3    Greenhouses
We are now able to always find large cultivated transactions. Next, we need an auxiliary tool that will
help us refine a thicket and extract additional parterre. Toward this goal we define an intermediate
structure.
     Let p ≥ t ≥ 3 be integers. A (t, p)-greenhouse segment is a (2t + 1, p)-wall W that is the union of a
(t, p)-wall W1 , called the base, whose t rows are exactly the first t rows of W, a (t + 1, p)-wall W2 whose
t rows are exactly the rows t + 1 up to 2t + 1 of W, and a (2, p) wall Q whose two rows are exactly the
rows t and t + 1 of W. We call the bricks of Q the plots of W.
     If G is a graph, δ is a Σ-decomposition of G, d is a positive integer, and W is a (t, p)-greenhouse
segment, we say that W is d-homogeneous if the d-folios of all plots of W are the same.
     We will use the greenhouse segments as a storage unit for societies we have found deep within a
segment and which will be turned into flowers of their respective parterre segments in a second step.
For this reason, greenhouses only appear as an intermediate structure within this subsection.

Lemma 6.8. Let b, d, h, p, t be positive integers where t ≥ 3. Let G be a graph δ be a Σ-decomposition
of G, W be a d-homogeneous (t + 10, p)-greenhouse segment in G, and let X be the perimeter of W.
Assume further that p ≥ d∗ · h(2t + 10bd) where d∗ is the size of the d-folio of the plots of W. Then
there exists a sequence ⟨W1 , . . . , Wd∗ ⟩ of (t, b, h)-parterre segments such that

   • for every i ∈ [d∗ ] Wi is fully contained in the graph drawn in the influence of X,

   • for every i ∈ [d∗ ] the wall W
                                  fi is a subwall of the base of W and its rows coincide with the rows
     of W,

   • for every simple society ⟨H, Λ⟩ in the d-folio of the plots there exists some i ∈ [d∗ ] such that Wi
     is a (t, b, h, H, Λ)-parterre segment.

Proof. Let O = {O1 , O2 , . . . , Od∗ } be the collection of all societies that belong to the d-folio of the
plots of W. Let us begin by partitioning W into a sequence ⟨W1′ , . . . , Wd′ ∗ ⟩ of (h(2t + 10bd), t + 10)-walls.
Fix some i ∈ [d∗ ] and now partition Wi′ further into a sequence ⟨W1i , . . . , Whi ⟩ of (2t + 10bd)-walls. It
suffices to show that each Wji can be turned into a (t, b, Oi )-flower segment since this means we can
find, for each i ∈ [d∗ ] a sequence of h disjoint such flower segments, resulting in a (t, b, h, Oi )-parterre
segment as desired.
    Notice that each Wji individually can be seen as a (t, 2t + 10bd)-greenhouse segment. Since W is
d-homogeneous, it follows that Wji is so as well since every plot of Wji is a plot of W. We may now
find a linkage P starting at the first vertex of each of the first t columns of Wji , then following these


                                                       61
Figure 15: Two illustrations for the proof of Lemma 6.8: a greenhouse subdivided into a small sequence
of pieces and one of those pieces transformed into a flower segment.


columns into the t + 2nd row, from there heading to the last t columns of Wji and following these down
to the bottom row. See Figure 15 for an illustration.
    This linkage intersects only 2t plots of Wji and will serve as the rainbow of our flower segment. To
create the petals we group the remaining bd columns into b groups, each containing 10d columns. We
may then use the same arguments as in the proof of Lemma 5.3 and Lemma 5.2 to pick first a plot
of each group which is the center of a (10, 10)-subwall of Wji that does not intersect any other group
nor P. Within the influence of this plot, we know we find a cell c whose vortex society contains Oi as
a minor. Now Lemma 5.2 allows us to link the boundary vertices of c to the columns of the group
we are currently handling. Again, see Figure 15 for an illustration. Since all groups can be handled
individually this indeed results in the desired flower segment.

6.4    Swapping a thicket and a parterre
A last issue we have to deal with before we can jump into the proof of our main tool is the following.
Suppose the thicket society of, at least, the first thicket is already of bounded depth. This means that
there is nothing more to be extracted from that thicket and we have to start treating some thicket
to the right of it. However, whenever we extract a parterre segment by first pulling out a part of
some d-homogeneous transaction in a thicket society of the ith thicket and then using Lemma 6.8, the
parterre will be “in between” thicket segments with respect to the linear ordering of the segments. As
we demand all parterre segments of an orchard to occur in order without being interrupted by other
segments, we need a way to move this parterre to the “left” of all thicket segments. This will cost us
a bit of the infrastructure of our thickets, but since we will only extract, and then move, a bounded
number of parterre segments in the end, this becomes just a matter of large enough numbers.
Lemma 6.9. Let d, x, y, t0 , t, b, h, s0 , s, and ℓ be positive integers where

                                        s ≥ s0 + ℓ · h(2t0 + bd) and
                                        t ≥ t0 + 2 · ℓ · h(2t0 + bd)


                                                     62
Now let, (G, δ, W ) be obtained from a d-blooming and (x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s)-orchard (G, δ ∗ , W ∗ ) by
replacing the ith (t, s)-thicket segment with a sequence ⟨W1 , . . . , Wℓ , Wℓ+1 , Wℓ+2 ⟩ where Wi , for each
i ∈ [ℓ] is a (t0 , b, h)-parterre segment and both Wℓ+1 and Wℓ+2 are (t0 , s0 )-thicket segments where we
allow either Wℓ+2 , or both Wℓ+1 and Wℓ+2 to be empty. Moreover, assume that every member of
d-folio(δ ′ ) is either a society of some parterre segment of δ, or a society of Wi for some i ∈ [ℓ]. Then
there exists a d-blooming (x, y)-fertile (t0 , b, h, s0 )-orchard (D, δ ′ , W ′ ) such that d-folio(δ ′ ) = d-folio(δ ∗ )
and the number of thicket segments of (D, δ ′ , W ′ ) is exactly the number of thicket segments of (G, δ, W )
minus one plus the number of non-empty members of {Wℓ+1 , Wℓ+2 }.

   The proof of Lemma 6.9 is mostly technical but not very difficult. We therefore choose to present a
rather informal description of the operation instead of a full formalization. A sketch of the core part of
the re-routing necessary is depicted in Figure 16.




Figure 16: A sketch on how to move a flower segment from the “right” of a thicket segment to its “left”
in the proof of Lemma 6.9.

Proof. Let C denote the exceptional cycle of W. For each i ∈ [ℓ], Wi is a (t0 , b, h)-parterre segment.
Hence, Wi consists of h (t0 , b)-flower segments in the sequence ⟨W1i , . . . , Whi ⟩.
    Moreover, for each i ∈ [ℓ] and j ∈ [b], each flower of Wji has at most d, indeed, at most 3, vertices in
common with C while the rainbow of Wji meets 2t0 additional vertices of C. Let us call these vertices
the terminals of Wji . To prove out statement it suffices to show that the parterre segments may be
“moved” sequentially from one side of a single thicket to the other. If this is true, then, by using the
same arguments, we can move the parterre segment to “the other side” of any number of consecutive
thicket segments since we only have to sacrifice the infrastructure locally. Moreover, if this works for a
single parterre segment, then by induction on ℓ it follows that we may repeat the procedure for each of
them.
    Indeed, to move a single parterre segment, it suffices, by the same arguments, to show that we may
“move” its flower segments individually, one after the other. To this end, all we have to prove is that a
single flower segment may be “moved”.
    We allocate the following part of the infrastructure to make our routing: Starting from C we select
2t0 + db many consecutive cycles from the concentric cycles of W. From the (i − 1)st thicket we allocate
the first 2t0 + db paths from its radial linkage together with the last 2t0 + db paths of its radial linkage.
Moreover, we reserve the outermost 2t0 + db cycles of its nest.
    Let C ′ denote the new exceptional cycle of W ′ in case W ′ is obtained from W by forgetting the
first 2t0 + db consecutive cycles starting from C.


                                                           63
    Observe that each part of the infrastructure allocated is large enough to host 2t0 + db disjoint
paths, which is at least the number of terminals from our flower segment. By following along the
vertical paths of W and the allocated infrastructure, we can now find a linkage from the terminals of
our flower segment to the intersection of the first 2t0 + db paths of the radial linkage of the (i − 1)st
thicket segment such that
   1. these paths stay within the allocated infrastructure except for their endpoints, and
   2. the endpoint of each path belongs to C ′ .
See Figure 15 for an illustration of how those paths are to be chosen.
    Notice that this operation reduces the infrastructure by at most 2t0 + db many cycles in the thicket
and W and at most 2(2t0 + db) many paths from its radial linkage. Moreover, the operation results,
after forgetting the used infrastructure and adjusting the numbers accordingly, in the flower segment
to now be on “the other side” of the (i − 1)st thicket segment.
    Since we perform this operation h times for each parterre segment and we have ℓ parterre segments
in total, the total amount of lost infrastructure is at most ℓ · h(2t0 + bd) cycles from the nest of each
involved thicket segment, at most 2 · ℓ · h(2t0 + bd) paths from the radial linkage of each involved thicket
segment, and at most ℓ · h(2t0 + bd) cycles of W. By our choice of numbers this implies that the nests
of each thicket segment, as well as the walloid W itself, each have at least t0 cycles left. Moreover,
each thicket segment has at least t0 paths in its radial linkage that remain untouched and at least s0
cycles in its nest that have not been used. Thus, our claim follows.

6.5        The extraction lemma
We are now ready to deal with our most technical tool that will facilitate most of the technicalities of
the proof of Lemma 6.2.
    Let x, y, t, b, h, and s be positive integers and (G, δ, W ) be an (x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard.
Moreover, let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be a thicket society of a (t, s)-thicket segment of (G, δ, W ) with nest C =
{C0 , . . . , Cs+1 }. Also, consider ρ to be the cylindrical rendition of ⟨H, Λ⟩ obtained from δ ∩ ∆ by
declaring the interior of the traceδ (C si )-avoiding disk of traceδ (C0 ) the unique vortex c0 of ρ, where
∆ is the traceδ (C si )-avoiding disk of traceδ (Cs+1 ). Moreover, let P be a barren transaction in ⟨G, Λ⟩.
Notice that there exists a rendition ρ′ of ⟨H, Λ⟩ in ∆ which can be obtained by combining ρ with a
vortex-free rendition of the strip society of P such that c0 is divided into two vortices, c1 and c2 . We
call these two vortices the residual vortices of ρ and P.
Lemma 6.10. Let d, x, y, t0 , b, h, ℓ, p, and s0 be positive integers where

                                       t :=ℓ · h(2t0 + 10bd) + 2t0 + 2,
                                       s :=s0 + 4t + 2t0 + 3, and
                                       p :=3t + 2s + 2.

Let (G, δ, W ) be a d-blooming and (x, y)-fertile (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard, ⟨H, Λ⟩ be one of its thicket societies
together with its nest C = {C0 , C1 , . . . , Cs , Cs+1 }, and P be a d-cultivated transaction of order p in
⟨H, Λ⟩.
    We assume that either ⟨H, Λ⟩ has a cross, or its folio contains at least one society that is not a
society of d-folio(δ). Let O be the collection of societies of detail d that belong to the folio of the parcels
of P but not to d-folio(δ), and assume |O| ≤ ℓ.
    Let δ ∗ = (Γ∗ , D∗ ) be a Σ-decomposition of G obtained by combining δ with a vortex-free rendition
of the strip society of Q and let c1 , c2 be the residual vortices of δ and Q. Then,
   1. if


                                                       64
         • O=
            ̸ ∅, or
         • O = ∅ but for each i ∈ [2] there either exists a simple society Oi ∈   / d-folio(δ) such that Oi
           belongs to the d-folio of a vortex society of ci or there exists a cross on the vortex society of
           ci ,
      there exists a d-blooming and (x, y)-fertile (t0 , b, h, s0 , Σ)-orchard (G, δ ′ , W ′ ) where δ ′ = (Γ′ , D′ ),
      Γ′ ⊆ Γ∗ , and every cell of δ ′ that is not a cell of δ ∗ is a vortex. Moreover, every vortex of δ ′ is
      either a vortex of δ or contained in one of c1 or c2 .
   2. Otherwise, there exists a pruning of (G, δ, W ).
Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time O(|G|2 ).
Proof. We break the proof into five steps as follows. During the proof we keep one bit of information
called the flag which will determine which of the two outcomes of the lemma we end up in. In case
the bit is 1, i.e. the flag is set, we find the first outcome, otherwise, we will end up with a pruning of
(G, δ, W ). In the beginning, our bit equals 0 and the flag is not set.
Step 1 This step is to establish the general setup. We divide P into five transactions (see Figure 17)
     which will be used for different parts of the construction later on.
Step 2 We then check if O = ̸ ∅. If this is true we set the flag and introduce an intermediate setup that
     will allow us to extract a large greenhouse. Otherwise, the flag remains unset and we move on.
Step 3 The transaction P defines two new “vortex regions”. For each of them, we test if it either a)
     has a cross, or b) its folio contains a society that does neither belong to the folio of δ, nor to the
     folio of the parcels of P. If for each of the two regions one of a) or b) holds and the flag is unset,
     we set the flag. Otherwise, we do not touch the flag and move on.
Step 4 Now, if the flag has been set in one of the previous steps we now extract a greenhouse, which
     will be converted in a sequence of parterre by Lemma 6.8, and two new thickets from the original
     thicket segment. This yields the first outcome of the assertion and our case distinction terminates
     here.
Step 5 This step is only reached if the flag is not set at any point. By our assumptions on ⟨H, Λ⟩
     this implies that exactly one of the two vortex regions satisfies a) or b) from Step 3 and every
     society of the folio of the parcels of P belongs to d-folio(δ). In this case, we make use of P to
     find a pruning of (G, δ, W ).
   We now dive directly into discussing the details of each step.

Step 1: Setup Let R be the radial linkage of ⟨H, Λ⟩ and C. Let I1 and I2 be the two minimal
segments of Λ such that P is a V (I1 )-V (I2 )-linkage. Moreover, by possibly shifting Λ slightly, we may
assume that every point of I1 appears before any point of I2 in Λ. Let x1 , x2 , . . . , xp be the endpoints of
the paths in P in V (I1 ) ordered according to their appearance in Λ, and y1 , y2 , . . . , yp be the endpoints
of the path in P in V (I2 ). We may number P = {P1 , P2 , . . . , Pp } such that Pi has endpoints xi and yi
for each i ∈ [p]. With this, we are ready to define the five transactions that provide the framework for
all following construction steps.

                                        S1 := {Pt+2 , . . . , Pt+s+1 }

any of these five transactions. See Figure 17 for an illustration.
    We also define two disks ∆1 and ∆2 . Let U1 be the cycle consisting of a subpath of Cs0 +2t+2t0 +2
together with a subpath of Pt+s+1 whose Cs -avoiding disk also avoids the paths in Q. Similarly, let
U2 be the cycle consisting of a subpath of Cs0 +2t+2t0 +2 together with a subpath of P2t+s+2 whose
Cs -avoiding disk also avoids the paths in Q. The, for each i ∈ [2], we define ∆i to be the Cs -avoiding
disk bounded by the trace of Ui . The two disks ∆i are our candidates for the new thickets.
    Finally, notice that ⟨H, Λ⟩ has two renditions in the disk:

   • δH := δ ∩ H where δH has a single vortex cell, say c0 and the vortex society of cH has a rendition
     in the disk with at most x vortices all of which have depth at most y.

   • a rendition ρ such that every cell of δH except c0 is also a cell of ρ and, moreover, ρ contains a
     rendition of the strip society of P and ρ has exactly two vortex cells, denoted by c1 and c2 , such
     that

         – for each i ∈ [2], ci is fully contained in ∆i , and
         – there exist non-negative integers x1 and x2 such that x1 + x2 = x and the vortex society of
           ci has a rendition in the disk with at most xi vortices all of which have depth at most y.

    At the moment c1 and c2 contain much more than what they need to contain in the end to form the
residual vortices. That is, at this stage they also contain the entire infrastructure that will eventually
form their respective nest. This will be adjusted in Step 4 and Step 5.

Step 2: Preparing the greenhouse In case O = ∅ we completely skip this step and move on to
Step 3.

which starts in V (I1 ), contains an edge which is completely drawn inside c0 , and ends on Cs0 +2t+2t0 +1 .
Then Q′ := {Q′ | Q ∈ Q}. Notice that, with respect to C ′ := {Cs0 +2t+2t0 +2 , Cs0 +2t+2t0 +3 , . . . , Cs }, Q′
is an orthogonal radial linkage. Moreover, the drawing of any path in Q′ is disjoint from ∆1 ∪ ∆2 .

Step 3: Preparing the “split” For each i ∈ [2], let ⟨Hi , Λi ⟩ be a vortex society of ci and let
Oi := d-folio(G, Λ) \ d-folio(δ). We say that ci is promising if ⟨Hi , Λi ⟩ has a cross or Oi ̸= ∅.
   If there is i ∈ [2] such that ci is not promising, we move on to the next step. Otherwise, ci is
promising for both i ∈ [2]. In this case, we set the flag (if it has not been set yet) and move on to the
next step.

   1. O =

    For every Q ∈ Q′′ as constructed in Step 2 let Q′ be the shortest V (I1 )-V (Ct+1 )-subpath of Q.

now call upon Lemma 6.6 on the (partial) nest {Ct+1 , . . . , Cs } to obtain an orthogonal radial linkage
R∗ from R′ such that R∗ is coterminal with R up to level C2t0 +2t+1 and V (R∗ ) ⊆ V (R) ∪ V (R′ ) ∪
   i∈[t+1,2t0 +2t] V (Ci ). Moreover, the endpoints of the paths in R which do not belong to V (Λ) coincide

                                            ′

illustration. For each i ∈ I let

               Ti′′ := {R ∈ R∗ | R − V (Λ) shares an endpoint with some path from Ti′ }.

    Next, we need to create new nests for each of the future thicket segments arising from ci , i ∈ I. We
will define, for each i ∈ [I], a cycle family Ci′ of s0 + 2 many concentric cycles “around” ci .
    Fix some point x1 in the interior of c1 which belongs to c0 and is disjoint from the disk defined by
the strip society of P. For each j ∈ [s0 + 2], let Cj1 be the cycle contained in Ct+1+j ∪ Pt+1+j which is
fully drawn within ∆1 and which separates x1 from the trace of Cs . Then

                                           C1′ := {Cj1 | j ∈ [s0 + 2]}.

Similarly, we fix some point x2 in the interior of c2 which belongs to c0 , is fully drawn within ∆2 , and
does not belong to the disk defined by the strip society of P. Then, for each j ∈ [s0 + 2], let Cj2 be the
cycle contained in Ct+1+j ∪ P2t+s+1+j whose trace separates x2 from the trace of Cs . Then

                                           C2′ := {Cj2 | j ∈ [s0 + 2]}.

    Next, notice that since Ti′′ is an railed linkage that is orthogonal to the (partial) nest {Ct+1 , . . . , Cs },
it must also be orthogonal to Ci′ .
    We now select, for every i ∈ I, t0 members of Ti′′ , excluding its first and last path, and collect
them into the radial linkage Ti∗ . Similarly, we set Ci∗ := {C2i , . . . , Csi0 +1 }. Finally, we select c∗i to be
the cell obtained from the disk bounded by the trace of C1i . We know that for each i ∈ [2] \ I, the
vortex society of ci has a vortex-free rendition in the disk and every member of its d-folio is also a
member of the d-folio of δ. It follows that we may augment ρ to be a rendition ρ∗ of ⟨H, Λ⟩ in the disk
where exactly {c∗i | i ∈ I} are the vortex cells of ρ∗ . Moreover, if c0 had a rendition in the disk with at
most x many vortices, each of depth at most y, then it follows from our construction that there exist
non-negative integers x1 and x2 such that x1 + x2 = x and for each i ∈ I, the vortex society of c∗i has
a rendition in the disk with at most xi many vortices, each of depth at most y.
    What remains is to describe the final step of the construction. That is, we have to describe how
to obtain the desired (x, y)-fertile (t0 , b, h, s0 , Σ)-orchard. To do this, we extend the radial linkage
R∗ through the circles of our walloid W all the way down to its simple face. The infrastructure
described above yields, a large greenhouse and for each i ∈ I a new thicket segment with a nest and


                                                        68
radial linkage of order t0 . Now, Lemma 6.8 allows us to transform the greenhouse into a sequence of
(t0 , b, h)-parterre segments such that for each member of O there exists one such parterre segment.
Together with the (up to two) new thicket segments we have now reached the situation of Lemma 6.9.
By applying Lemma 6.9 and then forgetting about some of the excess infrastructure of the walloid W
and its segments, we finally obtain a (x, y)-fertile (t0 , b, h, s0 , Σ)-orchard (G, ρ∗ , W ∗ ) which has the
desired properties by construction.

Step 5: Pruning All that is left is to discuss the case where the flag was not set.
    In the case where neither c1 not c2 is promising it follows that the vortex society of c0 does not
contain a cross, nor does its d-folio contain a society that does not belong to d-folio(δ). This, however,
would mean that the entire thicket could have been forgotten to begin with and thus we may ignore
this case.
    The only remaining option is that exactly one of c1 or c2 is promising. Without loss of generality
we may assume that c2 is not promising.
    For every path T in T1 let T ′ be a shortest V (I2 )-V (C1 )-subpath of T. We set

                                            T := {T ′ | T ∈ T1 }.

Then T is a radial linkage of order t that is orthogonal to C. We then apply Lemma 6.6 to make T
coterminal with R up to level t + 1. Let L be the resulting orthogonal radial linkage.
    Next we define our new nest. This construction is similar to the construction from Step 4. We
begin by fixing a point x1 in the interior of c1 ∩ c0 which avoids the disk induced by the strip society
of P. Now, for each i ∈ [0, s + 1], let Ci∗ be the cycle in Ci ∪ Pt+2+i whose trace in δ separates x1 from
the trace of Cs+1 in δ. Moreover, let C ∗ := {Ci∗ | i ∈ [s]}.
    Now, notice that the radial linkage L must also be orthogonal to the new nest C ∗ . Let c∗0 be a cell
obtained from the disk bounded by the trace of C0∗ which contains the point x1 . It follows that ⟨H, Λ⟩
has a rendition ρ∗ in the disk where c∗0 is its only vortex cell, and d-folio(ρ∗ ) ⊆ d-folio(δ).
    With this we may augment δ to a Σ-decomposition δ ′ of G by using ρ∗ and adjust W by replacing
the thicket with nest C and radial linkage R with a thicket segment with nest C ∗ and radial linkage
L, thereby obtaining a new walloid W ′ . It is now straightforward to verify that (G, δ ′ , W ′ ) is indeed
a pruning of (G, δ, W ) since no path in T2 (except its first one), which is an exposed transaction on
⟨H, Λ⟩, belongs to any of the thickets of (G, δ ′ , W ′ ). With this, our proof is complete.

6.6    The proof of Lemma 6.2
We introduce the following convention for the rest of this section. Let us define the d-folio number to
be

                                               d⋆ := f2.2 (d).

This is purely for convenience in the proof of Lemma 6.2 as we need to have a lot of discussion around
the maximum number of different members of d-folio(δ). By Observation 2.2 this number is upper
bounded by d⋆ .

Proof of Lemma 6.2. We prove the following slightly stronger claim by induction on z.

Inductive claim. There exists an algorithm that, given an (x, y)-fertile and d-blooming single-thicket
(t(t∗ , z), b, h, s(s∗ , t∗ , z), Σ)-orchard (G, δ, W ), outputs a (z ∗ , p∗ )-ripe and d-blooming (t∗ , b, h, s∗ , Σ)-
  19 Recall that d⋆ is not a precise value but hides some constant within its O-notation.




                                                                70
orchard in time O(|G|3 ).
   Here z represents an upper bound on the number of times Lemma 6.10 can yield the first outcome
before, in the resulting orchard (G, δ, W ), d-folio(δ) contains all possible societies of detail d and δ has
x vortices, each with a cross on its society. The two main points here are that, for an (x, y)-fertile
orchard, repeated applications of Lemma 6.10 can never produce strictly more than x thickets such
that the thicket society of each of them has a cross. Moreover, any thicket whose thicket society does
not have a cross must contain some detail-d-society in its folio that does not belong to d-folio(δ) since
otherwise we could simply discard the thicket as it does not pose an obstacle to embeddability.
   Under these principles, there are three situations under which an application of Lemma 6.10 does
not yield a pruning (which is a situation we will handle online).

   1. the outcome increases the number of thickets with a cross on their respective thicket societies,

   2. the outcome produces a parterre segment for some detail-d-society that did not belong to d-folio(δ)
      before, or

   3. the outcome increases the number of “flat” thickets whose societies contain some detail-d-society
      as a minor which does not belong to d-folio(δ).

As discussed above, the first case can only occur x times. Moreover, the second case can occur at
most d⋆ times since this is an upper bound on the total number of non-isomorphic societies of detail d.
For the third case, we will see that whenever the total number of “flat” thickets goes above a certain
threshold we are able to apply a pigeonhole argument to turn some of them into parterre segments
and thereby increase |d-folio(δ)|. For this reason, also the third case can only occur a bounded number
of times.

Base of the induction. For the base of the induction, we consider the case z = 0. This case
reflects the situation where (G, δ, W ) has x thickets which have a cross on their thicket society
and where d-folio(δ) contains all societies of detail d. In this situation, by the discussion above,
there does not exist a thicket that does not have a cross on its society. Let ⟨H, Λ⟩ be the thicket
society of some thicket of (G, δ, W ). We know that (G, δ, W ) is an (x, y)-fertile and d-blooming
(t∗ , b, h, s∗ + 6t∗ + 12, Σ)-orchard. What remains to show is that it can be turned into a (z ∗ , p∗ )-ripe
and d-blooming (t∗ , b, h, s∗ + 6t∗ + 12, Σ)-orchard.
      Let C = {C1 , . . . , Cs∗ +6t∗ +12 } be the nest of ⟨H, Λ⟩ and let ∆ be the Cs∗ +6t∗ +11 avoiding disk
defined by the trace of C6t∗ +12 . Moreover, let ⟨H ′ , Λ′ ⟩ be a society defined by ∆. Notice that ⟨H ′ , Λ′ ⟩
has a nested cylindrical rendition ρ in ∆ with a single vortex, say c0 , and nest C ′ := {C1 , . . . , C6t∗ +11 }.
Moreover, by cropping the radial linkage of ⟨H, Λ⟩ we obtain a radial linkage R′ of order t∗ for ⟨H ′ , Λ′ ⟩.
      If ⟨H ′ , Λ′ ⟩ is of depth at most

O(|G|2 ).
    Notice that every time we find a pruning as the outcome of one of the above steps, at least one of
the cycles of C ′ is “pushed” slightly closer to the vortex. That means, at least one edge of G is “pushed”
further to the outside, or fully outside, of a thicket in each of these occurrences. Since there are at
most |E(G)| many edges and only 6t∗ + 12 many cycles in C ′ , we cannot find a pruning more than
(6t∗ + 12) · |E(G)| times. As a result, and since we may assume that G excludes a graph of bounded
size as a minor which means |E(G)| ∈ O(|G|), after O(|G|3 ) many iterations of the procedure above
the only possible case left is that ⟨H ′ , Λ′ ⟩ is of depth at most

                                                                                       
                                                                                   + 2) − 1.

Since there are only x thickets, these arguments establish the base of the induction.

The inductive step. The arguments that bounded the number of times we may encounter a pruning
in the base of our induction still apply when we find a pruning in the inductive step. In essence, we
cannot encounter a pruning more than O(|G|) in total and thus, by iterating the arguments enough
times, we must always encounter an outcome that is not a pruning. To avoid repetition, we continue
to properly mark all places in the arguments were pruning might occur, but we will always assume
that in these cases we encounter one of the non-pruning outcomes. Formally, we can establish such a
situation by assuming that (G, δ, W ) is a minimal counterexample to out inductive claim with respect
to improvements via pruning. Please note that, according to Lemma 6.4, it is imperative that we
perform all possible improvements via pruning, if we are to also guarantee in the end that not only the
vortex societies within thickets have bounded depth but also their entire thicket societies.
    From here on we may assume that z ≥ 1.

Step 1: No useless thickets. First, we may assume that for every thicket segment W c whose society
does not have a cross, there exists some society of detail at most d which is a minor of the thicket
society of W
           c but does not belong to d-folio(d). We may assume this since any thicket that does not
meet this criterion can safely be forgotten.

of its thicket society. Since there are at most 2d many possible such d-folios, there must be at least

the assumption from Step 1. Moreover, z ≥ |F| must hold since we at least allow one “split” for each
possible society of detail at most d.

In both cases, the total number of times we are allowed to “split” the vortex has been reduced by at
least one. Hence, by applying the induction hypothesis for z −1 to (G, δ ′ , W ′ ) the proof is complete.


7      Harvesting
The goal of this section is to prove the two lemmata of Subsection 7.5, namely Lemma 7.9 and
Lemma 7.10. The former shows how we can control the behavior of an extension, invading a well-
insulated area of a Σ-decomposition, i.e., an area containing many concentric cycles around a vortex
cell of the decomposition. The latter shows how we can exploit the structure of such a controlled
extension in order to show that whenever an extension that invades a well-insulated area cannot be hit
with a bounded number of vertices within this area, then we can find a large packing of the invading
part of the extension along the boundary of this area.

7.1     Lines in cylindrical renditions
In this subsection, we examine more closely the structure of minimal separators between disjoint
segments of the society corresponding to a cylindrical rendition. In particular, we show that their
structure in the flat part of the cylindrical rendition defines a clear frontier in terms of what we call a
line.
    We need the following proposition on the structure of a low-depth society.

Proposition 7.1 ( [63]). Let ⟨G, Λ⟩ be a linear society where Λ = ⟨v1 , . . . , vl ⟩. If ⟨G, Λ⟩ has depth at
most w, then there is a sequence B = ⟨Bi | i ∈ [l]⟩ of subsets of G such that
     S
  1. B = V (G),

    2. for every edge xy ∈ E(G) there is some i ∈ [l] such that {x, y} ⊆ Bi ,

    3. for every i ∈ [l], yi ∈ Bi ,

    4. for every i, j, h ∈ [l], if i ≤ j ≤ h, then Xi ∩ Xh ⊆ Xj , and

    5. for every i, j ∈ [l], if i ̸= j, then |Bi ∩ Bi+1 | ≤ w.

    The proof of Proposition 7.1 builds B by successively looking for separators along prefixes of the
linear society Λ (see also [39, Theorem 12.2]). This requires a linear number of calls of a min-flow
algorithm for flow, each of size bounded by the depth w. So we can compute B in time O(w · |G|2 ).




                                                        74
Fibers of a rendition. Let ρ = (Γ, D, c) be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk
∆ around a cell c. A fiber of ρ is any fiber µ = (M, T, Λ)
                                                        b of ⟨G, Λ⟩. Whenever we work with a fiber of a
rendition ρ, we assume that they are drawn in the closed disk ∆ as indicated by the ∆-decomposition
ρ ∩ M of ⟨G, Λ⟩, where dissolve(µ) = ⟨H, Λ⟩. b

Combined depth of a cylindrical rendition. Let ρ = (Γ, D, c) be a cylindrical rendition of a
linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆ around a cell c. We define the combined depth of ρ as the maximum
between the depth of ⟨G, Λ⟩ and the depth of a vortex society of c in ρ and we denote it by cdepth(ρ).
This allows us to assume that ρ is accompanied with a sequence Bc = ⟨Bi | i ∈ [l]⟩ of subsets of Gc for
which Proposition 7.1 holds for every vortex society of c in ρ where w ≤ cdepth(ρ).

Lines. A ∆-line is a line L of ∆ in ρ connecting (and containing) two distinct points of B∆ , with
the additional property that L does not meet a flap cell of ρ or a boundary point of a vortex cell that
is not the point of a vertex, i.e.,
                       [                                [
                         {c | c ∈ Cf (ρ)} ∩ L = ∅ and        (bd(c) − e
                                                                      c) ∩ L = ∅.
                                                          c∈Cv (ρ)


The vertex set of a ∆-line L is πρ (L ∩ N (δ)) and we denote it by Vρ (L) or simply V (L) when ρ is clear
from the context. Given two distinct vertices y, y ′ ∈ V (Λ) we use Ly,y′ to denote the ∆-line of ∆ such
that Vρ (Ly,y′ ) ⊆ B∆ , starting at the point of y, finishing at the point of y ′ , and following the rotation
ordering of ∆. We also use Λy,y′ for the consecutive sub-sequence of Λ that starts with y and finishes
with y ′ .
   The following lemma gives us more information about the structure of a minimum size separator
between disjoint segments of a linear society corresponding to a cylindrical rendition in a disk ∆ in the
form of a ∆-line of minimum length. The fact that we see this separator as a line is important for the
arguments of the rest of the proofs of this section.
Lemma 7.2. Let ρ = (Γ, D, c) be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆ around
a cell c and let x and y be two vertices in Λ where x appears before y in Λ. Then there is a ∆-line L
of ∆ between x and y such that
   1. |Vρ (L)| ≤ cdepth(ρ) + 2,

   2. L ∩ bd(∆c ) contains at most two points, and

   3. there is no ∆-line L′ of ∆ between x and y such that |Vρ (L′ )| < |Vρ (L)|.
Proof. Let G′ be the graph obtained from G after removing every vertex and edge drawn in every cell
of ρ and then making the vertices in e c′ pairwise-adjacent, for every cell c′ of ρ that is not c. Also we
define Γ to be the drawing of G in ∆ obtained from Γ by drawing the newly added edges in a way so
        ′                        ′

that they do not cross, inside their corresponding cell of ρ. Clearly Γ′ is a ∆-embedding (and also a
plane embedding) of G′ . Moreover, ρ′ = (Γ′ , D) is a cylindrical rendition of the linear society ⟨G′ , Λ⟩
in ∆ around c such that cdepth(ρ′ ) ≤ cdepth(ρ).
    Let T be a maximum order transaction between Λxy and Λxy in ρ′ . By definition |T | ≤ cdepth(ρ).
Let S ⊆ V (G′ ) be a minimum Λxy -Λxy separator in G′ . By Menger’s theorem, |S| ≤ cdepth(ρ).
    Since Γ′ is a plane embedding of G′ and S is a minimum Λxy -Λxy separator, it is implied that
there exists a simple closed curve N in the plane, that intersects G′ only in vertices and that intersects
exactly the vertices in S. By definition, N must intersect the boundary of ∆ in at least two points.
Also, since ∆c is a subset of a face of Γ, we can assume that N intersects the boundary of ∆c in at
most two points. Let L be the shortest line between two points of bd(∆) that is a subset of N and
that is drawn in ∆. Since N separates Λxy from its complement it must be that L intersects bd(∆)


                                                     75
in points drawn between the point of x and the point of its previous vertex on Λ and the point of y
and the point of its next vertex on Λ. Hence, we can extend L so that its endpoints are the points
corresponding to x and y respectively without intersecting any other vertices or edges of G′ , by possibly
increasing the length of L by at most two vertices.
    Now, observe that by definition L is a ∆-line in ρ′ that satisfies properties 1, 2, and 3 in ρ′ . We
argue that L also satisfies properties 1, 2, and 3 in ρ. This is indeed the case, since by definition of ρ′
any line of ∆ in ρ must also be a line of ∆ in ρ′ with the same vertex set and vice versa.

7.2    Pseudo-disks in cylindrical renditions
In this subsection, we introduce the concept of a pseudo-disk of a cylindrical rendition to facilitate
the procedure of locating a fiber of a linear society, within the area defined by a segment of the linear
society corresponding to the rendition and the line that corresponds to the separator of this segment,
as demonstrated in the previous subsection.

Pseudo-disks. Let ρ be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆. A pseudo-disk
of ρ is a set Ψ ⊆ ∆ such that

   1. Ψ is the union of the ∆-line Ly,y′ , a ∆-line Ltop between y and y ′ for some y, y ′ ∈ Vρ (Λ) where
      y appears before y ′ in Λ, and the arc-wise connected components of ∆ − (Ly,y′ ∪ Ltop ) whose
      points in ∆ can reach Lyy′ without intersecting Ltop ,

   2. ΛΨ := Λy,y′ − Vρ (Ltop ) is non-empty,

   3. Ltop ∩ bd(∆c ) contains at most two points, and

   4. there is no ∆-line L between y and y ′ such that |Vρ (L)| < |Vρ (Ltop )|.

   We define the boundary of Ψ as bd(Ψ) := Lyy′ ∪ Ltop . We call the ∆-line Ltop the top-line of Ψ
and we call y the starting and y ′ the finishing vertex of Ψ. We also define last(Ψ) := y ′ . Let GΨ be
the subgraph of G whose vertices and edges are drawn in Ψ minus Ltop . We call ⟨GΨ , ΛΨ ⟩ the society
of Ψ, we call the set V (ΛΨ ) the base boundary of Ψ, we call the set Vρ (Ltop ) the line boundary of Ψ.
Assume that Ltop ∩ bd(c) consists of two distinct points v and v ′ where y, v, v ′ , y ′ appear in this order
in Ltop and let Λc = Λ∆c ,v . Then the vortex boundary of Ψ is the set

                                  (Bv ∩ BnextΛc (v) ) ∪ (Bv′ ∩ BpreviousΛc (v′ ) )

where BnextΛc (v) and BpreviousΛc (v′ ) are members of B c and that has size at most 2 · cdepth(ρ). If
|Ltop ∩ bd(c)| ≤ 1, then the vortex boundary of Ψ is empty. The top boundary of Ψ is the union of its
line boundary and its vortex boundary and it is denoted by Vρ (Ψ).
    An ⟨H, Λ⟩-fiber
            b       of ρ inside Ψ is an ⟨H, Λ⟩-fiber
                                            b        of ρ that is also a fiber of ⟨GΨ , ΛΨ ⟩. An ⟨H, Λ⟩-fiber
                                                                                                     b
of ρ outside Ψ is an ⟨H, Λ⟩-fiber
                          b              b of ρ where none of the points of V (M ) are in Ψ.
                                  (M, T, Λ)

Chains. Let ρ be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩. A sequence Ψ = ⟨Ψ1 , . . . Ψr ⟩ of
pseudo-disks of ρ is called a chain of ρ if

   • Ψ1 , . . . , Ψr are pairwise internally-disjoint,

   • the starting vertex of Ψ1 is the starting vertex of Λ, and

   • for every i ∈ [r − 1], if yi′ is the finishing vertex of Ψi , yi′ is the starting vertex of Ψi+1 .




                                                         76
    The starting vertex of Ψ is the starting vertex of Ψ1 , i.e., the starting vertex of Λ. The finishing
vertex of Ψ is the finishing vertex of Ψr if Ψ ̸= ⟨⟩, and the last vertex of Λ otherwise. An ⟨H, Λ⟩-fiber
                                                                                                      b
of ρ outside Ψ is an ⟨H, Λ⟩-fiber
                          b       of ρ that is outside Ψi , for every i ∈ [r], if Ψ ̸= ⟨⟩, and an ⟨H, Λ⟩-fiber
                                                                                                      b
of ρ where V (M ) does not contain the starting and finishing vertex of Ψ otherwise.
   The following lemma permits us to associate pairs of boundary vertices of ⟨G, Λ⟩ to pseudo-disks of
some suitably chosen cylindrical rendition of it.

Lemma 7.3. Let ρ = (Γ, D, c) be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆ around
a cell c, Ψ be a chain of ρ with starting vertex x and finishing vertex y, and z be a vertex of Λ that
is not in the consecutive sub-sequence of Λ with first vertex nextΛ (x) and last vertex y. Then ρ has a
pseudo-disk Ψ, starting from the finishing vertex of Ψ and finishing at z, that is internally-disjoint
from every Ψ′ ∈ Ψ, and where |Vρ (Ψ)| ≤ 3 · cdepth(ρ) + 2.

Proof. First, we apply Lemma 7.2 to obtain a ∆-line L between y and z such that |Vρ (L)| ≤ cdepth(ρ) +
2, L ∩ bd(∆c ) contains at most two points, and such that there is no ∆-line L′ between y and z such
that |Vρ (L′ )| < |Vρ (L)|.
     Next, we argue that L does not contain any internal point of any of the pseudo-disks of Ψ. Assume
towards contradiction that it is not the case. Let Ψ′ ∈ Ψ such that L contains an internal point of Ψ.
Let Ltop be the top-line of Ψ′ . This particularly implies that L and Ltop intersect in at least in two
points. Let L′ be a subline of L whose endpoints are points of Ltop . Let Ltop  2   be the corresponding
subline of Ltop . Consider the ∆-line L′′ obtained by replacing the subline Ltop  2   of Ltop by L′ . Now,
observe that by the minimality of L guaranteed by the specifications of Lemma 7.2, it must be that
|L′′ | is strictly smaller the line boundary of Ψ′ which contradicts the definition of Ψ′ .
     Then, we define the desired pseudo-disk Ψ as the union of L ∪ Lyz and the arc-wise connected
components of ∆ − (L ∪ Lyz ) whose points in ∆ can reach Lyz without intersecting L.

7.3    Critical pseudo-disks and fibers
In this subsection we define the notion of a critical pseudo-disk which will be used to find fibers of a
given linear society within pseudo-disks, in a way that will allow us to either find many disjoint, in a
sequential fashion along the boundary of the linear society corresponding to our cylindrical rendition or
hit all of them by removing a small number of vertices which will be the frontiers of the corresponding
critical pseudo-disks in which we locate our fibers.

Critical pseudo-disks. Let ρ = (Γ, D, c) be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a
disk ∆ around a cell c and let ⟨H, Λ⟩

   5. |Vρ (Ψ)|, |Vρ (Ψ)|

⟨GΨ , ΛΨ ⟩. Let x be the finishing and y be the starting vertex of Ψ respectively. Also, let Λxy be the

of Λxy is not in Ψ. Notice that since Ψ is internally disjoint from every Ψ′′ ∈ Ψ, it must be that
                  S
every vertex of the top-line of any Ψ′′ ∈ Ψ, that is also a vertex of ΛΨ , must also be a vertex of the
top-line of Ψ. This implies that Λxy = ΛΨ .
   Now, for every vertex z of Λxy distinct from y, let Ψxz be the pseudo-disk of ρ given by Lemma 7.3.

    In order to compute Ψ, we start by computing a decomposition as the one in Proposition 7.1, which
as we already briefly discussed, can be done in time O(w · |G|2 ). This gives us all the top boundaries
that correspond to the pseudo-disks we define by calling upon Lemma 7.3. To finally compute the
critical pseudo-disk Ψ we iteratively apply at most O(|G|) times the minor-checking algorithm of [37]
that runs in time O|H| (|G|2 ).

    Critical pseudo-disks will allow us to find “minimal packings” of fibers along the boundary of linear
societies. The “criticality” in their definition is important in order to show an Erdős-Pósa duality for
fibers within bounded-depth societies. This is the subject of the following subsection.

7.4     Harvesting fibers
In this subsection, we show how to combine all the partial results in the previous subsection in order
to either find many sequential fibers of a given linear society or hit all of them with a small number of
vertices found within the disk of the given cylindrical rendition.

Corridors and ∆-compact sets. Let ρ = (Γ, D, c) be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society
⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆ around a cell c. Let Ψ be a pseudo-disk of ρ with starting vertex y and finishing
vertex y ′ whose top line is Ltop and assume that Ψ is a closed disk of ∆. Then we define Ψ    b as the set
containing the interior of the set Ψ \ c and all internal points of Ly,y′ . We call any subset of ∆ that is
a set Ψ
      b as above a corridor of ρ and its frontier is the set of all vertices of G whose points belong in
bd(Ψ) but not in the interior of Ly,y′ ∪ ∆c . The union K of a finite set of pairwise internally disjoint
corridors of ∆ is called a corridor system of ρ and the union of the frontiers of the corridors of this set
is the frontier of K.
   We say that a vertex set S ⊆ V (G) is ∆-compact if there is a corridor system K of ρ such that

   • none of the points of S belongs in K and

   • the frontier of K is a subset of S.

Moreover, we assume any ∆-compact set S to be accompanied by such a corridor system of ρ.

Observation 7.5. Let ρ = (Γ, D, c) be a cylindrical rendition of a linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆
around a cell c. If S and S ′ are two ∆-compact sets of G then S ∪ S ′ is also a ∆-compact set of G.




                                                                79
    The notion of a corridor and its frontier is important in our proofs as when the outcome of our
Erdős-Pósa duality is a “cover” S, then we require that this cover does not contain vertices drawn on
the interior of the corridor as well as that all vertices of the boundary of the corridor belong in S.
That way, the covers that we consider give us “valuable space” for rerouting the fibers that are not
intersected by S in a way that we can bound the number of vertices on the boundary.
   The algorithm that extracts the Erdős-Pósa duality is heavily based on the notion of a critical
pseudo-disk in bounded depth societies and is the following.

   Notice that whenever the output of the previous algorithm is a “cover” set S, it is important that
