Lemma 7.6 (Harvesting lemma). There exists a function f7.6 : N3 → N and an algorithm that, given

    • a cylindrical rendition ρ = (Γ, D, c) of a linear society ⟨G, Λ⟩ in a disk ∆ around a cell c, where
      w := cdepth(ρ), or

    • a linear society ⟨H, Λ⟩,
                           b and

    • a k ∈ N≥1 ,

either outputs

    • an ⟨H, Λ′ ⟩(k) -fiber of ⟨G, Λ⟩, for a shift Λ′ of Λ,
                                                         b or

    • a ∆-compact set S ⊆ V (G), where |S| ≤ f7.6 (k, w, |H|) and ⟨G, Λ⟩ − S has no ⟨H, Λ′ ⟩-fiber, for
      any shift Λ′ of Λ,
                      b



                                                             80
in time
                                                 O|H| (kw · |G|3 ).
Moreover
                                          f7.6 (k, w, |H|) = O|H| (kw).

Proof. We define f7.6 (k, w, |H|) := k · (3w + 2) · |H|2 .
    Let Λ1 , . . . , Λq be the shifts of Λ,
                                         b where q = |Λ|  b ≤ |H|. For every i ∈ [q], we execute the algorithm
find_all(∆, G, Λ, ρ, k, ⟨H, Λi ⟩). If for some i ∈ [q] the output is an ⟨H, Λi ⟩(k) -fiber of ρ we can
conclude. Hence, we can assume that for every i ∈ [q], the output of the corresponding execution is a
set Si = frontierρ (Ψi ) ∪ Vρ (Ψi ), where Ψi is a chain of ρ and Ψi is a pseudo-disk of ρ starting from the
finishing vertex of Ψi , finishing at the starting vertex of Ψi , and that is internally-disjoint from every
pseudo-disk of Ψi . First, observe that  S by the definition of ∆-compact sets, for every i ∈ [q], the set
Si is ∆-compact. We define S := i∈[q] Si . Then, Observation 7.5 implies that S is ∆-compact, as
required.
    Also, note that for fixed i ∈ [q], Ψi can contain at most k · |H| critical pseudo-disks of ρ, since
|dec(H, Λi )| ≤ |H|, and for each such critical pseudo-disk by definition we have that its frontier has
size at most 3w + 2. Hence in total |Si | ≤ k · (3w + 2) · |H| and thus |S| ≤ k · (3w + 2) · |H|2 as required.
    It remains to show that ⟨G, Λ⟩−S has no ⟨H, Λi ⟩-fiber, for any i ∈ [q]. Assume towards contradiction
that ⟨G, Λ⟩ − S has an ⟨H, Λi ⟩-fiber, for some i ∈ [q], and that dec(H, Λi ) := ⟨⟨H1 , Λi1 ⟩, . . . , ⟨Hq , Λidi ⟩⟩.
Then, it must be that ⟨G, Λ⟩ − S has a ⟨H, Λij ⟩-fiber, for every j ∈ [di ], such that all vertices of Λij
appear before the vertices of Λij ′ in Λ, where j < j ′ ∈ [di ] and moreover such that these fibers are
pairwise-disjoint.
    First, observe that since the algorithm outputted the set frontierρ (Ψi ) ∪ Vρ (Ψi ) it is implied that
there is an l ∈ [di ] such that if there is a ⟨H, Λil ⟩-fiber outside Ψi , this fiber is not a fiber of ⟨GΨi , ΛΨi ⟩,
implying that any such fiber is intersected by Vρ (Ψi ). So, our only hope to find a fiber for each ⟨H, Λij ⟩,
j ∈ [l], in the correct order, is within Ψi . Now, observe that by the way the chain Ψi of ρ is defined,
for any sequence Ψ1 , . . . , Ψl of pseudo-disks of Ψi , respecting the order in Ψi , such that for every
j ∈ [l], there is an ⟨H, Λij ⟩-fiber of ρ inside Ψj , there must be an index j ′ ∈ [l] such that Ψj ′ is a
⟨H, Λij ′ ⟩-critical pseudo-disk of ρ, which implies that the set frontierρ (Ψi ) intersects this fiber. This
contradicts the existence of the sequence of fibers we require in their correct order within Ψi , and this
concludes the proof.
    The algorithm above assumes that we have at hand all separators from a decomposition as the
one in Proposition 7.1. As we already briefly explained, this can be done in time O(w · |G|2 ) and it
gives us frontiers of the corresponding pseudo-disks we compute by calling upon Lemma 7.4. The total
running time is dominated by the time required to find a ⟨Hi , Λ        b i ⟩-critical pseudo-disk Ψ′ of ρ in the
while-loop of algorithm find_all. This is done, using Lemma 7.4, in time O|H| (w · |G|3 ) time and is
applied O(k) many times.

7.5     Main harvesting lemmata
In this subsection, we conclude with the proofs of the main lemmata of the entire section. An important
notion for our proofs is the one of an extension of some graph Z in a graph G.

Extensions. Given a graph M and a set T ⊆ V (M ) we call (M, T ) a subdivision pair if every vertex
not in T has degree two in M. Given a subdivision pair (M, T ) we define dissolve(M, T ) to be the
graph obtained from M after dissolving all vertices in V (M ) \ T. We define the detail of a subdivision
pair (M, T ) to be the number of edges of dissolve(M, T ). Consider two graphs Z and G. We say that a
subdivision pair (M, T ) is an extension of Z in G if

   1. M is a connected subgraph of G,


                                                        81
   2. every vertex of V (M ) \ T has degree two, and

   3. dissolve(M, T ) contains Z as a minor.
   It is important to take into account that the graph M of an extension is always connected. Also
the vertices of V (M ) \ T can be seen as subdivision vertices of paths joining the “terminals” in T.
By considering a minimal subgraph M satisfying the above properties, we can obtain the following
observation.
Observation 7.7. Let Z and G be two graphs. If Z is a minor of G, then there exists an extension
of Z in G that has detail O(|Z|2 ).
   We define the function h : N → N so that for every graph Z that is a minor of a graph G, h(|Z|) is
the bound on the detail of the minimal extension given by Observation 7.7.
   An extension (M, T ) of a graph Z in a graph G is δ-bound if M is δ-bound. For the purposes of
our local structure theorem we are interested in “covering” the δ-bound extensions of some obstruction
Z ∈ obs(H).

Disk collection of a Σ-decomposition. Let δ be a Σ-decomposition of a graph G. A disk collection
of δ is a set ∆ of |Cv (δ)| pairwise-disjoint δ-aligned disks of Σ where every disk ∆ of ∆ contains a
vortex c∆ of δ.
    We assume that a disk collection ∆ is accompanied with a starting vertex assignment function
y∆ : ∆ → V (G) mapping ∆ ∈ ∆ to a vertex y∆ (∆). For brevity, we use the term svaf instead of the
longer one “starting vertex assignment function”. We also define Λ(∆) := Λ∆,y∆ (∆) , i.e., y∆ (∆) “fixes”
the starting vertex of a linear ordering of the vertices on the boundary of ∆.
    Hence, for every ∆ ∈ ∆, ρ∆ := δ ∩ ∆ is a cylindrical rendition of the society ⟨G ∩ ∆, Λ(∆) ⟩. around
a vortex cell c∆ . Moreover, we say that a disk ∆ ∈ ∆ is q-insulated if ρ∆ is a q-nested cylindrical
rendition around the vortex c∆ .
    We call the triple (δ, ∆, y∆ ) a Σ-disk-collection triple of G and moreover q-insulated if every disk
∆ ∈ ∆ is q-insulated.
    We
    S say that (M, T ) is ∆-respecting if at least one of the points of V (M ) is drawn in the closure of
Σ \ ∆.

Covers from harvesting. Let k, d ∈ N, and let (δ, ∆, y∆ ) be a Σ-disk-collection triple of a graph
G. Consider a disk ∆ ∈ ∆ and for every linear society ⟨H, Λ⟩
                                                           b of detail at most d, apply Lemma 7.6
to ρ∆ , ⟨H, Λ⟩,
            b and k. Let S ∆ be the union of all sets that correspond to the second outcome of all
previous applications of Lemma 7.6. We define a (d, k)-cover of (δ, ∆, y∆ ) as the set ∆∈∆ S ∆ .
                                                                                      S
    Notice that, using Lemma 7.6, we get the following observation on the time it takes to compute a
(d, k)-cover of (δ, ∆, y∆ ).
Observation 7.8. There is an algorithm that, given k, d ∈ N≥1 and a Σ-disk-collection triple
(δ, ∆, y∆ ) of a graph G, outputs a (d, k)-cover S of (δ, ∆, y∆ ) in time Od (w · k · |∆| · |G|3 ), where
w := max{cdepth(ρ∆ ) | ∆ ∈ ∆}. Moreover |S| ≤ Od (w · k · |∆|).

Crops. Let (δ, ∆, y∆ ) be a disk-collection triple of a graph G, (M, T ) be a subdivision pair where
M is a subgraph of G, and consider a disk ∆ ∈ ∆. Let M ′ be the union of all paths in M ∩ ∆
connecting a vertex of B∆ with a vertex whose point is in ∆c∆ and whose internal vertices are not
in B∆ . We say that (M, T ) has a crop in ∆ if M ′ is a non-empty graph and, if this is the case, we
define crop(M, T, ∆) as the fiber (M ′ , T ∩ V (M ′ ), Λ)
                                                       b of ρ∆ where Λb ⊆ Λ(∆) . For an example of the
crop operation, see Figure 20.
    Intuitively the operation of cropping filters out parts of the subdivision pair that invade the disks
of our disk-collection, that do not reach all the way to the vortex cells of the renditions associated to


                                                   82
                                                        c


                                          y1




Figure 20: A disk ∆ containing some vortex c in is interior and a fiber (M, T, Λ) of ⟨G ∩ ∆, Λ⟩. We
depict by red the vertices not in T. If M ′ is the subgraph of M obtained after removing the black edges
and the resulting set S of isolated vertices, then (M ′ , T − S, Λ − S) = crop(M, T, Λ).
                                                                                     b The subgraphs
formed by the black edges are excluded because of the blue-dotted ∆-lines.


the disk-collection. The “forgotten” parts have the nice property that they use only flat parts of δ.
Exploiting this fact, we shall later see that all forgotten parts can be reconstructed as all such flat
parts are sufficiently represented in the parterre segments of our walloid. Hence, in the final proofs of
this section, we only deal with the essential part of the extension of Z which is what remains after
cropping. Our goal is to sufficiently represent all essential parts in the form of flowers extracted from
the thickets of our walloid.
    Notice that the parts of an extension that survive the crop operation and are not covered by a
(d, k)-cover S must invade the vortices only through the corridors corresponding to S. The space
provided by the corridors allows for the application of the rerouting of Lemma 3.4 (based on the vital
linkage theorem), which implies that if the graph M of an extension invades the vortex of a disk ∆,
then an equivalent extension will invade it in a way that only a bounded number of vertices of M
intersect the boundary of ∆. Therefore we may assume that the restriction of M in ∆ is “represented”
by an alternative fiber of bounded detail. This is proved in Lemma 7.9. With such a “representation”
lemma at hand we can later argue, in Lemma 7.10, that if S does not hit such a fiber, then a big
enough packing of this fiber exists in G − S.

Lemma 7.9 (Representation lemma). There exist functions f7.9

    To begin, we assume that the detail of (M, T ) is at most d := h(|Z|) and that no two vertices in T
are adjacent in M. Indeed, the former property is implied by Observation 7.7 and can be achieved
by removing vertices of M as long as the conditions that M is connected, dissolve(M, T ) contains Z
as a minor, (M, T ) is δ-bound, and ∆-respecting are satisfied. For the latter, if two vertices in T are
adjacent in M, we simply subdivide this edge once, without affecting any of the desired properties.
    The fact that (M, T ) is ∆-respecting implies that M is not entirely drawn in the interior of some
∆ ∈ ∆ without intersecting its boundary B∆ .
    Also, notice that by definition of an extension, the graph M − T is a graph whose connected
components are paths. Let L be a linkage in G′ that is the set of connected components of M − T.
Observe that since the detail of (M, T ) is at most d, we also have that the order of L, r := |L|, is at
most d.

    We are now in the position to prove the main lemma of this section which can be seen as a form of
a local Erdős-Pósa duality inside a collection of bounded depth disks. It essentially implies that for
every graph on h vertices, an (α, k)-cover of a Σ-disk-collection triple provides such a duality for all
their extensions. The key condition is that α is bounded by a function in h, depending on the unique
linkage function ℓ.

δ-bound, implies that there is a path P in G that is internally disjoint from M whose endpoints are

8      Plucking flowers from thickets
The main outcome of Section 7, namely Lemma 7.10, states a type of local Erdős-Pósa duality confined
inside a collection of bounded depth disks of our Σ-decomposition. In this section, we advance towards
our goal by first applying that lemma to the Σ-disk-collection (δ, ∆, y∆ ) that naturally corresponds
to the thickets of our refined orchard, for a given graph Z. If none of the (f7.9 3
                                                                                     (|Z|), k)-covers, that
correspond to the disks of (δ, ∆, y∆ ), obtained from the application of Lemma 7.10, intersect the graph
M of an extension of Z, then we can find a sequence of many copies of the crop of ∆ ∩ M inside ∆, for
every ∆ ∈ ∆. We, then proceed with a series of successive transformations of these sequences that, in
the end, will allow us to see every thicket as a flower that will be used in order to certify the existence
of a guest walloid, as required for the proof of our local structure theorem in Section 9.

8.1     Certifying flatness
As we already mentioned, we use Lemma 7.10 in order to extract a “packing” of fibers rooted on a
collection of disks around the vortices of our Σ-decomposition δ. This collection will later permit us to
see the thickets of our orchard as the flowers of a guest walloid. Recall that in an orchard all flaps of δ
are already represented by its flower segments, so the goal of applying Lemma 7.10 for the purposes of
this section, is in order to guarantee that we can also sufficiently represent the parts of an extension
that invade the vortices of our Σ-decomposition. To make the interaction between the vortex-invading
part of an extension of some graph Z with its flat part more precise, we first give a way to formally
certify flatness.

Flatness certifying complements. Let Z be a graph and μ = ⟨µ1 , . . . , µz ⟩ be a sequence of fibers
where µi = (M i , T i , Λi ), i ∈ [z].
   A Σ-flat Z-certifying complement of μ is a tuple C = (Σc , M c , T c , δ c , Y ) where

  1. Σc is the closure of Σ \ ∆ where ∆ = {∆1 , . . . , ∆z } is a collection of pairwise-disjoint disks of
                                   S
     Σ, all oriented counter-clockwise,

    2. (M c , T c ) isSa pair, where M c is a graph, T c ⊆ V (M c ) and a vertex x ∈ V (M c ) \ T c has degree
       two if x ̸∈ i∈[z] V (Λi ), otherwise it has degree zero or one in M c .

    3. δ c is a vortex-free Σc -decomposition of M c ,

    4. for every i ∈ [z], V (M i ) ∩ V (M c ) = V (Λi ) and the points, relatively to δ c , of all vertices of V (Λi )
       are on the same boundary of Σc ,

    5. for every i ∈ [z] and every y ∈ V (Λi ) \ T i it holds that degM i (y) + degM c (y) = 2,

    6. Y = {y 1 , . . . , y z }, where for i ∈ [z], the ordering of the vertices of V (M i ) whose points are on the
       boundary of Σc , when starting from y i and following the clockwise ordering of the boundary of
       Σc where they belong, is the same as the ordering of Λi (following the counterclockwise ordering
       of bd(∆i )), and

    7. (M c ∪ i∈[z] M i , T c ∪ i∈[z] T i ) is an extension of Z. We call this extension the (C, μ)-certifying
             S                      S
       extension of Z.


                                                         86
    The detail of the pair (C, μ) is the number of terminals of the extension of Z in condition 7 above.
Notice that a bound on the detail of C implies also a bound on the total number of terminals of the
fibers of μ. We also define
                                         d-folio(C) = d-folio(δ c ).
    We say that a society ⟨H, Λ⟩ is prime if dec(H, Λ) = ⟨⟨H, Λ⟩⟩. Also, we say that a fiber µ = (M, T, Λ)
is prime if dissolve(µ) is prime. Given two disjoint fibers µ1 = (M1 , T1 , Λ1 ) and µ2 = (M2 , T2 , Λ2 ),
we say that they are disjoint if V (M1 ) ∩ V (M2 ) = ∅. Given that µ1 and µ2 are disjoint, we define
µ1 ⊕ µ2 = (M1 ∪ M2 , T1 ∪ T2 , Λ1 ⊕ Λ2 ).

8.2    Flatness certifying complements of thickets
Our next step is to apply Lemma 8.1 in the thickets of a (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard. In this subsection we
consider such a thicket and we explain how the packages of the fibers in the output of Lemma 8.1 can
be seen as fibers rooted on the boundary of a thicket society of our given orchard.

Thickets and their renditions. Let (G, δ, W ) be a (t, b, h, s, Σ)-orchard and let c ∈ Cf (δ) be the

8.3    A variant of the combing lemma
We need the following variant of the Combing lemma in [26]. The statement is slightly different
(and simpler) than the one of [26, Theorem 5] and is suitable for the needs of the final result of this
subsection. The proof uses the same ideas and the bulk of them have already been used for the results
of Subsection 3.2. This lemma will be useful for the proof of the main result of this section, Lemma 8.4,
that is proven in Subsection 8.4.

Figure 22: A visualization of the proof of Lemma 8.3. The rivers of LG are blue while the 0-mountains
and the (d + 1)-valleys are grey. The cycles in C are red and the substitution paths Pih,out , Pih,in , and

    Observe that all components of G ∩ A are paths that, in turn, are all disjoint from L. Notice also
                                                                                                   S
that for every cell c of δ where c ⊆ A, it holds that σδ (c) is either a path between two of the vertices in
    c) or three paths from a vertex of σδ (c) − πδ (e
πδ (e                                                    c) to the three vertices of πδ (e
                                                                                         c). This permits us to see
the part of G thatSis drawn inside A as a planar embedding. Next observe that (LG , B) is an LB-pair
of G, where B = C. Among all linkages that are equivalent to LG we assume that LG minimizes the
quantity cae(LG , B) := |E(L) \ E(B)|. By Proposition 3.3 we know that tw(LG ∪ B) ≤ ℓ(h) = a.
    Similarly to what we did in Subsection 3.2, we may classify the connected components of G ∩ A, to
rivers, mountains, and valleys: A river is a path whose endpoints are drawn in different boundaries of
A. For every i ∈ [0, d] an i-mountain is a connected component of G ∩ Ai,d+1 and for every i ∈ [1, d + 1]
an i-valley is a connected component of G ∩ A0,i . Also the height (resp. depth) of an i-mountain
(i-valey) P is the maximum j such that Ci+j−1 (resp. Ci−j+1 ) intersects P. As we already argued
in Subsection 3.2, because of [26, Lemma 4], there are no more than tw(LG ∪ B) ≤ a rivers. we
set b := 32 a + 1. Moreover, because of [26, Lemma 7], the height (resp. depth) of an l-valey (resp.
l-mountain) is never more than 32 tw(LG ∪ B) < b. With these bounds at hand, we may reroute the
≤ a rivers by reproducing a simpler version of the proof of [26, Theorem 5]. Let R1 , . . . , Rq be the
rivers, for q ≤ a. For i = 1, . . . , q, let xiout be the last vertex of Cib that Ri meets when traversed from
its endpoint in B∆out to its endpoint in B∆in . Symmetrically, for i = 1, . . . , q, let xiin is the last vertex
of Cd−ib−1 that Ri meets when traversed from its endpoint in B∆in to its endpoint in B∆out . We then
discard from each Pi the subpath with endpoints xiout and xiin and replace each of the removed paths
by the union of three paths: two “horizontal” paths Pih,out , Pih,in and one “vertical” Piv , as indicated
in Figure 22. The fact that, for i ∈ [q] the horizontal path Pih,out , does not intersect the surviving
portions of the paths P1 , . . . , Pi−1 follows by the fact that all (i − 1)b-mountains have height < b and
the symmetric argument holds for the horizontal path Pih,in . It is now easy to see that the above
substitution creates a linkage that is equivalent to LG does not change outside A, and moreover, it
traverses the annulus Az+1,z+r by exclusively using the paths in Lz+1,z+r , as required.

8.4       Plucking a flower
We have now gathered all the tools that we need for the proof of the final result of this subsection.
The statement is similar to the one of Lemma 8.2 however, we now need to “root” the fibers in
{µ∆ | ∆ ∈ ∆′ } not only to vertices of the boundaries are in B∆ (this is already done in Lemma 8.2)
but to the particular vertices of this boundary that are crossed by the linkage R∆ of the thicket. i..e,
the linear ordering V (Λ
                       b ∆ ). This rerouting is important as it permits us to eventually see each thicket


                                                        92
as a flower.

as they are not used neither in its conclusion nor in its proof. However, now we need to give a lower
bound, not only for s, but also to the order of the thicket, that is t = |R|∆ , in order to obtain our
rerouting.

    All the transformations of this proof can be implemented in linear time. Therefore the running
time of the algorithm is dominated by one of the applications of the algorithm of Lemma 8.2 plus O(t)
and that runs in Oh (bk · (s+p) · z · |G|3 )-time. As b  k = Oh (k), we obtain the claimed running time.

we make of Lemma 8.4 in the next section, this additional feature is not necessary anymore. However,
we could also further discard all fibers extracted by Lemma 8.4 that do not invade their corresponding
vortices and update C by enhancing it with the “flat” drawings of the discarded fibers as we did in
Lemma 8.1. For instance, this extra modification would discard the rightmost package in Figure 26
that contains fibers that have flat embeddings away from the vortex (depicted by the red territory). In
the visualization of the flower of Figure 27 we assume that this rightmost package has been cropped.


9       The local structure theorem
This section is the first key step in unifying most of the previously introduced machinery into a single
statement: the local structure theorem. This theorem takes as input simply a large wall W and integers
k and h and outputs either a large clique-minor which is grasped by W, or produces the entire orchard
infrastructure necessary for the application of Lemma 8.4 together with a small set S and a list of
walloids representing all graphs of size at most h which are not fully controlled by S.

9.1      Finding the universal obstruction
To make the outcome of Lemma 8.4 more accessible, we start with an intermediate statement that
directly extracts our obstructions from the orchard and its thickets whenever the set S misses a graph
Z on at most h vertices.

                                                         100
9.2    Bringing everything together
With Lemma 9.1 at hand, we are finally ready to prove the main result of this section and the most
central statement of this paper.
Theorem 9.2 (Local structure theorem). There exist functions f9.2  1    2
                                                                     , f9.2 : N2 → N and an algorithm
                                                    1
that, given two integers k and h, a graph G and an f9.2 (h, k)-wall W in G finds, in time Oh,k (|G|3 )
either
  1. a K3k2 +k·h minor grasped by W,
  2. there are non-negative integers h′ and c′ ≤ 2 such that every graph on at most h vertices can
     be embedded in every surface of Euler-genus at least 2h′ + c′ and G contains a subdivision of a
     walloid W ′ which is the cylindrical concatenation of h′ many k-handle segments and c′ many
     k-crosscap segments such that the base cylinder of W ′ is a subgraph of W, or
                                        2
  3. a set S ⊆ V (G) of size at most f9.2 (h, k) and a list of graphs Z, all of which are of size at most
     h, such that, if J is the unique component of G − S which contains a full row and a full column
     of W,
      (a) for every graph Z on at most h vertices, if Z ∈
                                                        / Z then J is Z-minor-free, and
      (b) for every graph Z ∈ Z there exists an embedding pair p ∈ P(Z) such that the algorithm
          outputs a subdivision WZ of Wp
                                       k of G where the base cylinder of WZ is a subgraph of W.

Moreover, we have that
                                                   Oh (1)                          Oh (1)
                                 1
                                f9.2 (k, h) ∈ 2k                 2
                                                            and f9.2 (k, h) ∈ 2k            .

Proof. Let us start by discussing the numbers. We set g := g(h) to be the smallest integer g such that
every graph on at most h vertices embeds in every surface of Euler-genus at least g. Observe that,
since there are, up to isomorphism, only finitely many graphs on at most h vertices, and thus g is
well-defined. Indeed, it holds that g ∈ poly(h). Similarly, we know that for every extension of a graph
on at most h vertices it holds that the graph of the extension has at most h := h(h) ∈ O(h2 ) terminals.
Hence, it will suffice to only consider folios of detail h.
   We then set
                                               
                 1              1                         1     2                 1
                f9.2 (h, k) := f4.1   h, k, f4.3 f5.4 f6.2  (f9.1 (k, h), k, h, f9.1 (k, h), x, y, h)
                                                                                                     
                                    2     2                 1
                                                                                             
                               + f6.2  (f9.1 (k, h), k, h, f9.1 (k, h), x, y, h) + 1, k, h, h , h, g

and
                                   2              2                3
                                  f9.2 (h, k) := f4.1 (h, k, k) + f9.1 (q, p, k, h).

10      Extensions of graphs in their guest walloids
This section is dedicated to the proof of the following theorem which is of crucial importance in
our quest of proving Erdős-Pósa dualities. The results of these sections will be used in the proof of
Lemma 11.7 in order to show that the walloids we consider contain large half-integral brambles.
                                                                                (Z)
Theorem 10.1. For every graph Z, there exists a function f10.1 : N2 → N such that, for every
embedding pair p of P(Z) , Z is a minor of Wp(Z) . Moreover
                                                       f10.1 (|Z|)

                                                (Z)
                                              f10.1 (|Z|) = 2O(ℓ(h(|Z|))) .


                                                             102
    This theorem can be seen as an extension of the fact (see [68]) that every planar graph Z is a
minor of some large enough grid, whose size only depends on the size of Z. This result has been
further extended by Gavoille and Hilaire [25] for the Dyck-walloids21 that are the Wp ’s corresponding
to embedding pairs of the form p = (Σ, ∅). Theorem 10.1 is an extension of these results to every
embedding pair.

10.1     Designs of embedding pairs
In this subsection, we introduce the notion of a design of embedding pairs. This notion, while similar
to that of a walloid (they are in fact equivalent as parametric graphs), its definition will prove more
beneficial towards showing Theorem 10.1.

Filaments. Let z, t ∈ N≥3 and consider a (z, t)-wall W. Let P1 , . . . , Pt and Q1 , . . . , Qz be the

refer to the set P as the paths of the filament.

depends on the size of Z.

   Let p = (Σ, B) and assume that p is generated by the triple (M, P, Γ) where, M is a connected
graph that contains Z as a minor, where |M | ≤ ext(|Z|), P ∈ P(M ), and Γ is a Σ-embedding of KP .

graph and whenever we refer to a hyperedge as an edge we mean a hyperedge of order two. We first
obtain a new hypergraph H from KP as follows.

   • For every vertex u ∈ V (KP ), let Eu denote the set of hyperedges that contain u. We define a
     hypergraph H ′′ from KP by replacing u by a path Pu on |Eu | many fresh vertices, where every
     vertex of Pu replaces u in exactly one distinct hyperedge in Eu . Observe that H ′′ is Σ-embeddable.
     Let Γ′ be a Σ-embedding of H ′′ obtained from Γ in the obvious way.

   • Next, from H ′′ we define a hypergraph H ′ by considering, for every i ∈ [h], a non-contractible
     cycle Cih cutting through the i-th handle of Σ that intersects Γ′ only in edges of H ′′ and exactly
     once per edge. We then subdivide each edge that Cih intersects twice and we consider Tih to be
     the unique set of edges incident to the newly introduced vertices. Additionally, for every i ∈ [c],
     we consider a non-contractible cycle Cic cutting through the i-th crosscap of Σ, i.e., a cycle such


                                                      106
      that one of the two sides is a Moebius strip, that intersects Γ′ only in edges of H ′′ and exactly

      point of intersection, and we consider Tic to be the unique set of edges incident to the newly
      introduced vertices.

say Bi , of boundary vertices of Wi that correspond to each neighbour of vi that was created by the
previously subdivided edges. We do this while making sure that the subgraph of Wi we have used to
make this connection is disjoint from all others and that the vertices in Bi are pairwise at distance at
least max{|Bi | | vi ∈ Λ} ∈ O(ext(|Z|)2 ) (i.e., if they are on the same vertical path of Wi there should
be at least O(ext(|Z|)2 ) horizontal paths between the ones they belong to and symmetrically if they
belong on the same horizontal path). By performing this step we have successfully managed to find
a subcubic model for each of the vertices of Λ. This can be done via standard routing arguments
(similarly to Lemma 5.2 for example). Now, every edge of H we want to model, can be modelled as
a Bi -Bj -path for distinct i, j ∈ [f ]. Moreover, the collection of all these paths we are looking form a
linkage. However, this is an instance of the disjoint paths problem in a planar graph as certified by
H − T being plane-embeddable. Hence, again by standard routing arguments, we can show that if we
                                                                 (Z)
carefully choose the constants hidden in the definition of f10.4 (|Z|) we can find the desired paths using
the infrastructure of Dp  t′ away from the walls Wi , i ∈ [f ] we have chosen at the previous step.

11      Universal obstructions for H-treewidth
In this section, we make a first step in tying together all of the previously established structural
theorems to show that Theorem 9.2 either provides an obstruction to H-treewidth or can be used to
produce an object we call a “H-tree decomposition” of small width.
    We first, in Subsection 11.1, provide a formal definition of H-tree decompositions and their width
and then show that the resulting parameter equals H-tw.
    Then, in Subsection 11.2 we provide an extension of the notion of brambles to the setting of
H-treewidth. These notions build the foundation for both the lower and the upper bound through our
universal obstructions. The proof of Theorem 1.4 is then presented in Subsection 11.3.
    Finally, towards the proof of Theorem 1.5 in Subsection 11.4 we provide an argument that shows
how Theorem 9.2 can be used to obtain an H-tree decomposition of small width in the absence of any
of our universal obstructions.

11.1      A torso-free definition of H-treewidth
Let us begin by finally introducing a definition of tree-decompositions.
   Let G be a graph. A tree decomposition of G is a pair (T, β) where T is a tree and β : V (T ) → 2V (G)
such that

   • t∈V (T ) β(t) = V (G),
      S

    • for every e ∈ E(G) there exists t ∈ V (T ) such that e ⊆ β(t), and

    • for every v ∈ V (G) the set {t ∈ V (T ) | v ∈ β(t)} induces a connected subgraph of T.

We refer to the vertices of T as the nodes of the tree decomposition and to the sets β(t) as its
bags. For every t ∈ V (T ) the sets in {β(d) ∩ β(t) | dt ∈ E(T )} are called the adhesions of
the node t. The width of (T, β) is defined as maxt∈V (T ) |β(t)| − 1 and its adhesion is defined as
maxt∈V (T ) maxA∈{β(d)∩β(t) | dt∈E(T )} |A|. The treewidth of G, denoted by tw(G), is defined to be the
minimum width over all tree decompositions of G.
    Now let H be any proper minor-closed graph class22 . A tree decomposition (T, β) for a graph G is
called an H-tree decomposition if for every leaf d ∈ V (T ) it holds that G[β(d) \ Ad ] ∈ H where Ad is
the unique adhesion set of d. The width of an H-tree decomposition (T, β) is defined as

                      max {0} ∪ {|β(t)| − 1 | t ∈ V (T ) is not a leaf of T }
                                 ∪ {|Ad | − 1 | d ∈ V (T ) is a leaf with adhesion set Ad }.

Where we consider ∅ to be the adhesion set of the node d if d is the unique vertex of T.
   We begin with the simple observation that H-tree decompositions allow for a purely decomposition-
based definition of H-treewidth.
 22 Please note that the definition can also be extended to any graph class H, but in this paper we are only interested in

minor-closed graph classes.




                                                          108
Lemma 11.1. Let H be a proper minor-closed graph class. Then for every graph G it holds that
H-tw(G) is equal to the minimum width over all H-tree decompositions of G.

Proof. We first show that there always exists an H-tree decomposition of width H-tw(G). To see
this let X ⊆ V (G) be a set of vertices such that every component of G − X belongs to H and
tw(torso(G, X)) is minimum over all choices for such a set X. Let G+ := torso(G, X) and let (T ′ , β ′ ) be
a tree decomposition of minimum width for G+ . It follows that the width of (T ′ , β ′ ) equals H-tw(G).
    Let J be any component of G − X, then N (J) is a clique of G+ . Hence, there exists a node
tJ ∈ V (T ′ ) such that N (J) ⊆ β(tJ ). Therefore, for every such component J we may introduce a new
vertex dJ adjacent to tJ and declare the set V (J) ∪ N (J) to be the bag of dJ . Let (T ′′ , β ′′ ) be the
resulting tree decomposition of G. Suppose there exists some leaf d ∈ V (T ′′ ) such that G[β(d)\Ad ] ∈
                                                                                                      / H.
This means, in particular, that d ∈ V (T ′ ). For each of these leaves d we introduce a new vertex d′
adjacent to d and declare its bag to also be β(d) which implies that |β(d′ )| − 1 = |β(d)| − 1 ≤ H-tw(G).
    It follows that the resulting decomposition, let us call it (T, β), is now an H-tree decomposition for
G. Moreover, its width is exactly the width of (T ′ , β ′ ).
    For the reverse inequality let (T, β) be an H-tree decomposition of G of minimum width, say k.
In case T consists of a single vertex we must have G ∈ H and H-tw(G) = 0 = k. Hence, we may
assume that T has more than one vertex.      S Let L be the set of all leaves of T. Then, for every leaf
d ∈ L let Jd := G[β(d) \ Ad ] and let F := d∈L V (Jd ). Finally, let (T ′ , β ′ ) be obtained from (T, β) by
deleting, for each d ∈ L, the set V (Jd ) from β(d) and setting T ′ := T. Notice that for each d ∈ L and
every component K of Jd it holds that N (K) ⊆ Ad . Hence, (T, β ′ ) is indeed a tree decomposition of
torso(G, V (G) \ F ). Thus, the H-treewidth of G is at most k as desired.

    Let H∅ be the graph class containing only the empty graph K0 and observe that obs(H∅ ) = {K1 }.
It is also easy to observe that H∅ -tw is the same as tw. According to the next observation, excluding
K1 defines an equivalent parameter as when excluding any ≤-antichain containing some planar graph.
    Recall that due to the Grid Theorem of Robertson and Seymour [62], a proper minor-closed graph
class H has bounded treewidth if and only if obs(H) contains a planar graph.

Observation 11.2. Let H be a proper minor-closed graph class. Then H-tw ∼ tw if and only if obs(H)
contains a planar graph.

Proof. To see this, notice that by Lemma 11.1 it suffices to consider H-tree decomposition of small
width since any tree decomposition of bounded width can easily be turned into a H-tree decomposition
by duplicating all leaves. Let G be some graph with H-tw(G) ≤ k and let (T, β) be an H-tree
decomposition of minimum width of G. Now let d ∈ V (T ) be some leaf with adhesion set Ad . Then
Gd := G[β(d) \ Ad ] ∈ H which means that tw(Gd ) ≤ hH by the Grid Theorem [62]. We may now
replace d by a whole tree decomposition of width at most hH for Gd , then add Ad to every bag of this
decomposition and finally join an arbitrary node of its tree to the ancestor of d in T. The result of
performing this operation for every leaf of T is a tree decomposition of width at most k + hH for G.

11.2      Strict H-brambles
To provide good lower bounds for H-treewidth we adapt the notion of brambles to our setting. The
basic ideas can be traced back to the papers by Reed but we adapt the proof ideas of Adler, Gottlob,
and Grohe [1].
    Let H be a proper minor-closed graph class23 . Let G be a graph. A strict H-bramble in G is a
collection B of connected subgraphs of G such that
  23 Notice that, as in the previous section, we only define these notions for minor-closed graph classes due to our setting,

but they work the same way for general graph classes. For Lemma 11.3 to hold, one should assume just hereditarity
though.



                                                            109
  1. V (B ′ ) ∩ V (B) ̸= ∅, and

  2. B ∩ H = ∅.

A hitting set for B is a set S ⊆ V (G) such that V (B) ∩ S ̸= ∅ for all B ∈ B. The order of B is then
defined as the minimum size of a hitting set for B. Finally, we define the strict H-bramble number of
G, denoted by H-sbn(G), to be the maximum order of a strict H-bramble in G.
    Note that the notion of strict brambles is a special case of the classic and more general notion of
brambles that provides an exact min-max duality in relation to the treewidth of a graph (see [74]). The
relation between strict brambles and classic brambles, as well as precise dualities for strict brambles,
have also been studied in the literature (see [6, 43, 46, 61]).

Lemma 11.3. Let H be a proper minor-closed graph class. Then for every graph G it holds that
H-sbn(G) ≤ H-tw(G) + 1.

Proof. Let k := H-tw(G) + 1 and suppose towards a contradiction that G contains a strict H-bramble
B of order at least k + 1. Notice that for every set S ⊆ V (G) of size at most k there most exist at least
one member B of B such that S ∩ V (B) = ∅. Moreover, there exists a unique component KS of G − S
such that for every B ∈ B that avoids S we have that B ⊆ K since the union of any two members of
B is connected.
     Now, notice that for every edge t1 t2 ∈ E(T ) there exists a unique i ∈ [2] such that the union of the
bags in the unique subtree Tti of T which is rooted at ti and avoids t3−i contains the vertex set of the
component Kβ(t1 )∩β(t2 ) as defined above. This is simply because |β(t1 ) ∩ β(t2 )| ≤ k while B is of order
at least k + 1. Let us call ti the dominant endpoint of t1 t2 . This observation induces an orientation T⃗
of T where each edge is oriented towards its dominant endpoint.
     Suppose there exists a node t ∈ V (T ) such that t is not the dominant endpoint of at least two of
its incident edges, say td1 and td2 . Since the unions of the bags of the two trees Tdi must intersect in a
subset of β(t) it follows that for each i ∈ [2], the bags in the subtree Tdi must contain the vertex set of
some element Bi that avoids β(t). Since V (B1 ) ∩ V (B2 ) ̸= ∅ this is impossible and thus for each node
of T there exists at most one incident edge for which the node is not dominant.
    Thus, there must exist some node t of T which is not dominant for any of its incident edges, i.e.,
t is a sink of T⃗ . In this case, none of the subtrees rooted at a neighbor of t can contain the vertex
set of a member of B which avoids β(t). Hence, β(t) is a hitting set for B which contradicts our
assumptions.

   Similar to strict H-brambles we may define a more local variant which also poses as an obstruction
to H-tw.

Highly linked sets. Let H be a proper minor-closed class. Let also G be a graph, X ⊆ V (G) be
a set of vertices, and k ∈ N. We say that the set X is k-H-linked in G if for every S ⊆ V (G), where
|S| ≤ k, there exists a component JS of G − S such that JS ̸∈ H and |V (J) ∩ X| > 32 |X|.
    As before, we associate a graph parameter with the notion of H-linked sets. For every graph G let
H-link(G) denote the maximum k such that there exists a k-H-linked set in G.

Lemma 11.4. Let H be a proper minor-closed graph class. For every graph G it holds that 2 ·
H-link(G) ≤ H-sbn(G).

Proof. Let G be a graph and X be a 2k-H-linked set on G for some positive integer k. We define the
following collection of subgraphs:

                                  BX := {JS | S ⊆ V (G) and |S| ≤ k}.



                                                   110
Moreover, we claim that BX is a strict H-bramble of order k in G. To see this we simply have to check
the axioms of strict H-brambles.
    First notice that, by definition of H-linked sets, B ∈  / H for all B ∈ BX . Moreover, every such B is
connected. This takes care of the second axiom.
    For the first axiom let JS1 , JS2 ∈ BX be two distinct members of our collection. Notice that
|S1 ∪ S2 | ≤ 2k. Thus, there also exists a component JS1 ∪S2 of G − S1 − S2 such that JS1 ∪S2 ∈        / H and
|V (JS1 ∪S2 ∩ X)| > 23 |X|. It follows that V (JS1 ∪S2 ) ∩ V (JSi ) ̸= ∅ for both i ∈ [2]. In fact, this means
JS1 ∪S2 ⊆ JSi for both i ∈ [2] and therefore V (JS1 ) ∩ V (JS2 ) ̸= ∅ as desired.
    Finally, to see that BX is of order k as claimed simply notice that, by definition, for every set
S ⊆ V (G) of size at most k there exists an element JS ∈ BX which fully avoids S.

   Finally, we complete the circle by showing that the absence of k-H-linked sets implies that H-tw is
bounded.
Lemma 11.5. Let H be a proper minor-closed graph class. For every graph G it holds that H-tw(G) ≤
4 · H-link(G) + 4.
Proof. Let k := H-link(G) + 1, then G does not contain a k-H-linked set.
   We prove the following claim by induction on |G| − |X|. The assertion will then follow immediately.

Inductive Claim. Let G be a graph with no k-H-linked set. For every set X ⊆ V (G) with
|X| ≤ 3k + 1 there exist an H-tree decomposition (T, β) of width at most 4k and a leaf d ∈ V (T ) such
that X ⊆ β(d) and |β(d)| ≤ 4k.
    Let X ⊆ V (G) be any set of size at most 3k +1. In case |X| < 3k +1 we either have that |G| < 3k +1
in which case we are immediately done, or may pick an arbitrary vertex v ∈ V (G) \ X and apply the
induction hypothesis to X ∪ {v}.
    Hence, we may assume |X| = 3k + 1. In the following, we define the desired H-tree decomposition
step by step. First let us introduce a node d0 and set β(d0 ) := X. Our goal is to make d0 into
a leaf of the final decomposition. To meet the requirements of H-tree decompositions this means
we should introduce a node d1 adjacent to d0 and also set β(d1 ) := X. This way we know that
G[β(d0 ) \ (β(d0 ) ∩ β(d1 ))] ∈ H. For each component G′ of G − X we now introduce a new vertex
tG′ adjacent to d1 . In the following, we describe how to extend this construction using our induction
hypothesis.
    Towards this goal, let us fix G′ to be the subgraph of G induced by a single component of G − X
together with X. Now, since X is not k-H-linked there exists a set S ⊆ V (G′ ) of size at most k such
that one of the following is true.
Case 1 There exists a component JS of G′ − S such that |V (JS ) ∩ X| > 23 |X| and JS ∈ H, or

Case 2 for every component J of G′ − S it holds that |V (J) ∩ X| ≤ 32 |X| ≤ 2k + 13 ≤ 2k.
   In either case we will set β(tG′ −X ) := X ∪ S. Notice that this means that |β(tG′ −X )| ≤ 4k + 1
which still fits our bounds.

Case 1. For each component J of G′ − S introduce a vertex tJ adjacent to tG′ −X and set β(tJ ) :=
(V (J) ∩ X) ∪ S. Notice that for the component JS as defined above this means |β(tJS )| ≤ 4k + 1 and for
each other component J of G′ − S we have that |V (J) ∩ X| < 31 |X| ≤ k implying |β(tJ )| ≤ 2k. We then
introduce a new node dJS adjacent to tJS and set β(dJS ) := V (JS ) ∪ S. By definition of JS we have
that G[β(dJS ) \ β(tJS )] ∈ H and thus we are fine leaving dJS as a leaf of our decomposition. For every
other component J of G′ − S we may now apply the induction hypothesis with XJ := S ∪ (X ∩ V (J))
to obtain an H-tree decomposition (TJ , βJ ) of G[V (J) ∪ S] of width at most 4k such that there exists a
leaf tJ,S ∈ V (TJ ) with XJ ⊆ β(tJ,S ) and |β(tJ,S )| ≤ 4k. We extend out partially defined decomposition


                                                     111
with the tree Tj and the bags βJ and introduce the edge tJ,S tJ . With this, we are done with the first
case.

Case 2. For each component J of G′ − S introduce a vertex tJ adjacent to tG′ −X and set β(tJ ) :=
(V (J) ∩ X) ∪ S. This time we may observe that |β(tJ )| ≤ k + 23 |X| ≤ 3k. For each J, if there exists
a vertex v ∈ J − X we introduce a vertex t′J adjacent to tJ , pick such a vertex v arbitrarily and set
β(t′J ) := β(tJ ) ∪ {v}. If there does not exist such a vertex v we still introduce the node t′J but in this
case we have that V (J) ⊆ X and thus we may simply set β(t′J ) := β(tJ ) and declare it a leaf. In this
case, it follows that G[β(t′J ) \ β(tJ )] ∈ H and thus t′J may remain a leaf for the rest of the construction.
    Hence, from now on we may assume that we were able to choose the vertex v. By calling the
induction hypothesis for G[S ∪ V (J)] and XJ := β(t′J ) and then merging our partial decomposition
with the resulting decomposition for G[S ∪ V (J)] in the same way as we did in Case 1, we also
complete this second case and thereby the proof by using Lemma 11.1.

    To conclude, we summarize the results of Lemma 11.3, Lemma 11.4, and Lemma 11.5.

Corollary 11.6. Let H be a proper minor-closed graph class. Then, for every graph G we have

                   2 · H-link(G) − 1 ≤ H-sbn(G) − 1 ≤ H-tw(H) ≤ 4 · H-link(G) + 4.

    The above corollary implies that H-link, H-sbn and H-link are equivalent with linear gap function.

11.3     The lower bound
With the lemmas from the two previous subsections, we are now ready to obtain the first link between
our walloids and the notion of H-treewidth. Indeed, we can prove an even stronger statement. For this
purpose, we need a special version of brambles.
    Let H be a proper minor-closed class. A strict H-bramble B in a graph G is said to be a 1/2-H-
bramble if no vertex of G appears in more than two members of B. Notice that, if B is a 12 -H-bramble
of order k which is not of order k + 1, then |B| ≤ 2k.

Lemma 11.7. There exists a function f11.7 : N2 → N such that for every graph Z, every embedding
pair p ∈ P(Z) and every positive integer k, Wp

   As an immediate consequence of Lemma 11.7 and Lemma 11.3, we obtain that for every proper

H-treewidth when found as a minor. In particular, this immediately implies Theorem 1.4.

11.4      The upper bound
We are now ready to prove Theorem 1.5. To do this we make use of Theorem 9.2 to show that any
graph which contains a highly H-linked set must also contain a member of WH of large order.
   In order to prove this statement we will need some additional notions from the theory of graph
minors.

Of walls and well-linked sets. Let G be a graph and X ⊆ V (G) be a vertex set. A set S ⊆ V (G) is
said to be a balanced separator for X if for every component J of G−S it holds that |V (J)∩X| ≤ 32 |X|.
We say that X is k-well-linked if there does not exist a balanced separator of size at most k for X.
    It is easy to see that well-linked sets form a special case of our H-linked sets. Indeed, they are a
well-studied object acting as obstructions to treewidth. Well-linked sets and walls both act as such
obstructions and they relate to each other in a specific way which is usually encoded in the language
of “tangles”. Since we do not have any need for the notion of tangles in this paper we provide a slightly
different definition here which can easily be seen to be equivalent by a reader familiar with these
concepts. See Section 3 of [77] for an in-depth discussion.
    Let k and r be positive integers such that k ≥ r. Let G be a graph X a k-well-linked set in G and
W an r-wall in G.
    First notice that for every set S ⊆ V (G) of order at most r − 1 there exists a unique component
JW,S of G − S which contains an entire row and an entire column of W. Similarly, for every S ⊆ V (G)
of order at most k there exists a unique component JX,S such that |V (JX,S ) ∩ X| > 23 |X|. We say that
X and W agree if for every S ⊆ V (G) of order at most r − 1 we have that JW,S ⊆ JX,S .
Theorem 11.10 ( [77]). Let k ≥ 3 be an integer. There exists a universal positive constant c ∈ N and
an algorithm that, given a graph G and a (36ck 20 + 3)-well-linked set X ⊆ V (G), computes in time
Ok (|G|2 |E(G)| log(|G|)) a k-wall W such that X and W agree.


                                                        113
   With this, we may now prove the main tool towards proving Theorem 1.5.

Lemma 11.11. Let H be a proper minor-closed graph class and let h := h(H) the largest size of an
obstruction of H. There exists a function f11.11 : N2 → N depending only on h such that, for every
graph G and every positive integer k one of the following statements holds

  1. there exists W = ⟨Wt | t ∈ N⟩ ∈ WH such that G contains a subdivision of Wk , or

  2. H-link(G) ≤ f11.11 (h, k).

Proof. Let c be the constant from Theorem 11.10. We set f11.11 (h, k) := 36c · f9.2
                                                                                  1
                                                                                     (h, k)20 + 3.
    Now suppose G contains an f11.11 (h, k)-H-linked set X. Then, in particular, for every set S ⊆ V (G)
of order at most f11.11 (h, k) there exists a unique component JX,S such that |V (JX,S ) ∩ X| > 32 |X|.
Hence, X is also f11.11 (h, k)-well-linked.
    It follows from Theorem 11.10 that there exists a f9.2
                                                        1
                                                           (h, k)-wall W which agrees with X. Now, by
Theorem 9.2 one of to things hold. Either

  1. G contains K3k2 +kh as a minor, or

  2. there exists a set A ⊆ V (G) of size at most f9.2
                                                   2             1
                                                       (h, k) < f9.2 (h, k) < f11.11 (h, k) and a set Z of
     graphs of size at most h such that the component JX,A is Z-minor-free for all graphs on at most
     h vertices which do not belong to Z.

In the first case, G must contain, as a minor, the walloid of order k which has k copies of Kh in a
single flower. Clearly, this walloid contains a member of WH of order k as desired.
    Hence, we may assume we are in the second outcome. Since JX,A ∈       / H and every obstruction of
H has size at most h, there must exist some graph H ∈ obs(H) ∩ Z. Therefore, the second outcome
of Theorem 9.2 holds for H which means that there exists WH = ⟨WH        t | t ∈ N⟩ ∈ Wexcl(H) such that
G contains a subdivision WH of WH     k . Moreover, the base cylinder of  WH is a subgraph of W. Since
Wexcl(H) ⊆ WH this is the first outcome of our claim. Hence, if the first outcome does not hold it must
be true that H-link(G) ≤ f11.11 (h, k).

    Please notice that, with standard techniques for finding balanced separators, Theorem 11.10, and
Theorem 9.2 the procedure described in the proof of Lemma 11.5 yields an algorithm that either finds
one of the obstructions, and therefore a large half-integral H-barrier, or an H-tree decomposition
of bounded width in time Oh,k (|G|4 log |G|). Based on this decomposition we can then proceed as
discussed in the proof of Lemma 12.3 to either find a bounded size modulator to H or a large integral
H-barrier. The purpose of Section 12 is to make this more explicit.
    We are now ready to give a proof of Theorem 1.5.

Proof of Theorem 1.5. Let f1.5 (h, k) := 4·f11.11 (h, k)+8. By Corollary 11.6 it follows that, if H-tw(G) ≥
f1.5 (h, k), then H-link(G) ≥ f11.11 (h, k) + 1. This means that the second outcome of Lemma 11.11 is
impossible and thus, the first outcome must hold as desired.


12     Universal obstructions for apexH and H-td
In this section, we give a proof that Fmin
                                       H is a universal obstruction for apexH . For this, we proceed
in two steps. First, in Subsection 12.1, we prove this result for the case where all obstructions of H
are connected, which is equivalent to the property that H is closed under taking disjoint subgraphs.
Then, using this special case as a starting point, we prove the general result in Subsection 12.2. In
Subsection 12.3 we apply similar ideas as those of Subsection 12.2 in order to find an obstruction set
for the parameter of elimination distance to H, defined by Bulian and Dawar in [9].



                                                   114
   The parameter apexH has been extensively studied for different instantiations of H. Given a proper
minor-closed class H we define, for every k ∈ N,

                                    APk          = {G | apexH (G) ≤ k}                                           (21)
                                                                     ap
In [2] it was shown that for every H there is a computable function fH  : N → N such that for every
                                      ap
k ∈ N, if Z ∈ obs(APk ), then |Z| ≤ fH (k) and this implied that the problem of checking whether
apexH (G) ≤ k is constructively in FPT when parameterized by k (see also [22]). Recently, in [73]
                                     polyh (k)
                      ap           22
an explicit bound of fH  (k) = 22        was given. Also, in [72], an algorithm checking whether
apexH (G) ≤ k was given, running in time 2polyh (k) |G|2 .

12.1    Global apex pairs
Given a graph Z we define the connectivization of Z, denoted by conn(Z), as the set of all edge-minimal
connected graphs that contains Z as a subgraph. Notice that every graph conn(Z) is the graph Z plus
a minimal number of edges that make it connected. Therefore, |conn(Z)| = 2O(|Z|·log |Z|) . Given a finite
set Z of graphs, we define its minor-connectivization as the set conn(Z) = min({conn(Z) | Z ∈ Z})
where, given some set of graphs A, we use min(A) for the set of minor-minimal graphs in A.
   Given a proper minor-closed class H we define its connectivity closure to be

                    H(c) := {G | every connected component of G belongs to H}.

The obstructions for H(c) were described by Bulian and Dawar [10] as follows.
Proposition 12.1. For every proper minor-closed class H it holds that obs(H(c) ) = conn(obs(H)).
   We define the parameter size : Gall → N such that size(G) = |G|. Based on this, by replacing tw
with size size in (6) we define the following parameter:

            H-size(G) := min{k | there exists X ⊆ V (G) s.t. |X| ≤ k and G − X ∈ H(c) }

   Observe that H-size = apexH(c) if and only if all obstructions of H are connected. However, in
general H-size ⪯ apexH . Let

                                           SH      :=     WH ∪ W∅
                                                                H(c)
                                                                                                                 (22)
                                         Smin
                                          H        :=     min(SH ).

Next, we prove that SH is an obstructing set for H-size. We first prove the lower bound.
Observation 12.2. For every minor-closed class H, it holds that pSH ⪯ H-size with linear gap
function.
Proof. Recall that pSH = max{pWH , pW∅                  }. The inequality pW∅               ⪯ H-size, with linear gap
                                                 H(c)                                H(c)

The other inequality pWH ⪯ H-tw, with linear gap function, holds because of Corollary 11.8 and
H-tw ≤ H-size.
   Now we prove the upper bound, i.e., the direction H-size ⪯ pSH .
Lemma 12.3. There exists a function f12.3 : N2 → N such that for proper every minor-closed class
H, where h(H) = h, every graph G, and every k ∈ N, either pSH (G) ≥ k or H-size(G) ≤ f12.3 (h, k),
                       O (1)

Proof. Let G be a graph where pSH (G) ≤ k. This implies that pWH (G) ≤ pSH (G) ≤ k and
pW∅ (G) ≤ pSH (G) ≤ k.
   H(c)
    As pWH (G) ≤ k, due to Theorem 1.5, we have that H-tw(G) ≤ f1.5 (h, k). Let q := f1.5 (h, k) and
let (T, β) be an H-tree decomposition of G of width at most q.
    Let mp(G) be the maximum size of a collection C of pairwise disjoint subgraphs of G such
that for every C ∈ C there is some graph in conn(obs(H)) which is a minor of C. Observe that
mp(G) ≤ |conn(obs(H))| · k, otherwise G would contain a H(c) -barrier of size larger than k which would
contradict the fact that pW∅ (G) ≤ k.
                             H(c)
    Let p be some positive integer. We claim that if mp(G) ∈ [p] then H-size(G) ≤ |S| ≤ (q +1)·(2p−1).
We prove this by induction on p. Given an edge tt′ of T where T1 (resp. T2 ) is the connected component
of T − e containing t (resp. t′ ), we define
                                    [                               [
                         Xt :=              β(b) and Xt′ :=                 β(b)                   (23)
                                b∈V (T1 )\{t}                    b∈V (T2 )\{t′ }


If, for some edge tt′ ∈ E(T ), it holds that mp(G[Xt \ Xt′ ]) = p1 ≥ 1 and mp(G[Xt′ \ Xt ]) = p2 ≥ 1,
then we have that p1 + p1 ≤ p and, from the induction hypothesis it follows that H-size(G) ≤
|Xt ∩Xt′ |+mp(G[Xt \Xt′ ])+mp(G[Xt′ \Xt ]) ≤ (q+1)+(q+1)·(2p1 −1)+(q+1)·(2p2 −1) ≤ (q+1)·(2p−1).
     If there exists some edge tt′ ∈ E(G) such that mp(G[Xt \ Xt′ ]) = p1 = 0 and mp(G[Xt′ \ Xt ]) =
p2 = 0, we obtain that every component of G − (Xt ∩ Xt′ ) belongs to H which would complete our
proof.
    The only remaining case is where for every edge tt′ of T, either mp(G[Xt \ Xt′ ]) = p1 = 0 or
mp(G[Xt′ \ Xt ]) = p2 = 0, but not both.
     In this case, we orient each tt′ towards the component where the value of mp is positive. This
means that there is a node t of T which is a sink of the resulting orientation of T. Thus, G − Xt does
not contain any graph of conn(obs(H)) as a minor. Therefore, every component of G − Xt belongs to
H and Xt is an H(c) -modulator of G of size q + 1 ≤ (q + 1) · (2p − 1) as desired.
    Applying the claim above for p = |conn(obs(H))| · k, one may obtains that H-size(G) ≤ 2(q +
                                                             2
1) · |conn(obs(H))| · k. Recall that |conn(obs(H))| = 2O(h ) . Therefore the lemma holds for some
                 O (1)
f12.3 (h, k) = 2k h .

   We just proved that pSH ∼ H-size with a single-exponential gap. This is a first step towards the
main result of this section, which is that FH is an obstructing set for apexH . This is proved in the next
subsection.

12.2      The disconnected case
Given a graph Z we use cc(Z) for the set of its components. Given two graphs Z1 and Z2 , we denote
by Z1 + Z2 the disjoint union of Z1 and Z2 . Also, given two parametric graphs Gi = ⟨Git | t ∈ N⟩, i ∈ [2],
we write G1 + G2 := ⟨G1t + G2t | t ∈ N⟩ for their index-wise disjoint union. Moreover, if Gi , i ∈ [2] are
families of parametric graphs, we define the parametric family

                              G1 ⊗ G2 := {G1 + G2 | (G1 , G2 ) ∈ G1 × G2 }.

Notice that the compositions defined above are commutative and they may be extended in the natural
way to Z1 + · · · + Zr , to G1 + · · · + Gr and to G1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ Gr respectively.

Lemma 12.5. Let Z1 and Z2 be two graphs and let F1 and F2 be obstructing sets for apexexcl(Z1 ) and
apexexcl(Z2 ) respectively both with gap function f. Then pF1 ⊗F2 ⪯ apexexcl(Z1 +Z2 ) .
Proof. Let k ∈ N and let Fki ∈ Fi ∈ Fi , i ∈ [2]. If a graph G contains some graph Fk1 + Fk2 as a
minor, then apexH (G) ≥ apexH (Fk1 + Fk2 ) ≥ barrierH (Fk1 + Fk2 ), which, by Observation 12.4, is at least
max{barrierH1 (Fk1 ), barrierH2 (Fk2 )} ≥ f (k). This immediately implies that pF1 ⊗F2 ⪯ apexexcl(Z1 +Z2 ) .

   For every proper minor-closed class H, we define the parameter pH := max{apexexcl(Z) | Z ∈
obs(H)}. One may immediately observe the following.
Observation 12.6. For every proper minor-closed class H, it holds that pH ⪯ apexH ⪯ |obs(H)| · pH .
   An immediate consequence of this observation is, that the parametric family Fexcl(Z) indeed defines
a parameter that acts as a lower bound to apexexcl(Z) even if Z is not connected.
Corollary 12.7. For every proper minor-closed class H, it holds that pFH ⪯ apexH , with linear gap.
Proof. By applying Lemma 12.5 inductively to (24), while using Observation 12.2 as the base case, we
obtain that for every graph Z, pFexcl(Z) ⪯ apexexcl(Z) . The corollary holds due to the first inequality of
Observation 12.6 and the fact that pFH = max{pFexcl(Z) | Z ∈ obs(H)}.

The function g(r, h, k) is a placeholder for the actual function f12.8 we wish to define to prove the

   Our proof proceeds by induction on r. That is, we prove the following statement by induction on
the number of components of Z.

Inductive statement. For every positive integer k, every graph Z on at most h vertices with at
most r components, and every graph G one of the following hold

   1. G contains Fk as a minor for some ⟨Fr ⟩r∈N ∈ Fexcl(Z) , or

Base of the induction. The base follows in a straightforward way from Lemma 12.3. This is true
since for the base we may consider the case r = 1 where g(r, h, k) = f12.3 (h, k) and Z is connected.
With Z being connected there is no difference between the connectivizations of Z and Z itself and
therefore the claim follows.

Inductive step. From here on, we may assume that r ≥ 2. As a first step, we consider the set Z2 of
all 2-connectivizations of Z. Notice that every graph Z ′ ∈ Z2 has exactly r − 1 components. Moreover,
|Z2 | ≤ |Z|2 ≤ h2 . For each Z ′ ∈ Z2 we may now call upon the induction hypothesis and obtain either

   • a set SZ ′ of size at most g(r − 1, h, ck) such that G − SZ ′ ∈ excl(Z ′ ), or

there would exist components H1 and H2 of H such that there exists a path from H1 to H2 in G1

     For every i ∈ [r] let Ki be the set of all components of G1 that contain the component Zi of Z
as a minor. Moreover, for any set K of components of G1 let K be the set of all components of G1
which do not belong to K. We first distinguish two cases, one of which is easily dealt with, and then
we enter, as part of the second case, an iterative construction process that eventually leads to either
our obstruction or the desired excl(Z)-modulator. The cases we distinguish are

Case 1: |Ki | ≥ r · k for every i ∈ [r], and

Case 2: there exists i1 ∈ [r] such that |Ki1 | < r · k.




                                                      118
Notice that, in Case 1, we may greedily select k components from Ki for every i and thereby obtain
k · Z as a minor of G1 . Since ⟨t · Z⟩t∈N ∈ Fexcl(Z) , this outcome would complete our proof and thus, we
may assume to be in Case 2. In the following, we will, at times, apply a similar counting argument.
To facilitate our writing, we will simply refer to this argument as the few components argument.
    We initialize I1 := {i1 } and iteratively construct sets Iq and Xq as follows.

Target of our construction. For every q ≥ 1 there exists a set Iq = {i1 , . . . , iq } ⊆ I such that
there exists a set Xq of size at most qr · rk · f12.3 (h, k) and a set Qq of at most q · r · k components of
G1 with the following properties

   1. for every i ∈ Iq and every component K ∈ Qq either K − Xq is Zi -minor-free, or K contains Fki
      as a minor for some ⟨Fti ⟩t∈N ∈ Fexcl(Zi ) ,

   2. for every i ∈ Iq and every component K ∈ Qq it holds that K is Zi -minor-free, and

   3. for every minimal subgraph H of G1 − Xq which contains Z as a minor and every i ∈ Iq there
      exists a component Ki ∈ Qq such that H ∩ Ki is minimal with respect to containing Zi as a
      minor. Moreover, if i, j ∈ Iq are distinct, then so are Ki and Kj .

Once we complete this construction, we obtain that Ir = I. During this construction, we stop whenever
we reach a point where G1 − Xq is Z-minor-free, or contains our desired obstruction. For the second
outcome of this stopping condition we will employ a small argument based on Hall’s Theorem, this
argument will finally also complete the case q = r.
    We proceed with this construction by induction on q and, as the base, show how to obtain the set
X1 .

Base of our construction. We set Q1 := Ki1 . Notice that the second and third conditions
immediately hold by the definition of Ki1 since no other component of G1 can contain Zi1 as a minor
and no Z-minor can realize two of its components in the same component of G1 . For each of the at
most r · k members of Ki1 we may apply Lemma 12.3 for k and the graph Zi1 . Let X1 be the union of
all hitting sets, of size at most f12.3 (h, k) each, that are returned this way. It follows that I1 and X1
also satisfy the first condition of our construction.

The inductive step of our construction. Let us now assume that for some q ∈ [r − 1] we have
already constructed the sets Iq , Qq , and Xq as above.
    First, let us introduce the matching argument. Let Bq be the bipartite graph with Iq as one of
the two color classes and Qq as the other color class. There exists an edge iK ∈ E(Bq ) if and only if the
component K ∈ Qq still contains Zi as a minor after deleting Xq . Notice that, by the second condition
of our construction, this means that K must contain Fki as a minor for some ⟨Fti ⟩t∈N ∈ Fexcl(Zi ) . Now,
if Bq does not have a matching that covers the entire set Iq , then, by Hall’s Theorem, there exists a
set I ′ ⊆ Iq such that |NBq (I ′ )| < |I ′ |. Since every occurrence of Z as a minor in G1 − Xq must use q
distinct members of Qq , one for each i ∈ Iq , to realize the components Zi , i ∈     SIq , this implies that
G1 − Xq is Z-minor-free and we may terminate. Hence, we may assume that Qq contains Fk as a
minor for some ⟨Ft ⟩t∈N ∈ Fexcl(Iq ) .
    Now suppose that |Ki \ Qq | ≥ |I \ Iq |k for every i ∈ I \ Iq . By the few components argument and
the matching argument above this implies that G1 contains Fk as a minor for some ⟨Ft ⟩t∈N ∈ Fexcl(Z) .
Hence, we may assume that there is some iq+1 ∈ I \ Iq such that |Kq+1 \ Qq | < |I \ Iq |k. Let
Qq+1 := Qq ∪ Kiq+1 .
    Next, we make use of Lemma 12.3. That is, we apply Lemma 12.3 for Ziq+1 and every member of
Qq+1 . Let Xq+1′

12.3     Universal obstructions for H-td
So far we presented universal obstructions for the parameters H-tw and apexH , namely Wmin
                                                                                         H and FH
                                                                                                min

respectively. Notice that the knowledge of WH has been important for the derivation of FH as it
permitted to assume that H-tw is bounded and consider a bounded width tree-apexH decomposition
in the proof of Lemma 12.3. We next apply the same approach to the elimination distance to H, also
known as the H-tree-depth, that is recursively defined as follows:

                                                                           if G ∈ H,
                     
                     0
                     
        tdH (G) =      1 + min{edH (G \ {v}) | v ∈ V (G)}                  if G is connected,  (28)
                       max{edH (H) | H is a connected component of G} otherwise.
                     
                     

Another way to define tdH is to just replace in (6) tw by td, that is the parameter of tree-depth. The
tree-depth of a graph G is defined as the minimum height of a rooted forest F with the property that
every edge of G connects a pair of vertices that have an ancestor-descendant relationship to each other
in F. It is easy to observe that td = td∅ (recall that ∅ = excl(K1 )). The parameter tdH was introduced
by Bulian and Dawar [9, 10] as a distance measure of a graph to some graph class H. Notice that tdH
remains the same parameter if we replace H by H(c) .
    It can be easily seen that for every graph G, twH (G) ≤ H-tw(G) ≤ apexH (G). For every k, let

                                      T DkH    =    {G | H-td(G) ≤ k}.                                            (29)

In [10], Bulian and Dawar proved that there is a computable function bounding the obstructions of T DkH ,
i.e., for every H, there is a computable function fH
                                                   td
                                                      : N → N where for every k ∈ N, if Z ∈ obs(T DkH ),
then |Z| ≤ fH (k). This implied that the problem of deciding whether H-td(G) ≤ k is constructively in
               td
                                                                                   2polyh (k)
FPT, when parameterized by k. This was improved in [53] where a time 22                         n2 algorithm for this
                                                                                                      polyh (k)
                                                                                                    22
problem was given. Moreover, the results in [53] imply an explicit bound fH   td
                                                                                 (k) = 22      .
    In this section, we explain how to use an approach similar to the one of Subsection 12.1 in order to
find a universal obstruction for H-td.
    We start from the following key observation of Bulian and Dawar in [10].

Lemma 12.9. For every minor-closed class H, obs(H(c) ) = conn(obs(H)).

   A 2-rooted-graph is a triple Z = (Z, v, u) where Z is a connected graph and v, u ∈ V (Z). We say
that two 2-rooted-graphs Z = (Z, v, u) and Z′ = (Z ′ , v ′ , u′ ) are isomorphic if there is an isomorphism


                                                      120
σ : V (Z) → V (Z ′ ) such that σ(v) = v ′ and σ(u) = u′ . We define R(Z) as the set of all pairwise
non-isomorphic 2-rooted-graphs whose graph is Z. An H-chain of size t is a graph obtained if we first
consider a sequence

                                       ⟨(Z 1 , v 1 , u1 ), . . . , (Z t , v t , ut )⟩                           (30)

of 2-rooted graphs, where for each i ∈ [t], there exists some Z i ∈ obs(H) such that (Z i , v i , ui ) ∈ R(Z),
and then add the edges in {ui , v i+1 } | i ∈ [t − 1] in the disjoint union of the graphs in (30). An
H-chain where (Z i , v i , ui ) is isomorphic to some particular Z, then we call it the Z-chain. For every
2-rooted graph Z = (Z, v, u) ∈ R(Z), we define the parametric graph EZ = ⟨EZ         t ⟩t∈N where Et is the
                                                                                                        Z

Z-chain of size t.

Observation 12.10. There exists a function f : N → N such that for every minor-closed class H and
every k ∈ N, every H-chain of size t · f (h) contains EZ
                                                       t as a minor for some Z ∈ R(Z).

   Let H be a minor-closed class and let OH = obs(H(c) ). Then we define the following collections of
parametric graphs.
                                               [ 
                              TH := WH ∪            EZ | Z ∈ R(Z)                                (31)
                                                             Z∈OH

                                  Tmin
                                   H       := min(TH )

   The next observation follows immediately from (28) and the definition of EZ .

Observation 12.11. Let H be a minor-closed class and Z ∈ OH . Then for every graphs G, every
2-rooted-graph graph Z ∈ R(Z), every EZ = ⟨EZ                                             Z
                                            t ⟩t∈N , and every t ∈ N, it holds that tdH (Et ) = Ω(log(t)).

Theorem 12.12. Let H be a minor-closed class. Then TH is a universal obstruction for H-td with
single exponential gap.

Proof. Let EH := Z∈OH EZ | Z ∈ R(Z) ). We first prove that pTH ⪯ H-td. For this let k ∈ N
                    S        

and let Tk ∈ T = ⟨Tk ⟩k∈N ∈ TH . In the case where T ∈ WH we know from Corollary 11.8 that
H-td(Tt ) ≥ H-tw(Tt ) = Ωh (k), where h = h(H). In case where T ∈ EH , it follows from Observation 12.11
that H-td(Gt ) = Ω(log(k)). This proves pTH ⪯ H-td.
   We proceed with the proof that H-td ⪯ pTH . Let k ∈ N and let G be a graph minor-excluding
Tk ∈ T for every T ∈ TH . W.l.o.g. we assume that G is connected, otherwise we apply the proof to
                                                                                         O (1)
each of the components of G. As TH ≲∗ WH we know that H-tw(G) ≤ q := 2k h . Let (T, β) be a
H-tree decomposition of G of width ≤ q. We define the parameter H-lc(G) as the maximum k for
which G contains an H-chain of size k as a minor. From Observation 12.10 we have that H-lc(G) is
bounded by Oh (pEH (G)) = Oh (k).
    Therefore, it is enough to prove that, for every pair of integers k ′ , q ′ ∈ N, and every graph G of
H-treewidth at most q ′ , if H-lc(G) ≤ k ′ , then H-td(G) ≤ (q ′ + 1) · k ′ . For this, we use induction on k ′ .
For every edge tt′ of T we define Xt and Xt′ as in (23).
   We first observe that if there is some edge e = tt′ of T where H-lc(G[Xt \ Xt′ ]) ≤ k ′ − 1 and
H-lc(G[Xt′ \ Xt ]) ≤ k ′ − 1, then

H-td(G) ≤ |Xt ∩ Xt′ | + max{H-td(G[Xt \ Xt′ ]), H-td(G[Xt′ \ Xt ])} ≤ k ′ + (q ′ + 1) · (k ′ − 1) = (q ′ + 1) · k ′ .

On the other side if it holds that if H-lc(G[Xt \ Xt′ ]) = H-lc(G[Xt′ \ Xt ]) = k ′ , then the connected
graph G contains some subgraph Z of G[Xt \ Xt′ ] and some subgraph Z ′ of G[Xt′ \ Xt ] such that both
Z and Z ′ are H-chains of size k ′ and there is a path joining Z and Z ′ . This leads to a contradiction
because two H-chains of length k ′ connected by some path implies the existence of some H-chain of
length k ′ + 1.


                                                              121
     The conclusion of the above is that for every e = tt′ exactly one of G[Xt \ Xt′ ] and G[Xt′ \ Xt ]
contains a H-chain of length k ′ . We orient every edge towards the direction where the H-chain of length
k ′ is located. This implies that there is a node t of T which is a sink with respect to this orientation of
T.
     If t is not a leaf of T let A := β(t), otherwise let A := At where At is the unique adhesion set of
the leaf t ∈ V (T ). As a consequence, if C is a connected component of G − A, then H-lc(C) ≤ k ′ − 1,
therefore, by the induction hypothesis, H-td(G) ≤ (q ′ + 1) · (k ′ − 1).
     Then,

     H-td(G) ≤ |Xt | + max{H-td(C) | C ∈ cc(G − A)} ≤ q ′ + 1 + (q ′ + 1) · (k ′ − 1) = (q ′ + 1) · k ′

which completes the proof of the theorem.


13     Consequences and open problems
In this paper we provided obstructing sets for the parameters H-tw, H-td, and apexH . Interestingly,
our obstructing set WH for H-tw is a common ingredient to all other obstructing sets constructed in
this paper. Our proofs are all essentially based on the fact that excluding the parametric graphs in
WH allows for the assumption of the existence of a H-tree decomposition of bounded width. This, in
turn, facilitates the proofs that determine the rest of the parametric graphs in the obstructing sets
for H-td, and apexH . In the case of apexH , the “half-integral” nature of the walloids in WH is the
essential reason for the half-integral Erdős-Pósa property of graph minors as asserted by Liu’s theorem.
Moreover, this is exactly how we obtain our constructive proof of Thomas’ conjecture and thereby
completely delineate the borderline between integrality and half-integrality in Erdős-Pósa dualities for
minors.
    The algorithmic consequences have already been discussed in Subsection 1.3. We discuss below
further directions yet to be investigated.

Compositions of classes and parameter. Let H be a minor-closed graph class and let p be a
minor-monotone graph parameter. Extending the idea of the definition in (6), we define the composition
of H and p as the parameter H-p : Gall → N where

                H-p(G) = min{k | there exists X ⊆ V (G) s.t. p(torso(G, X)) ≤ k and                       (32)
                                    every component of G − X belongs to H}

Notice that if p ⪯ p′ then H-p ⪯ H-p′ . From this point of view, H-tw ⪯ H-td ⪯ H-size is implied
immediately by the fact that tw ⪯ td ⪯ size. These three parameters have been studied extensively
from the algorithmic point of view in [4, 5, 18, 29, 31, 32, 53].
    Observe that the edgeless graphs in ⟨t · K1 ⟩t∈N is a universal obstruction of size and that the paths
in ⟨Pt ⟩t∈N is a universal obstruction of td. According to (22), another way to define the collection SH
is the following.

                                                       [ 
                                   SH     = WH ∪             ⟨t · Z⟩t∈N                                   (33)
                                                     Z∈OH

Let’s discuss the similarities in the definitions of (31) and (33).
                                                                  The first is that WH always appears
as a common “half-integral” component. Notice that, in (31), EZ | Z ∈ R(Z) expresses all possible
ways of substituting the vertices an edges in the paths of the universal obstructions of td by replacing



                                                    122
vertices and edges by an obstruction Z of H. Similarly, in (33), ⟨t · Z⟩t∈N substitutes the vertices of
the edgeless graphs by the universal obstructions of size by copies of some obstruction Z of H.
     We believe that a similar composition mechanism should be able to construct universal obstruc-
tions for every parameter H-p, where tw ⪯ p ⪯ size and p ̸∈ {tw, size}. Assume that p has some
finite universal obstruction Q and that Q = ⟨Qt ⟩t∈N is one of its parametric graphs. Given some
obstruction Z of H, we apply the following transformation to each Qt : pick three 2-rooted graphs
(Z v , v, xv ), (Z x , x, x), (Z u , xu , u) ∈ R(Z) and consider the 2-rooted graph Z = (Z, v, u) where Z is
obtained by the disjoint union of Z v , Z x , and Z u and the identification of x, xv , and xu to x. We
denote by RZ the set containing all 2-rooted graphs Z that can be defined in this way. Next, we
replace each edge {v, u} of Qt by Z = (Z, v, u) in the natural way. We denote by QZ = ⟨QZ          t ⟩t∈N the
parametric graph obtained this way. We believe that this procedure creates an obstructing set for H-p
from WH together with a finite collection of parametric graphs whose structure is “upper bounded” by
some QZ , as above. In particular, we set
                                                            [ 
                                      GH := min(WH ∪              QZ | Z ∈ R(Z) )
                                                    Z∈OH

and we conjecture that if p has a finite universal obstruction ZH , then ZH ≲∗ GH . We stress that this
conjecture, if resolved, would indicate that WH is “ubiquitous” to the universal obstructions of H-p.
However, it would not give a finite universal obstruction to H-p but rather an “upper bound” revealing
some of the structural characteristics of an obstruction set. An interesting question is whether the
result of Thomas in [78] on the BQO of graphs with respect to the minor relation may be useful for
proving the finiteness of the obstructions of H-p for particular instantiations of p.

This bound propagates to all obstructing sets defined in this paper, i.e., |Wmin H |, |TH |, |FH |, and it
